Right Cross Contraction
I.10
:

(A\Perp B\given D\cup C)\land(D\Perp A\given C)\implies A\Perp B\cup D\given C
.

k)

Flipped Left Cross Contraction
I.11
:

(A\Perp B\given D\cup C)\land(B\Perp D\given C)\implies B\Perp A\cup D\given C
.

Remark 5.12
.

In particular, we have the equivalences:

(A\Perp B\cup D\given C)\quad\iff\quad(A\Perp B\given D\cup C)\quad\land\quad(A\Perp D\given C),

(A\cup D\Perp B\given C)\quad\iff\quad(A\Perp B\given D\cup C)\quad\land\quad(D\Perp B\given C).

Remark 5.13

(Symmetry)
.

Let the assumptions be like in
5.11
.
We also have the following rules:

l)

Restricted Symmetry
I.16
:

(A\Perp B\given C)\land(B\Perp\emptyset\given C)\implies B\Perp A\given C
.

m)

J
-Restricted Symmetry
I.17
:

A\Perp B\given C\cup J\implies B\Perp A\given C\cup J
.

n)

Symmetry
I.18
: If
J=\emptyset
then:

A\Perp B\given C\implies B\Perp A\given C
.

Lemma 5.14

(More separoid like rules)
.

Let the assumptions be like in
5.11
.
We also have the following rules:

o)

Left Composition
I.12
:

(A\Perp B\given C)\land(D\Perp B\given C)\implies A\cup D\Perp B\given C.

p)

Right Composition
I.13
:

(A\Perp B\given C)\land(A\Perp D\given C)\implies A\Perp B\cup D\given C.

q)

Left Intersection
I.14
: If
A\cap D=\emptyset
then:

(A\Perp B\given D\cup C)\land(D\Perp B\given A\cup C)\implies A\cup D\Perp B\given C.

r)

Right Intersection
I.15
: If
B\cap D=\emptyset
then:

(A\Perp B\given D\cup C)\land(A\Perp D\given B\cup C)\implies A\Perp B\cup D\given C.

s)

More Redundancies
I.19
:

A\Perp B\given C\iff(A\setminus C)\Perp(B\setminus C)\given C\iff A\cup C\Perp J\cup B\cup C\given C
.

6
Applications to Graphical Models

In this section we want to relate transitional conditional independence to
\sigma
-separation in graphs.
This will be done in Theorem
6.3
about the global Markov property for causal Bayesian networks.

6.1
Causal Bayesian Networks

We now introduce a definition of causal Bayesian networks that allows for non-stochastic input variables.

Definition 6.1

(Causal Bayesian network)
.

A
causal Bayesian network (CBN)

\mathbf{M}
consists of:

1.

a (finite) conditional directed acyclic graph (CDAG):
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))
,

2.

input variables
X_{j}
,
j\in J
, and
(stochastic) output variables
X_{v}
,
v\in V
,

3.

a measurable space
\mathcal{X}_{v}
for every
v\in J\,\dot{\cup}\,V
,
where
\mathcal{X}_{v}
is standard
4
4

4

We could also work under the weaker assumption of universal measurable spaces if we would replace all mentionings of measurability with the weaker notion of universal measurability.

if
v\in V
,

4.

a Markov kernel, suggestively written as:
\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right)
:

\begin{array}[]{ccrcl}&&\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}&\dashrightarrow&\mathcal{X}_{v},\\
&&\qquad(A,x_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)})&\mapsto&\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\in A\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}=x_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right),\end{array}

for every
v\in V
, where we write for
D\subseteq J\,\dot{\cup}\,V
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{D}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{v\in D}\mathcal{X}_{v},

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle:=\Asterisk=\{\ast\},

\displaystyle X_{D}

\displaystyle:=(X_{v})_{v\in D},

\displaystyle X_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle:=\ast,

\displaystyle x_{D}

\displaystyle:=(x_{v})_{v\in D},

\displaystyle x_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle:=\ast.

By abuse of notation, we denote the causal Bayesian network as:

\mathbf{M}(V|\doit(J))=\left(\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J)),\left(\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right)\right)_{v\in V}\right).

Definition 6.2
.

Any CBN
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}(V|\doit(J))
comes with its
joint Markov kernel
:

\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J})):\,\mathcal{X}_{J}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{V},

given by:

\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J})):=\bigotimes_{v\in V}^{>}\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right),

where the product
\otimes^{>}
is taken in reverse order of a fixed topological order
<
, i.e. children appear only on the left of their parents in the product.
Note that by Remark
2.8
about associativity and (restricted) commutativity of the product the
joint Markov kernels does actually not depend on the topological order.

6.2
Global Markov Property for Causal Bayesian Networks

We now turn to probably the most striking application of transitional conditional independence:
the
global Markov property
for causal Bayesian networks that allow for (non-stochastic) input variables.
The global Markov property relates the graphical structure
\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))
to transitional
conditional independence relations between the corresponding transitional random variables
X_{A}
.
So checking the graph for
\sigma
-separation relations will then automatically imply
the existence of a regular conditional Markov kernel that does not depend on the specified variables,
which can be stochastic or not.

This will be the first time the global Markov property will be proven in this generality of measure theoretic probability, in the presence of input variables and with such a strong notion of conditional independence.
Direct generalizations to causal Bayesian networks or
structural causal models
that allow for latent confounders, cycles, selection bias and (non-stochastic) input variables
are immidiate, see
[
FM17
,
FM18
,
FM20
,
Ric03
,
Eva16
,
Eva18
,
RERS17
]
, etc., because all those cases can be reduced to a CBN in one
or the other form and only the graphical separation criteria need to be adjusted.
In Appendix
K
in the Supplementary Material
[
For21
]
we will show how the global Markov property together with transitional
conditional independence gives us a measure theoretically clean
proof of the main rules of
do-calculus
, see
[
Pea09
]
, in this generality.

The proof of the global Markov property follows similar arguments as used in
[
LDLL90
,
Ver93
,
Ric03
,
FM17
,
FM18
,
RERS17
]
,
namely chaining the separoid rules for conditional independence (see Theorem
3.11
) and the ones for
d/m/
\sigma
-separation
(see Theorem
5.11
) together in an inductive way.
The main difference here is that we never rely on the Symmetry property but instead use
the left and right versions of the separoid rules separately. Note, again, that the validity of those separoid rules
in this vast generality is a non-trival result,
see Theorem
3.11
,
and were only known for corner cases for other notions of extended conditional independence, rendering those less useful
for the applications to graphical models.
The full proof of the global Markov property is given in Appendix
J
in the Supplementary Material
[
For21
]
.

Theorem 6.3

(Global Markov property for causal Bayesian networks)
.

Consider a causal Bayesian network
\mathbf{M}(V|\doit(J))
with graph
\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))

and joint Markov kernel:
\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J}))
.
Then for all
A,B,C\subseteq J\,\dot{\cup}\,V
(not-necessarily disjoint) we have the implication:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))}B\given C\qquad\implies\qquad X_{A}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J}))}X_{B}\given X_{C}.

Recall that we have - per definition - an implicit dependence on
J
,
X_{J}
, resp., in the second argument on each side.

Remark 6.4
.

The global Markov property says that already checking the graphical criterion
\displaystyle A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))}B\given C
is
enough to get
the existence of a Markov kernel, suggestively written as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|\cancel{X_{B}},X_{C\cap V},\doit(X_{C\cap J}),\cancel{\doit(X_{J})})\right),

such that:

\mathbf{P}(X_{A},X_{B},X_{C}|\doit(X_{J}))=\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|\cancel{X_{B}},X_{C\cap V},\doit(X_{C\cap J}),\cancel{\doit(X_{J})})\right)\otimes\mathbf{P}(X_{B},X_{C}|\doit(X_{J})).

The full power of the global Markov property is unleashed when applied to the causal Bayesian network that arises from
augmentation with further soft intervention variables and hard interventions, etc.
It will then automatically relate many of the marginal conditional interventional Markov kernels of extended and sub-CBNs to each other,
see Appendix
K
in the Supplementary Material
[
For21
]
for a glimpse of the possibilities.

Example 6.5
.

Let
A\subseteq\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))
be an ancestral subset, i.e.
A=\bigcup_{v\in A}\mathrm{Anc}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)
.
Then we have:

\displaystyle(V\cap A)\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))}(J\setminus A)\given J\cap A
,
and thus, by the global Markov property, a Markov kernel
\mathbf{P}(X_{V\cap A}|\doit(X_{J\cap A}))
such that:

\mathbf{P}(X_{A}|\doit(X_{J}))=\mathbf{P}(X_{V\cap A}|\doit(X_{J\cap A}))\otimes\mathbf{P}(X_{J\cap A}|\doit(X_{J})).

So for ancestral subsets we can only work with input variables from
J\cap A
and ignore the ones from
J\setminus A
,
which is in correspondence with our expectations about ancestral causal relations.

7
Discussion

7.1
Extensions to Universal Measurable Spaces

Remark 7.1
.

We presented the theory of the main paper is terms of general measurable spaces whereever it was possible.
Some results needed more restrictive assumptions like
standard measurable spaces. These restrictions can be weakend if one replaces standard with universal measurable spaces and measurability with the weaker notion of universal measurability. All theory has been developed for this more
general setting in Appendix
B
in the Supplementary Material
[
For21
]
.
If
\mathcal{X}
is a measurable space we denote by
\mathcal{X}_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{X},(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet})

its
universal completion
, where
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
is the intersection of all completions.
A map
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is
universally measurable
if
f:\,\mathcal{X}_{\bullet}\to\mathcal{Y}
is measurable.
We then advocate the use of transition probability spaces
\left(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T},\mathbf{K}(W|T)\right)
,
with universally measurable
\mathbf{K}(W|T):\,\mathcal{T}_{\bullet}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{W}

and the following class of transitional random variables:

\mathcal{C}:=\left\{X:\,(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T})_{\bullet}\to\mathcal{X}\text{ universally measurable}\,|\,\mathcal{X}\text{ universal measurable space}\right\},

where a
universal measurable space
, see Corollary
B.34
,
is - up to universal completion - countably generated, countably separated
with perfect probability measures.
The class
\mathcal{C}
together with
\precsim_{\mathbf{K},\bullet}
5
5

5

We define:

X\precsim_{\mathbf{K},\bullet}Y
if there is a univerally measurable
\varphi:\,\mathcal{Y}_{\bullet}\to\mathcal{X}

such that
\mathbf{K}(X,Y|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|T)
.

and
X\lor Y:=(X,Y)

forms a
join-semi-lattice
up to
\approx_{\mathbf{K},\bullet}
-anti-symmetry,
see Appendix
D
in the Supplementary Material
[
For21
]
,
and together with

\Indep_{\mathbf{K},\bullet}
6
6

6

We define:
X\Indep_{\mathbf{K},\bullet}Y\given Z
if there
exists a universally measurable Markov kernel

\mathbf{Q}(X|Z):\mathcal{Z}_{\bullet}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)
.

a
T
-
\ast
-separoid,
see Appendix
E
in the Supplementary Material
[
For21
]
.
Furthermore, all
\bullet
-regular conditional Markov kernels

\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z):\,(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z})_{\bullet}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}
exist for
\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)
,
when
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
are universal measurable spaces,
see Theorem
C.7
, and are strongly consistent along universally measurable maps,
see Theorem
C.13
.

This is our contribution to make the theory applicable in as much generality as possible.

7.2
Comparison to Other Notions of Extended Conditional Independence

Remark 7.2
.

Several other notions of extended conditional independence can be
found in the literature, see e.g.
[
CD17a
,
RERS17
,
FM20
]
, and there is also variation conditional independence.
We elaborate in more details in Appendix
L
in the Supplementary Material
[
For21
]
.
In overview, it can be said that variation conditional independence, a non-probabilistic,
set-theoretic
notion of conditional independence, only shares formal similarities with the other notions of
extended conditional independence. The correspondence would be to replace the space of
probability measures
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
with the power set
2^{\mathcal{X}}
, see Example
L.6
.

The three other notions of extended conditional independence from
[
CD17a
,
RERS17
,
FM20
]

are weaker than transitional conditional independence, i.e. whenever they were defined
transitional conditional independence implies the other extended conditional independence.

In contrast to
[
CD17a
]
transitional conditional independence satisfies all (asymmetric)
separoid axioms, where two of them need standard or universal measurable spaces, see
Theorem
3.11
,
making it suitable for the use in graphical models, see the global Markov property,
Theorem
6.3
.

In contrast to
[
RERS17
]
transitional conditional independence is an asymmetric notion,
directly propagating the asymmetry between “input and output variables”, making it able to equivalently express
classical statistical concepts like ancillarity, sufficiency, adequacy, etc. Furthmore,
we developed the theory of transitional conditional independence in a more
general context of (universal) measurable spaces, transition probability spaces
and transitional random variables.

In contrast to
[
FM20
]
transitional conditional independence provides one with
the existence of certain Markov kernels and
factorizations. This gives a much stronger conclusion in the global Markov property in
graphical models, while starting from the same assumptions,
see Theorem
6.3
.

More will be said in Appendix
L
in the Supplementary Material
[
For21
]
.

7.3
Conclusion

We developed the theory of transition probability spaces, transitional random variables
and transitional conditional indpendence. These concepts are most well behaved if the
underlying spaces have similar properties to standard measurable spaces. To extend the theory as
far as possible we studied, developed and generalized
the theory of univeral measurable spaces.
Furthermore, we proved the disintegration of transition probabilities, i.e. the existence
of regular conditional Markov kernels on standard, analytic and universal measurable spaces.

Transitional conditional independence was defined as an asymmetric notion of (ir)relevance relations.
We developed the theory of asymmetric separoids and showed that transitional conditional
independence and the graphical notion of
\sigma
-separation satisfy all those asymmetric
separoid rules. We then showed how to relate those notions in graphical models and proved
a global Markov property for those. We also showed in the Supplementary Material
[
For21
]
how the global Markov property
implies the validity of the 3 main rules of the causal do-calculus in measure theoretic generality
and indicated how to apply it to further causal concepts.

We then compared transitional conditional independence to other notions of extended conditional
independence and showed that it is stronger than all of those.

We also showed that
transitional conditional independence can express classical statistical concepts like
ancillarity, sufficiency, adequacy and invariant reductions, etc.
We also demonstrated what it can say about Bayesian statistics, the likelihood principle,
propensity scores, etc.

Finally, we want to stress the simplicity of the definition of
transitional conditional independence
:

X\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}Y\given Z\qquad:\iff\qquad\exists\mathbf{Q}(X|Z):\quad\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T).

Supplementary Material

Appendix A
Symmetric Separoids and Asymmetric Separoids

This section aims at formalizing a notion of “asymmetric separoids”. Separoids were introduced in
[
Daw01a
]
as a way to
express (ir)relevance relations. Similar ideas and the notion of graphoids were also investigated
in
[
PP85
,
Spo94
]
.
“Asymmetric separoids” will naturally arise from symmetric ones when one fixes parts of one, say the second argument and/or the third argument. Depending on their behaviour in the second and third argument we will call such a ternary relation
\Indep
a

\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid
.
Our notion of
transitional conditional independence
, see Definition
3.1
, will form an
T
-
\ast
-separoid, see Appendices
D
and
E
.
Also
\sigma
-separation, an extension of d-separation, to graphs that allow for (cycles and) external “input” nodes will form
a
J
-
\emptyset
-separoid, see Section
5
and Appendix
I
.
The global Markov property for the probabilistic graphical models, which relates the conditional independence structure
of the graph in terms of
\sigma
-separation to transitional conditional independence of the corresponding transitional random variables
can then be interpreted as a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid homomorphism.
The global Markov property will be presented in Section
6.2
and the proof is given in Appendix
J
.

Since the separoid axioms from
[
Daw01a
]
are not logically independent, i.e. parts of the rules can be deduced from the others,
we have to investigate which of the rules generalize in which form. This is the reason we give a slightly different definition of separoids
than it was presented in
[
Daw01a
]
. We also do not require the order on the space to fully form a (bounded) join-semi-lattice.
We will mainly relax the requirement of the elements to be reflexive. The reason is that under these relaxed conditions one still has
the corresponding separoids rules. This is helpful when one works with probabilistic maps (Markov kernels) in contrast to deterministic maps.
To make the distinction between our version of separoid and the ones from
[
Daw01a
]
clearer we will stick to the
names
symmetric separoid
and
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid
in this paper.
We then study the relation between symmetric separoids and
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid.
The main findings are Theorem
A.16
and Theorem
A.17
.
The first,
A.16
, states, as discussed above, that if we start with a symmetric separoid and fix parts of the
second and/or third argument we will arrive at a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid. The other one,
A.17
, shows that if
we symmetrize an
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid with the logical “or” we will get a symmetric separoid.

One can certainly argue which of the separoid rules should be included in the notion of
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid and which not.
The guiding principle of our definition was that it should: (i) generalize the notion of (symmetric) separoids and recover it,
(ii) be strong enough to prove the global Markov property, see Theorem
6.3
and Appendix
J.1
, (iii) be weak enough such that we can verify these rules for both, transitional conditional independence, see Appendices
D
and
E
, and
\sigma
-separation in graphs with input nodes, see
5

and
I
,
(iv) be adjusted in such a way that we can prove the two
Theorems,
A.16
and
A.17
, mentioned above to get a close relationship between symmetric separoids and
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoids. This is, for instance, the reason we included 4 Contraction rules, which are all needed to prove
Theorem
A.17
. These then also give us Restricted Symmetry
1
. The obstruction to become symmetric
then gives a clear motivation for
\tau
-Restricted Right Redundancy
2
, allowing
\diameter
-
\kappa
-separoids
to become symmetric separoids. A look at the proof of the global Markov property in Appendix
J.1
shows that all the other separoid
rules are needed, thus motivating the minimal set of rules that we require.

A.1
Compatibility Relations

Remark A.1
.

In the following let
\Omega
be a class that is endowed with an equivalence relation
\cong
.
We then further consider a commutative and associative binary operation
\lor
on (the equivalence clases of)
\Omega
,
i.e. we require that
for all
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\dots\in\Omega
we have:

1.

(\alpha\cong\alpha^{\prime})\,\land\,(\beta\cong\beta^{\prime})\,\implies\,(\alpha\lor\beta)\cong(\alpha^{\prime}\lor\beta^{\prime})
.

2.

(\alpha\lor\beta)\cong(\beta\lor\alpha)
.

3.

((\alpha\lor\beta)\lor\gamma)\cong(\alpha\lor(\beta\lor\gamma)).

We will then also consider an element
\diameter\in\Omega

(so called
bottom element
) satisfying for all
\alpha\in\Omega
:

4.

\diameter\lor\alpha\cong\alpha
.

From this it is clear that if
\diameter^{\prime}
is another bottom element we have:
\diameter\cong\diameter^{\prime}
.

Furthermore, we consider a ternary relation
\Indep
on (the equivalence clases of)
\Omega
:

5.

\left(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma\right)\,\land\,(\alpha\cong\alpha^{\prime})\,\land\,(\beta\cong\beta^{\prime})\,\land(\gamma\cong\gamma^{\prime})\,\implies\,\alpha^{\prime}\Indep\beta^{\prime}\given\gamma^{\prime}.

More properties of
\Indep
will be fixed further in the definitions later on.

At one point we will also have
another binary relation
\ll
on
\Omega
.
We will require that for all
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\dots\in\Omega
we have:

6.

(\alpha\ll\beta)\,\land\,(\alpha\cong\alpha^{\prime})\,\land\,(\beta\cong\beta^{\prime})\,\implies\,(\alpha^{\prime}\ll\beta^{\prime}).

7.

\alpha\ll\beta\ll\gamma\,\implies\,\alpha\ll\gamma
.

8.

\alpha\ll\beta\,\implies\,\alpha\ll\beta\lor\gamma
.

9.

(\alpha\ll\gamma)\,\land\,(\beta\ll\gamma)\,\implies\,\alpha\lor\beta\ll\gamma
.

10.

\diameter\ll\alpha
.

Besides the
\cong
-compatibility of
\ll
most of these rules are actually not needed later on,
but all binary relations
\ll
we encounter will satisfy those rules.
We also include them here because of the following result.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
(\Omega,\cong,\lor,\ll,\diameter)
satisfy all the occuring compatibility relations from Remark
A.1
.
We also put for
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega
:

\alpha\ll\gg\beta\quad:\iff\quad\alpha\ll\beta\ll\alpha.

Furthermore, we define the class of reflexive elements:

\tilde{\Omega}:=\left\{\alpha\in\Omega\,|\,\alpha\ll\alpha\right\}.

Then
\tilde{\Omega}
forms a
bounded join-semi-lattice
with partial order
\ll
, join
\lor
and bottom element
\diameter

(up to
\ll\gg
-anti-symmetry and set-theoretic considerations).

Proof.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\cdots\in\tilde{\Omega}
.

1.

Reflexivity:
\alpha\ll\alpha
holds by definition for
\alpha\in\tilde{\Omega}
.

2.

Anti-symmetry:
\alpha\ll\beta\ll\alpha
implies
\alpha\ll\gg\beta
per definition.

3.

Transitivity:
\alpha\ll\beta\ll\gamma
implies
\alpha\ll\gamma
by assumption.

4.

Join is upper bound: For
\alpha\ll\alpha
we have
\alpha\ll\alpha\lor\beta
.
Similarly, for
\beta\ll\beta
we have
\beta\ll\alpha\lor\beta
.

5.

Join is smallest upper bound:
\alpha\ll\gamma
and
\beta\ll\gamma
implies
\alpha\lor\beta\ll\gamma
by assumption.

6.

Closedness under join:
\alpha\ll\alpha
and
\beta\ll\beta
implies
\alpha\lor\beta\ll\alpha\lor\beta
because the join

\gamma:=\alpha\lor\beta
is an upper bound to
\alpha
and
\beta
and it is the smallest one, thus:
\alpha\lor\beta\ll\alpha\lor\beta
.

7.

Bottom element is neutral:
\alpha\ll\gg\diameter\lor\alpha
. This holds since
\diameter\lor\alpha\cong\alpha
and
\alpha\ll\alpha
for
\alpha\in\tilde{\Omega}
and
\ll
is compatible with
\cong
.

8.

Bottem element is reflexive:
\diameter\ll\diameter
by assumption.

As in any bounded join-semi-lattice we also have:

9.

Idempotent:
\alpha\ll\gg\alpha\lor\alpha
for
\alpha\in\tilde{\Omega}
.

∎

A.2
Asymmetric Separoids

Definition A.3

(
\tau
-pre-separoid)
.

Let
\tau\in\Omega
be fixed.
We call
(\Omega,\cong,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
a
\tau
-pre-separoid
if all occuring compatibility relations from Remark
A

hold and
if the following rules hold for all

\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\lambda\in\Omega
:

1.

Left Redundancy:

\diameter\Indep\beta\given\gamma
.

2.

\tau
-Restricted Right Redundancy:

\alpha\Indep\diameter\given\gamma\lor\tau
.

3.

\tau
-Inverted Right Decomposition:

\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep\tau\lor\beta\given\gamma
.

4.

Left Decomposition:

\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma\implies\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma
.

5.

Right Decomposition:

\alpha\Indep\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep\lambda\given\gamma
.

6.

Left Weak Union:

\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma
.

7.

Right Weak Union:

\alpha\Indep\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma
.

8.

Left Contraction:

(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma)\implies\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma
.

9.

Right Contraction:

(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\alpha\Indep\lambda\given\gamma)\implies\alpha\Indep\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma
.

10.

Right Cross Contraction:

(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\lambda\Indep\alpha\given\gamma)\implies\alpha\Indep\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma
.

11.

Flipped Left Cross Contraction:

(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\beta\Indep\lambda\given\gamma)\implies\beta\Indep\alpha\lor\lambda\given\gamma
.

Definition A.4

(
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid)
.

Let
\tau,\kappa\in\Omega
be fixed and
\ll
a binary relation on
\Omega
.
We then call
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid

if
(\Omega,\cong,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
is a
\tau
-pre-separoid,
if
\ll
satisfies the compatibility relations from Remark
A.1
,
and if the following rule holds for all

\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\Omega
:

1.

\kappa
-Extended Left Redundancy:

\alpha\ll\kappa\lor\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma
.

Remark A.5
.

Let
(\Omega,\cong,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
be a
\tau
-pre-separoid.

1.

We can define the following binary relation
\ll^{\prime}
on
\Omega
:

\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gamma\qquad:\iff\qquad\forall\beta\in\Omega:\quad\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma.

One can show, see Theorem
A.13
, that
\ll^{\prime}
is the weakest binary relation
\ll
on
\Omega
that
turns
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
into a
\tau
-
\diameter
-separoid.

2.

Let
\ll
be another binary relation on
\tau
-pre-separoid
\Omega
satisfying the compatibility relations from Remark
A.1

and let
\kappa\in\Omega
.
We can then define a new binary relation
\ll_{\kappa}
via:

\alpha\ll_{\kappa}\gamma\quad:\iff\quad\alpha\ll\kappa\lor\gamma.

For
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
to be a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid we require
the validity of
\kappa
-Extended Left Redundancy
1
, which in the above terms can be equivalently expressed as
the following implication between binary relations,
\alpha,\gamma\in\Omega
:

\alpha\ll_{\kappa}\gamma\implies\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gamma.

Lemma A.6
.

Let
\left(\Indep_{i}\right)_{i\in I}
be a familiy of ternary relations on
\Omega
.
For every
i\in I
let
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep_{i},\diameter)
be a
\tau
-pre-separoid, a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid, resp.
Then we can define for
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\Omega
:

\alpha\Indep^{\land}\beta\given\gamma\qquad:\iff\qquad\forall i\in I:\quad\alpha\Indep_{i}\beta\given\gamma.

Then
\displaystyle(\Omega,\cong,\lor,\ll,\Indep^{\land},\diameter)
is also a
\tau
-pre-separoid, a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid, resp.

Proof.

This can easily be checked via the definition.
∎

In the following we will derive additional rules that hold in any
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
.
Note that only the
\cong
-compatibility is needed for
\ll
from the compatibility rules from Remark
A.1

in addition to the
\kappa
-Extended Left Redundancy
1
.

Lemma A.7

(
\kappa
-Extended Inverted Left Decomposition)
.

Let
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
be a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid. Then we have the implication:

(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma)\land(\lambda\ll\alpha\lor\kappa\lor\gamma)\implies\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma.

Proof.

We have the implications:

\displaystyle\lambda\ll\alpha\lor\kappa\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\kappa\text{-Extended Left Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:k-ext-l-red}}}

\displaystyle\lambda\Indep\beta\given\alpha\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma)\;\land\;\text{Left Contraction \ref{sep:gen:l-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma.

∎

Lemma A.8

(
\tau
-
\kappa
-Extended Inverted Right Decomposition)
.

Let
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
be a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid. Then we have the implication:

\left(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma\right)\land\left(\lambda\ll\tau\lor\beta\lor\kappa\lor\gamma\right)\implies\alpha\Indep\tau\lor\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma.

Proof.

We have the implications:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\tau\text{-Inverted Right Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:t-inv-r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau\lor\beta\given\gamma,

\displaystyle\lambda\ll\tau\lor\beta\lor\kappa\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\kappa\text{-Extended Left Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:k-ext-l-red}}}

\displaystyle\lambda\Indep\alpha\given\tau\lor\beta\lor\gamma,

both together

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Flipped Left Cross Contraction \ref{sep:gen:flc-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau\lor\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma.

∎

Lemma A.9

(
\kappa
-Equivalent Exchange)
.

Let
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
be a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid. Then we have the implication:

\left(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma\right)\land\left(\gamma\ll\kappa\lor\gamma^{\prime}\right)\land\left(\gamma^{\prime}\ll\kappa\lor\gamma\right)\implies\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma^{\prime}.

Proof.

We have the implications:

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}\ll\kappa\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\kappa\text{-Extended Left Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:k-ext-l-red}}}

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}\Indep\alpha\lor\beta\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Weak Union \ref{sep:gen:r-uni}}}

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}\Indep\alpha\given\beta\lor\gamma,

(a)

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:int-a2}\land(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma)

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Flipped Left Cross Contraction \ref{sep:gen:flc-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\beta\lor\gamma^{\prime}\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Weak Union \ref{sep:gen:r-uni}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma^{\prime}\lor\gamma,

(b)

\displaystyle\gamma\ll\kappa\lor\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\kappa\text{-Extended Left Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:k-ext-l-red}}}

\displaystyle\gamma\Indep\beta\given\gamma^{\prime},

(c)

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:int-b2}\land\eqref{eq:int-c2}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Contraction \ref{sep:gen:l-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\gamma\Indep\beta\given\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:l-dec}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma^{\prime}.

∎

Remark A.10
.

Note that if
\ll
satisfies the following compatibility rule with
\lor
for
\alpha,\beta\in\Omega
:

\alpha\ll\beta\implies\alpha\ll\kappa\lor\beta,

then
\gamma\ll\gamma^{\prime}\ll\gamma
will imply the condition from Lemma
A.9
:

\gamma\ll\kappa\lor\gamma^{\prime}\quad\land\quad\gamma^{\prime}\ll\kappa\lor\gamma.

Lemma A.11

(Full
\kappa
-Equivalent Exchange)
.

Let
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
be a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid.
If
\alpha^{\prime}\ll\kappa\lor\alpha
and
\beta^{\prime}\ll\kappa\lor\beta
and
\gamma\ll\kappa\lor\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime}\ll\kappa\lor\gamma

then we have:

\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma\qquad\implies\qquad\alpha^{\prime}\Indep\beta^{\prime}\given\gamma^{\prime}.

Proof.

We have the implications:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\ll\kappa\lor\alpha

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\kappa\text{-Extended Left Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:k-ext-l-red}}}

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\Indep\beta\lor\gamma\given\alpha

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Weak Union \ref{sep:gen:r-uni}}}

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\Indep\beta\given\alpha\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma)\;\land\;\text{Left Contraction \ref{sep:gen:l-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\alpha^{\prime}\Indep\beta\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:l-dec}}}

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\Indep\beta\given\gamma,

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}\ll\kappa\lor\beta

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\kappa\text{-Extended Left Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:k-ext-l-red}}}

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}\Indep\alpha^{\prime}\lor\gamma\given\beta

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Weak Union \ref{sep:gen:r-uni}}}

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}\Indep\alpha^{\prime}\given\beta\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{(\alpha^{\prime}\Indep\beta\given\gamma)\;\land\;\text{Fl. Left Cr. Contraction \ref{sep:gen:flc-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\Indep\beta\lor\beta^{\prime}\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\Indep\beta^{\prime}\given\gamma,

\displaystyle(\gamma\ll\kappa\lor\gamma^{\prime})\land(\gamma^{\prime}\ll\kappa\lor\gamma)

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{(\alpha^{\prime}\Indep\beta^{\prime}\given\gamma)\;\land\;\kappa\text{-Equivalent Exchange \ref{sep:gen:k-eq-ex}}}

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\Indep\beta^{\prime}\given\gamma^{\prime}.

∎

Lemma A.12

(Symmetry)
.

Let
(\Omega,\cong,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
be a
\tau
-pre-separoid (e.g. a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid) then we have the implication:

1.

Restricted Symmetry:

(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma)\land(\beta\Indep\diameter\given\gamma)\implies\beta\Indep\alpha\given\gamma
.

2.

\tau
-Restricted Symmetry:

\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma\lor\tau\implies\beta\Indep\alpha\given\gamma\lor\tau
.

3.

Symmetry: If
\tau\cong\diameter
then:

\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma\implies\beta\Indep\alpha\given\gamma
.

Proof.

Restricted Symmetry
1
follows from Flipped Left Cross Contraction
11
with

\lambda=\diameter
.

\tau
-Restricted Symmetry
2
follows by replacing
\gamma
with
\gamma\lor\tau
and using

\tau
-Restricted Right Redundancy
2
.
Symmetry follows from
\tau
-Restricted Symmetry
2
with
\tau\cong\diameter
.
∎

A.3
Join-Semi-Lattices from Asymmetric Separoids

Theorem A.13
.

Let
(\Omega,\cong,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
be a
\tau
-pre-separoid and
\ll^{\prime}
for
\alpha,\gamma\in\Omega
be
defined by:

\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gamma\qquad:\iff\qquad\forall\beta\in\Omega:\quad\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma.

We also put:

\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gg\gamma\qquad:\iff\qquad\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gamma\ll^{\prime}\alpha.

Then for all
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\cdots\in\Omega
we have the following rules:

1.

Anti-symmetry:
\alpha\ll^{\prime}\beta\ll^{\prime}\alpha\,\implies\,\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gg\beta.

2.

Transitivity:
\alpha\ll^{\prime}\beta\ll^{\prime}\gamma\,\implies\,\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gamma
.

3.

Product extension:
\alpha\ll^{\prime}\beta\,\implies\,\alpha\ll^{\prime}\beta\lor\gamma
.

4.

Product stays bounded:
(\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gamma)\,\land\,(\beta\ll^{\prime}\gamma)\,\implies\,(\alpha\lor\beta)\ll^{\prime}\gamma
.

5.

Product compatibility:
(\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gamma)\,\land\,(\beta\ll^{\prime}\lambda)\,\implies\,(\alpha\lor\beta)\ll^{\prime}(\gamma\lor\lambda)
.

6.

Bottom element:
\diameter\ll^{\prime}\gamma
.

7.

\diameter
-Extended Left Redundancy:
\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gamma\,\implies\,\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma
.

Thus,
(\Omega,\cong,\ll^{\prime},\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
forms a
\tau
-
\diameter
-separoid.

Furthermore, if we define the class of reflexive elements:

\Omega^{\prime}:=\left\{\alpha\in\Omega\,|\,\alpha\ll^{\prime}\alpha\right\},

then
\Omega^{\prime}
is a
bounded join-semi-lattice
with partial order
\ll^{\prime}
, join
\lor
and bottom element
\diameter

(up to
\ll^{\prime}\gg
-anti-symmetry and set-theoretical considerations),
\tau\in\Omega^{\prime}
and
(\Omega^{\prime},\cong,\ll^{\prime},\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
also
forms a
\tau
-
\diameter
-separoid.

Proof.

1.

Anti-symmetry holds per definition of
\ll^{\prime}\gg
.

2.

Transitivity: For
\lambda\in\Omega
we get:

\displaystyle\alpha\ll^{\prime}\beta

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\lambda\lor\gamma\given\beta

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Weak Union \ref{sep:gen:r-uni}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\lambda\given\beta\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\beta\ll^{\prime}\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\beta\Indep\lambda\given\gamma,

both together

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Contraction \ref{sep:gen:l-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\beta\Indep\lambda\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:l-dec}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\lambda\given\gamma.

Since this holds for all
\lambda\in\Omega
we get:
\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gamma
.

3.

Product extension: For
\lambda\in\Omega
we get:

\displaystyle\alpha\ll^{\prime}\beta

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\lambda\lor\gamma\given\beta

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Weak Union \ref{sep:gen:r-uni}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\lambda\given\beta\lor\gamma.

Since this holds for all
\lambda\in\Omega
we get:
\alpha\ll^{\prime}\beta\lor\gamma
.

4.

Product stays bounded: For
\lambda\in\Omega
we get:

\displaystyle\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\lambda\lor\beta\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Weak Union \ref{sep:gen:r-uni}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\lambda\given\beta\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\beta\ll^{\prime}\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\beta\Indep\lambda\given\gamma,

both together

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Contraction \ref{sep:gen:l-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\beta\Indep\lambda\given\gamma.

Since this holds for all
\lambda\in\Omega
we get:
\alpha\lor\beta\ll^{\prime}\gamma
.

5.

Product compatibility:

\displaystyle\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Product extension}}

\displaystyle\alpha\ll^{\prime}\gamma\lor\lambda,

\displaystyle\beta\ll^{\prime}\lambda

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Product extension}}

\displaystyle\beta\ll^{\prime}\gamma\lor\lambda,

both together

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Product stays bounded}}

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\beta\ll^{\prime}\gamma\lor\lambda.

6.

Bottom element: Left Redundancy
1
:
\diameter\Indep\beta\given\gamma
for
all
\beta
implies
\diameter\ll^{\prime}\gamma
.

7.

\diameter
-Extended Left Redundancy
1
holds per definition of
\ll^{\prime}
.

8.

\tau
is reflexive: For
\beta\in\Omega
we have:

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\tau\text{-Restricted Right Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:t-res-r-red}}}

\displaystyle(\tau\Indep\diameter\given\beta\lor\tau)\,\land\,(\beta\Indep\diameter\given\tau)

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\tau\text{-Inverted Right Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:t-inv-r-dec}}}

\displaystyle(\tau\Indep\diameter\given\beta\lor\tau)\,\land\,(\beta\Indep\tau\given\tau)

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Cross Contraction \ref{sep:gen:rc-con}}}

\displaystyle\tau\Indep\beta\given\tau.

Since this holds for all
\beta\in\Omega
we get the claim:
\tau\ll^{\prime}\tau
and thus
\tau\in\Omega^{\prime}
.

Note that
\Omega^{\prime}
by the product compatibility is closed under
\lor
.
That
\Omega^{\prime}
forms a bounded join-semi-lattice follows from the rules above and Lemma
A.2
.
Since
\tau\in\Omega^{\prime}
it is clear that
\Omega^{\prime}
also forms a
\tau
-
\diameter
-separoid.
∎

A.4
Relations to Symmetric Separoids

Definition A.14

(Symmetric separoid)
.

We call
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
a
symmetric separoid
if
all occuring compatibility relations from Remark
A
hold and if
the following rules hold for all

\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\lambda\in\Omega
:

1.

(Unrestricted) Symmetry:

\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma\implies\beta\Indep\alpha\given\gamma
.

2.

(Extended Left) Redundancy:

\alpha\ll\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma
.

3.

(Left) Decomposition:

\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma\implies\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma
.

4.

(Left) Weak Union:

\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma
.

5.

(Left) Contraction:

(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma)\implies\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma
.

Remark A.15
.

1.

Every symmetric separoid is a
\diameter
-
\diameter
-separoid.

2.

Every
\diameter
-
\kappa
-separoid is a symmetric separoid by Lemma
A.12
.

3.

Let
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
be a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid.
Then
(\Omega,\cong,\ll_{\kappa},\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
is a
\tau
-
\diameter
-separoid,
where
\ll_{\kappa}
is given via:

\alpha\ll_{\kappa}\beta\quad:\iff\quad\alpha\ll\kappa\lor\beta.

4.

So every
\diameter
-
\kappa
-separoid is a (symmetric)

\diameter
-
\diameter
-separoid either using
\ll
,
\ll_{\kappa}
or even
\ll^{\prime}
.

The following will be the first main theorem of this section. One may first consider the case where
\tau_{1}
and
\kappa_{1}

both are equal to
\diameter
, i.e. the case of a symmetric separoid. The theorem will then show how one can turn a symmetric separoid into
a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid by partially fixing the arguments in the second and third argument of
\Indep
.

Theorem A.16
.

Let
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
be a
\tau_{1}
-
\kappa_{1}
-separoid and
\tau_{2},\kappa_{2}\in\Omega
further elements
such that
\tau_{2}\ll\kappa_{1}\lor\tau_{2}
. For
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\lambda\in\Omega
we define the ternary relation:

\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma\quad:\iff\quad\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma.

Then
\displaystyle(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})},\diameter)
is a
(\tau_{1}\lor\tau_{2})
-
(\kappa_{1}\lor\kappa_{2})
-separoid.

Proof.

We put
\tau:=\tau_{1}\lor\tau_{2}
and
\kappa:=\kappa_{1}\lor\kappa_{2}
.

1.’

\kappa
-Extended Left Redundancy:

\displaystyle\alpha\ll\kappa\lor\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma
.

Proof
:

\displaystyle\alpha\ll\kappa\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\ll\kappa_{1}\lor(\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma),

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\kappa_{1}\text{-Extended Left Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:k-ext-l-red}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma.

1.

Left Redundancy:

\displaystyle\diameter\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma
.

Proof
:

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:l-red}}}

\displaystyle\diameter\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\diameter\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma.

2.

\tau
-Restricted Right Redundancy:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\diameter\given\gamma\lor\tau
.

Proof
:

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\tau_{1}\text{-Restricted Right Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:t-res-r-red}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\diameter\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma\lor\tau_{2}\lor\tau_{1},

\displaystyle\tau_{2}\ll\kappa_{1}\lor\tau_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\kappa_{1}\text{-Extended Left Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:k-ext-l-red}}}

\displaystyle\tau_{2}\Indep\alpha\lor\diameter\lor\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma\lor\tau_{1}\given\tau_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\tau_{2}\Indep\alpha\given\diameter\lor\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma\lor\tau_{2}\lor\tau_{1},

both

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Flipped Left Cross Contraction \ref{sep:gen:flc-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\diameter\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma\lor\tau_{2}\lor\tau_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\diameter\given\gamma\lor\tau.

3.

\tau
-Inverted Right Decomposition:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\tau\lor\beta\given\gamma
.

Proof:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\tau_{1}\text{-Inverted Right Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:t-inv-r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\tau_{1}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\tau_{2}\ll\kappa_{1}\lor\tau_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\kappa_{1}\text{-Extended Left Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:k-ext-l-red}}}

\displaystyle\tau_{2}\Indep\alpha\lor\tau_{1}\lor\beta\lor\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma\given\tau_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\tau_{2}\Indep\alpha\given\tau_{2}\lor\tau_{1}\lor\beta\lor\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma,

both

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Flipped Left Cross Contraction \ref{sep:gen:flc-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\tau_{1}\lor\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\tau\lor\beta\given\gamma.

4.

Left Decomposition:

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma\implies\lambda\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma
.

Proof:

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:l-dec}}}

\displaystyle\lambda\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\lambda\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma.

5.

Right Decomposition:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\lambda\given\gamma
.

Proof:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\lor\lambda\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\lambda\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\lambda\given\gamma.

6.

Left Weak Union:

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma
.

Proof:

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Weak Union \ref{sep:gen:l-uni}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\lambda\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma.

7.

Right Weak Union:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma
.

Proof:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\lor\lambda\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Weak Union \ref{sep:gen:r-uni}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\lambda\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma.

8.

Left Contraction:

\displaystyle(\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\lambda\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma)\implies\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma
.

Proof:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\lambda\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\lambda\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\lambda\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma,

both together

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Contraction \ref{sep:gen:l-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\gamma.

9.

Right Contraction:

\displaystyle(\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\lambda\given\gamma)\implies\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma
.

Proof:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\lambda\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\lambda\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\lambda\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\lambda\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma,

both together

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Contraction \ref{sep:gen:r-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\lor\lambda\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma.

10.

Right Cross Contraction:

\displaystyle(\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\lambda\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\alpha\given\gamma)\implies\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma
.

Proof:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\lambda\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\lambda\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\alpha\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\lambda\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\alpha\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\lambda\Indep\alpha\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma,

both together

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Cross Contraction \ref{sep:gen:rc-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\lor\lambda\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\lor\lambda\given\gamma.

11.

Flipped Left Cross Contraction:

\displaystyle(\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\beta\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\lambda\given\gamma)\implies\beta\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\alpha\lor\lambda\given\gamma
.

Proof:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\lambda\lor\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Weak Union \ref{sep:gen:r-uni}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\beta\given\kappa_{2}\lor\tau_{2}\lor\lambda\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\beta\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\lambda\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\beta\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\lambda\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Flipped Left Cross Contraction \ref{sep:gen:flc-con}}}

\displaystyle\beta\Indep\tau_{2}\lor\alpha\lor\lambda\given\kappa_{2}\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\beta\Indep_{(\tau_{2}|\kappa_{2})}\alpha\lor\lambda\given\gamma.

∎

We now directly present the second main theorem of this section, showing the close relation between symmetric and
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoids.
The theorem states that if one symmetrizes a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid by the logical “or” relation one gets a symmetric separoid out.
Note that all four contraction rules are needed to make this work.

Theorem A.17
.

Let
(\Omega,\cong,\ll,\lor,\Indep,\diameter)
be a
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoid.
We then define for
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\Omega
the symmetrized ternary relation:

\alpha\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma\qquad:\iff\qquad\left(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma\right)\quad\lor\quad\left(\beta\Indep\alpha\given\gamma\right),

where with
\lor
in the middle of the right we mean the logical OR.
We further define:

\alpha\ll_{\kappa}\beta\qquad:\iff\qquad\alpha\ll\kappa\lor\beta.

Then
\displaystyle(\Omega,\cong,\ll_{\kappa},\lor,\Indep^{\lor},\diameter)
is a (symmetric)
\diameter
-
\diameter
-separoid.

Proof.

1.

(Unrestricted) Symmetry:

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma\implies\beta\Indep^{\lor}\alpha\given\gamma.

Proof.
Clear by construction.

2.

(Extended Left) Redundancy:

\displaystyle\alpha\ll_{\kappa}\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma.

Proof.

\displaystyle\alpha\ll_{\kappa}\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\ll\kappa\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\kappa\text{-Extended Left Redundancy \ref{sep:gen:k-ext-l-red}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma,

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\left(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\gamma\right)\lor\left(\beta\Indep\alpha\given\gamma\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma.

3.

(Left) Decomposition:

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma\implies\lambda\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma

Proof.

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\left(\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma\right)\lor\left(\beta\Indep\alpha\lor\lambda\given\gamma\right),

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:l-dec}}}

\displaystyle\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma,

\displaystyle\beta\Indep\alpha\lor\lambda\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Decomposition \ref{sep:gen:r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\beta\Indep\lambda\given\gamma,

both together

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\left(\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma\right)\lor\left(\beta\Indep\lambda\given\gamma\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\lambda\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma.

4.

(Left) Weak Union:

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma\implies\alpha\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma
.

Proof.

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\left(\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma\right)\lor\left(\beta\Indep\alpha\lor\lambda\given\gamma\right),

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Weak Union \ref{sep:gen:l-uni}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma,

\displaystyle\beta\Indep\alpha\lor\lambda\given\gamma

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Weak Union \ref{sep:gen:r-uni}}}

\displaystyle\beta\Indep\alpha\given\lambda\lor\gamma,

both together

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\left(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma\right)\lor\left(\beta\Indep\alpha\given\lambda\lor\gamma\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma.

5.

(Left) Contraction:

\displaystyle(\alpha\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\lambda\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma)\implies\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma
.

Proof.

\displaystyle(\alpha\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\lambda\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma)

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\left((\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\lor(\beta\Indep\alpha\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\right)

\displaystyle\land\left((\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma)\land(\beta\Indep\lambda\given\gamma)\right),

\displaystyle(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma)

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Contr. \ref{sep:gen:l-con}}}

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma,

\displaystyle(\alpha\Indep\beta\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\beta\Indep\lambda\given\gamma)

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{FL Cr. Contr. \ref{sep:gen:flc-con}}}

\displaystyle\beta\Indep\alpha\lor\lambda\given\gamma,

\displaystyle(\beta\Indep\alpha\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma)

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{R. Cr. Contr. \ref{sep:gen:rc-con}}}

\displaystyle\beta\Indep\alpha\lor\lambda\given\gamma,

\displaystyle(\beta\Indep\alpha\given\lambda\lor\gamma)\land(\beta\Indep\lambda\given\gamma)

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Contr. \ref{sep:gen:r-con}}}

\displaystyle\beta\Indep\alpha\lor\lambda\given\gamma,

all together

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\left(\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep\beta\given\gamma\right)\lor\left(\beta\Indep\alpha\lor\lambda\given\gamma\right),

\displaystyle\qquad\implies

\displaystyle\alpha\lor\lambda\Indep^{\lor}\beta\given\gamma.

∎

Appendix B
Measure Theory

In this section we review the necessary measure theory that is needed to prove the disintegration theorem for Markov kernels
in more generality. Since already the existence of disintegrations or regular conditional probability distributions is
in general dependent on the existence of countably compact approximating classes (see Pachl’s theorem in
[
Fre15
]
452I and remarks 452J, 452K) we need to dive into topological measure theory and descriptive set theory.
To short-cut this endeavor (i.e. to skip Souslin-F, K-analytic, analytic spaces, etc.) here
we directly define Souslin’s
\mathcal{S}
-operator on the level of measurable spaces (without the need for complicated combinatorics and topological considerations in the statements) by only defining it on
\sigma
-algebras and cite or rephrase the relevant results from
[
Kec95
]
,
[
Bog07
]
,
[
Fre15
]
, etc. directly for measurable spaces.
We also introduce the notion of (or generalize from separable metric spaces to) “universal measurable spaces”,
a measure space analogon to Radon spaces (plus some countability conditions).
Universal measurable spaces will have the a property that allows for general disintegrations.
We can not expect to get a general existence result for (regular) conditional Markov kernels in spaces far beyond these universal measurable spaces, but we will get stronger versions for the more restrictive standard and analytic measurable spaces.

B.1
Completion and Universal Completion of a Sigma-Algebra

Definition B.1

(Completion of a
\sigma
-algebra)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space and
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
the space of probability measures on

\mathcal{X}
or, more precisely, on its
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

1.

For
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
consider the set of
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
-null sets
of
\mathcal{X}
:

\mathcal{I}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}:=\left\{M\subseteq\mathcal{X}\,|\,\exists N\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}},\,M\subseteq N,\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(N)=0\right\}.

\mathcal{I}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is a
\sigma
-ideal, i.e. it contains
\emptyset
and is closed under subsets and countable unions.

2.

The
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
-completion
of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
is the following
\sigma
-algebra:

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=\left\{A\triangle M\,|\,A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}},M\in\mathcal{I}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\}.

We use the notation:
A\triangle M:=(A\cup M)\setminus(A\cap M)
.

Note that
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
can uniquely be extended to
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.

3.

We will write
\mathcal{X}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
to refer to
\mathcal{X}
equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.

Definition B.2

(Universal completion of a
\sigma
-algebra)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space.

1.

The
universal completion
of the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
is the following
\sigma
-algebra:

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}:=\bigcap_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\nu}},

i.e. the intersection of all
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
-completions of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

2.

The sets
A\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
will be called the
universally measurable subsets of
\mathcal{X}
.

3.

We will write
\mathcal{X}_{\bullet}
to refer to
\mathcal{X}
equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.

4.

We call
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
, resp.,
universally complete
if
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.

Remark B.3
.

For a measurable space
\mathcal{X}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
we clearly have the following inclusions:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}.

Notation B.4
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a set and
\mathcal{E}\subseteq 2^{\mathcal{X}}
a non-empty set of subsets of
\mathcal{X}
.
We then write:

(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}:=(\sigma(\mathcal{E}))_{\bullet}:=\bigcap_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\sigma(\mathcal{E}))}(\sigma(\mathcal{E}))_{\boldsymbol{\nu}},

for the smallest universally complete
\sigma
-algebra containing
\mathcal{E}
.

Definition B.5
.

Let
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a (not necessarily measurable) map between measurable spaces.

f
will be called
universally measurable

if it is
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
-measurable.

Lemma B.6
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be measurable spaces and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
.
Let
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a (not necessarily measurable) map.
We then have:

1.

If
f
is
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
-
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
-measurable
then also
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
-measurable,
where
f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is the push-forward measure of
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.

2.

If
f
is
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
-
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
-measurable then also
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
-measurable.

3.

f
is
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
-measurable if and only if it is

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
-measurable.

4.

If
f
is
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
-measurable
then also
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
-measurable.

Proof.

1.) If
B\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
then
B=C\triangle M
with
C\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and
M\subseteq N

with
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(f^{-1}(N))=0
for some
N\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Then
f^{-1}(B)=f^{-1}(C)\triangle f^{-1}(M)
with

f^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
,
f^{-1}(M)\subseteq f^{-1}(N)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(f^{-1}(N))=0
.
So
f^{-1}(B)\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.

2.) This follows from the previous point by noting that:

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}=\bigcap_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\subseteq\bigcap_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}.

3.) Clear from point 2.

4.) Same as point 1. with
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.
∎

Lemma B.7
.

Let
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a map and
\mathcal{E}\subseteq 2^{\mathcal{Y}}
a non-empty set.
Then we have the equalities:

\sigma(f^{*}\mathcal{E})=f^{*}(\sigma(\mathcal{E})),\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\left(f^{*}(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}\right)_{\bullet}=(f^{*}\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}.

Proof.

Recall:
f^{*}\mathcal{E}:=\left\{f^{-1}(E)\,|\,E\in\mathcal{E}\right\}
.
We clearly have the inclusions:

f^{*}\mathcal{E}\subseteq f^{*}(\sigma(\mathcal{E})):=\left\{f^{-1}(B)\,|\,B\in\sigma(\mathcal{E})\right\}.

Since the latter is a
\sigma
-algebra we get:

f^{*}\mathcal{E}\subseteq\sigma(f^{*}\mathcal{E})\subseteq f^{*}(\sigma(\mathcal{E})).

Then the
\sigma
-algebra:

f_{*}\sigma(f^{*}\mathcal{E})=\left\{C\subseteq\mathcal{Y}\,|\,f^{-1}(C)\in\sigma(f^{*}\mathcal{E})\right\}

contains
\mathcal{E}
, since clearly
f^{-1}(E)\in\sigma(f^{*}\mathcal{E})
for
E\in\mathcal{E}
.
It then follows that:

\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subseteq f_{*}\sigma(f^{*}\mathcal{E}),

from which we get:

f^{*}\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subseteq\sigma(f^{*}\mathcal{E}),

thus equality.

For the second claim first consider the inclusions:

f^{*}\mathcal{E}\subseteq\sigma(f^{*}\mathcal{E})\subseteq(f^{*}\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}.

Since
\sigma(f^{*}\mathcal{E})=(f^{*}\sigma(\mathcal{E})
we also get:

f^{*}\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subseteq(f^{*}\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}.

So
f
is
(f^{*}\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}
-
\sigma(\mathcal{E})
-measurable and thus

(f^{*}\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}
-measurable,
from which follows:

f^{*}(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}\subseteq(f^{*}\mathcal{E})_{\bullet},

which directly implies:

\left(f^{*}(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}\right)_{\bullet}\subseteq(f^{*}\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}.

So we have the chain of inlcusions:

f^{*}\mathcal{E}\subseteq\sigma(f^{*}\mathcal{E})=f^{*}(\sigma(\mathcal{E}))\subseteq f^{*}(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}\subseteq\left(f^{*}(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}\right)_{\bullet}\subseteq(f^{*}\mathcal{E})_{\bullet},

showing that
\left(f^{*}(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}\right)_{\bullet}
is universally complete and contains
f^{*}\mathcal{E}
, which shows:

\left(f^{*}(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}\right)_{\bullet}=(f^{*}\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}.

∎

B.2
Analytic Subsets of a Measurable Space

Definition B.8

(Analytic subsets, see
[
Fre15
]
421H, 423E)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space and
\mathbb{R}
be equipped with its Borel
\sigma
-algebra.

1.

A subset
A\subseteq\mathcal{X}
will be called
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
-analytic
,
Souslin-
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
or just
analytic
(if the context is clear)
if
A
is the projection of a measurable set
C\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
of the product space

\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{X}
:

A=\mathrm{pr}_{\mathcal{X}}(C):=\{x\in\mathcal{X}\,|\,\exists u\in\mathbb{R}:(u,x)\in C\}.

2.

The set of
analytic subsets
of
\mathcal{X}
is then abbreviated by:

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}):=\{\mathrm{pr}_{\mathcal{X}}(C)\,|\,C\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\}.

Note that
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
and that

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
is closed under countable unions and countable intersections, but in general NOT w.r.t. complements (see
[
Fre15
]
421E).

3.

We denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the analytic subsets by:
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
.

4.

We will write
\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{S}}
to refer to
\mathcal{X}
equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
.

Lemma B.9

(See
[
Fre15
]
421C (c)+(d))
.

Let
f:\,(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\to(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a measurable map between any measurable spaces.

1.

If
B\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
then
f^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
.

2.

If
f
is surjective,
A\in\mathcal{S}(f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
then
f(A)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
.

Note that we use:
f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}:=\{f^{-1}(B)\subseteq\mathcal{X}\,|\,B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\}
.

Lemma B.10

(See
[
Fre15
]
431A, 421D)
.

For any measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
and probability measure
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
we have the
inclusion of
\sigma
-algebras:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}.

Furthermore, we have the equalities:

\mathcal{S}((\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet})=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{S}((\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}.

Lemma B.11
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
be a a measurable space.
Then the identity maps:

\mathrm{id}:\;(\mathcal{X},(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet})\to(\mathcal{X},\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}))\to(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}),

are measurable.
They induce measurable bijective restriction maps:

\mathrm{res}:\;\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\bullet})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{S}})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}),\qquad{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{|},

where we endow the spaces of probability measures each with the smallest
\sigma
-algebra such that the evaluation maps:

j_{A}:\,\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B})\to[0,1],\quad j_{A}({\boldsymbol{\nu}}):={\boldsymbol{\nu}}(A),

are measurable for all
A\in\mathcal{B}
, where we use
\mathcal{B}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
,
\mathcal{B}=\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
,
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
, resp.
Note that the inverses of the bijective maps might not be measurable in general.

Lemma B.12
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be measurable spaces,
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
and
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a map.

1.

If
f
is
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
-
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
-measurable
then also
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
-
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
-measurable.

2.

If
f
is
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
-
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
-measurable
then also
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
-measurable.

3.

If
f
is
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
-measurable
then also
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
-measurable.

B.3
Countably Generated and Countably Separated Sigma-Algebras

Definition B.13

((Universally) countably generated
\sigma
-algebras)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
be a measurable space. Then
\mathcal{X}
or
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
are called:

1.

countably generated
if there exists a countable
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}

such that
\sigma(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

2.

universally countably generated
if there exists a countable
\mathcal{E}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}

such that
(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.

Clearly, the first implies the second.

Theorem B.14

(See
[
Bog07
]
Thm. 6.5.5)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space. The following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{X}
is countably generated.

2.

There exists a measurable map
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
such that
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}
.

Here
\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
[0,1]
. We denote
f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}:=\left\{f^{-1}(C)\,|\,C\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\right\}
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\sigma(\left\{A_{n}\,|\,n\in\mathbb{N}\right\})
then:

f:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}3^{-n}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}:\quad\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]

does the trick. The reverse is clear.
∎

Corollary B.15
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space. The following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{X}
is universally countably generated.

2.

There exists a universally measurable map

f:\,\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]

such that
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})_{\bullet}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathcal{X}
is universally countably generated.
Let
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}
with countable
\mathcal{E}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.
Then by Theorem
B.14
there exists a measurable map:

f:\,(\mathcal{X},\sigma(\mathcal{E}))\to([0,1],\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}),

such that
f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}=\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.
It thus follows that:
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(\sigma(\mathcal{E}))_{\bullet}=(f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})_{\bullet}
.
This shows the claim.

For the reverse implication now assume that
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})_{\bullet}
.
Since
\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}
is countably generated so is
f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}
and we get the claim.
∎

Remark B.16

(Universally generated
\sigma
-algebras)
.

The result from above could inspire another, seemingly more general, definition:
A measurable space
\mathcal{X}
could be called
universally generated

if there exists a universally measurable map
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
such that:

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=\left(f^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})_{\bullet}\right)_{\bullet}.

Since by Lemma
B.7
we have:

\left(f^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})_{\bullet}\right)_{\bullet}=\left(f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}\right)_{\bullet},

this definition is equivalent to
universally countably generated

\mathcal{X}
.
We thus get nothing new.

Definition B.17

(Separating the points)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a set and
\mathcal{E}\subseteq 2^{\mathcal{X}}
a subset.
We then say that
\mathcal{E}

separates the points
of
\mathcal{X}
if
for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}
with
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
there exists an
E\in\mathcal{E}
with:

x_{1}\in E
and
x_{2}\notin E
or
x_{1}\notin E
and
x_{2}\in E
.

Remark B.18

(See Lem.
B.39
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space.
Then the map:

\delta:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}),\quad x\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{x},

is measurable with
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\delta^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}
.
Furthermore, it is injective if and only if
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
separates the points of
\mathcal{X}
.

Lemma B.19
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a set and
\mathcal{E}\subseteq 2^{\mathcal{X}}
a subset.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{E}
separates the points of
\mathcal{X}
.

2.

\sigma(\mathcal{E})
separates the points of
\mathcal{X}
.

3.

(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}
separates the points of
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subseteq(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}
we have
1.\implies 2.\implies 3.

Now assume that
(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}
separates the points of
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\mathcal{X}
be endowed with
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}:=\sigma(\mathcal{E})

and
\mathcal{X}_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{X},(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet})
. We then have the commutative diagram of measurable maps:

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}).}

Now the vertical maps are bijective. By Lemma
B.39
the upper
\delta
is injective
because
(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}
separates the points of
\mathcal{X}
.
This then implies that the lower
\delta
is also injective, which again by Lemma
B.39

implies that
\sigma(\mathcal{E})
separates the points of
\mathcal{X}
.
If now
\mathcal{E}
would not separate the points of
\mathcal{X}
, say
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
, then the
\sigma
-algebra:

\left\{A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\,|\,\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\subseteq A\,\lor\,\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\subseteq A^{\mathsf{c}}\right\}

contains
\mathcal{E}
and thus
\sigma(\mathcal{E})
, in contradiction to the assumption. Thus already
\mathcal{E}
must separate the points of
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

Definition B.20

((Universally) countably separated
\sigma
-algebras)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
be a measurable space. Then
\mathcal{X}
or
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
are called:

1.

countably separated
if there exists a countable
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
that separates the points of
\mathcal{X}
.

2.

universally countably separated
if there exists a countable
\mathcal{E}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}

that separates the points of
\mathcal{X}
.

Clearly, the first implies the second.

Theorem B.21

(See
[
Bog07
]
Thm. 6.5.7)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space and

\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}:=\left\{(x,x)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\,|\,x\in\mathcal{X}\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
its diagonal.
The following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{X}
is countably separated.

2.

There exists an injective measurable map
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
.

3.

\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{E}=\left\{A_{n}\,|\,n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
separates the points of
\mathcal{X}
then:

f:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}3^{-n}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}:\quad\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]

is injective and measurable. For the rest see
[
Bog07
]
Thm. 6.5.7.
∎

Corollary B.22
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space.
The following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{X}
is universally countably separated.

2.

There exists an injective universally measurable map
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
.

3.

\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}\otimes(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.

Proof.

This follows directly from Theorem
B.21
and Definition
B.20
.
For this note that we have the equivalence between
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
-measurability and

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
-measurability.
∎

This result inspires another definition:

Definition B.23

(Universally separated
\sigma
-algebras)
.

A measurable space
\mathcal{X}
will be called
universally separated

if
\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.

Lemma B.24
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space.

1.

If
\mathcal{X}
is countably separated then we have
\{x\}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

2.

If
\mathcal{X}
is universally (countably) separated then we have
\{x\}\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

Let
\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}:=\left\{(x,x)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\,|\,x\in\mathcal{X}\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}
be the diagonal
of
\mathcal{X}
.
Fix
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
.
For any
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
on
\mathcal{X}
the identity map
\mathrm{id}:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
and the constant map

c_{x_{0}}:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
with
c_{x_{0}}(x):=x_{0}
are
\mathcal{B}
-
\mathcal{B}
-measurable.
This implies that the map:

\iota_{x_{0}}:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X},\quad x\mapsto(x,x_{0}),

is
\mathcal{B}
-
(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B})
-measurable and thus
(\mathcal{B})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B})_{\bullet}
-measurable.
This implies that
\{x_{0}\}=\iota_{x_{0}}^{-1}(\Delta_{\mathcal{X}})\in\mathcal{B}
if
\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B}

and
\{x_{0}\}=\iota_{x_{0}}^{-1}(\Delta_{\mathcal{X}})\in(\mathcal{B})_{\bullet}
if
\Delta_{\mathcal{X}}\in(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B})_{\bullet}
.

Each of the cases is implied by the assumptions by Theorem
B.21
, Corollary

B.22
and Definition
B.23
.
∎

Corollary B.25
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space. The following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{X}
is countably generated and countably separated.

2.

There exists an injective measurable map
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]
such that
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\sigma(\mathcal{E})
and
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
separates the points of
\mathcal{X}
with countable
\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{F}

then with
