\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}=\left\{A_{n}\,|\,n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
:
and

f:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}3^{-n}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}:\quad\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]

we get the claim. The reverse is clear.
∎

Corollary B.26
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space. The following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{X}
is universally countably generated and universally countably separated.

2.

There exists an injective universally measurable map
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to[0,1]

such that
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})_{\bullet}
.

Proof.

Assume the first. If
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}
and
\mathcal{F}

separates the points of
\mathcal{X}
with countable
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}

then put
\mathcal{G}:=\mathcal{E}\cup\mathcal{F}=\left\{A_{n}\,|\,n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
.
Again using:

f:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}3^{-n}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}:\quad\mathcal{X}\to[0,1],

gives us an injective
\sigma(\mathcal{G})
-
\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}
-measurable map
f
with

f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}=\sigma(\mathcal{G})\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.
From this it follow that
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(\sigma(\mathcal{G}))_{\bullet}=(f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})_{\bullet}
.
This shows the claim.

The reverse direction is clear from
Corollaries
B.22
and
B.15
.
∎

B.4
Standard, Analytic and Universal Measurable Spaces

Definition B.27
.

Let
([0,1],\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})
be the unit interval with its Borel
\sigma
-algebra.
A measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
will be called:

1.

standard (Borel)
if
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\cong(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]|\mathcal{Y}})
with a
\mathcal{Y}\in\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}
,

2.

analytic
if
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\cong(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]|\mathcal{Y}})
with a
\mathcal{Y}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})
,

3.

countably perfect
if
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\cong(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]|\mathcal{Y}})
with a
\mathcal{Y}\in(\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})_{\bullet}
.

4.

universal
if
(\mathcal{X},(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet})\cong(\mathcal{Y},(\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]|\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet})
with a
\mathcal{Y}\in(\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})_{\bullet}
.

Here “
\cong
” means “measurably isomorphic” (i.e. using a measurable map with a measurable inverse) and we will in the following
write
\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}}
for the induced Borel (sub-)
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]|\mathcal{Y}}
in
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
(for universal measurable spaces). In the first cases of course:
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}}
.

Remark B.28
.

1.

The inclusion
\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})_{\bullet}
shows that every standard measurable space is also an analytic measurable space and
every analytic measurable space is countably perfect
and (the universal completion of)
any countably perfect measurable space is a universal measurable space.

2.

Note that in the definition of universal measurable spaces we have

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
, but we don’t need to have
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}}
. Since the definition only depends on the universal completion we, nevertheless, can always replace
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
with
\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}}
, when needed and which usually has the better properties.

Lemma B.29
.

1.

Any countable product of standard, analytic, countably perfect or universal measurable spaces, resp., is of the same type.

2.

If
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
is a standard, analytic, countably perfect or universal measurable space then
\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}}
is countably generated and countably separated (cp.
[
Bog07
]
6.5.8).

Proof.

This directly follows from the Borel isomorphism
[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}\cong[0,1]
(see
[
Fre15
]
424C).
∎

Example B.30

(Standard measurable spaces, see
[
Bog07
]
6.8.8,
[
Fre15
]
424C)
.

Any
Polish space

\mathcal{X}
, i.e. a separable completely metrizable space (e.g.
\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
[0,1]
,
\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}
, any topological manifold, any countable simplicial complex, etc.), is a standard measurable space with its Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Example B.31

(Analytic measurable spaces, see
[
Bog07
]
6.7.4)
.

Any
Souslin space

\mathcal{X}
, i.e. a Hausdorff space that is the continuous image of a Polish space, is an analytic measurable space with its Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

B.5
Perfect Measures and Perfect Measurable Spaces

Definition B.32

(Perfect measures and perfect measurable spaces)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space.
Then we call:

1.

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})

perfect
if for all measurable maps:

f:\;\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R},

we have that:

f_{*}((\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}})_{f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}.

In particular:
f(\mathcal{X})\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}})_{f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
.

2.

\mathcal{X}

perfect
if every
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
is perfect.

Note that we define the push-forward
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathcal{B}
along a map
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
as:

f_{*}\mathcal{B}:=\left\{C\subseteq\mathcal{Y}\,|\,f^{-1}(C)\in\mathcal{B}\right\}.

Theorem B.33

(See
[
Bog07
]
7.5.7 (i), 7.5.7 (iii), 6.5.8, 7.5.6 (iv)))
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{X}
is countably perfect.

2.

\mathcal{X}
is countably generated, countably separated and perfect.

3.

\mathcal{X}
is countably generated, countably separated and every probability measure
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})

has a countably compact approximating class
(see
[
Fre15
]
451A or
[
Bog07
]
§1.12(ii) for precise definitions).

4.

\mathcal{X}
is countably generated, countably separated and there exists a (countably) compact class that approximates
every probability measure
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
.

Corollary B.34
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{X}
is a universal measurable space.

2.

\mathcal{X}
is universally countably generated, univerally countably separated and perfect.

3.

\mathcal{X}
is universally countably generated, universally countably separated and every probability measure
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
has a countably compact approximating class.

4.

\mathcal{X}
is universally countably generated, universally countably separated and there exists a (countably) compact class that approximates
every probability measure
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
.

Example B.35
.

1.

Every universally measurable subset
\mathcal{X}\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
of an analytic or standard measurable space
\mathcal{Y}
is a countably perfect space when equipped with the subspace
\sigma
-algebra of measurable sets:
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|\mathcal{X}}
.

2.

Every universally measurable subset
\mathcal{X}\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
of an analytic or standard measurable space
\mathcal{Y}
is a countably perfect space when equipped with the subspace
\sigma
-algebra of universal
measurable sets:
((\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet})_{|\mathcal{X}}
.

Example B.36
.

Consider a
Radon space

\mathcal{X}
, i.e. a Hausdorff space where every Borel probability measure is
inner regular w.r.t. the (closed) compact subsets. Let
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
be the Borel
\sigma
-algebra.
A
countable network
for the topology is a
countable set
\mathcal{E}\subseteq 2^{\mathcal{X}}
such that every open set
O
can be written as:

O=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\in\mathcal{E}\\
A\subseteq O\end{subarray}}A.

Then we have the following:

1.

If
\mathcal{X}
has a countable network
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
then
\mathcal{X}
is a countably perfect measurable space.

2.

If
\mathcal{X}
has a countable network
\mathcal{E}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
then
\mathcal{X}
is a universal measurable space.

Proof.

The first statement is similar to the second.

The second statement follows from
B.34
. For this let
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{X}}
be the set of open sets.
By assumption every open set
A\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{X}}
is the (countable) union of elements of
\mathcal{E}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
. So we have:
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{E})
and thus:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\sigma(\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{X}})\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}.

Since
\mathcal{X}
is Hausdorff
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{X}}
separates points, so
\mathcal{E}
as well.
Furthermore, on Radon spaces every Borel measure is Radon. And in general every Radon measure is perfect (see
[
Bog07
]
7.5.10(i)).
Or, alternatively, the compact sets build a compact approximating class for every Borel measure.
So
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
is a universal measurable space.

Also see
[
Fre15
]
451M for a bit more general situation.
∎

B.6
The Space of Probability Measures

Remark B.37

(The
\sigma
-algebra on the space of probability measures)
.

Recall that for any measurable space
\mathcal{X}
we endow the space of probability measures
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
with
the smallest
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}
such that all the evaluation maps:

j_{A}:\,\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\to[0,1],\qquad j_{A}({\boldsymbol{\nu}}):={\boldsymbol{\nu}}(A),

are measurable for
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

Lemma B.38
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
be a measurable space and
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\sigma(\mathcal{E})
with a set
\mathcal{E}
that is closed under
pairwise intersections.
Then we have:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}=\sigma\left(j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\,|\,A\in\mathcal{E},\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}\right).

Note that:

j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])=\{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\,|\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A)>\alpha\}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{E}}:=\sigma\left(\left\{j_{A}^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}\,|\,A\in\mathcal{E}\right\}\right)
.
The system:

\mathcal{D}:=\left\{A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\,|\,j_{A}=({\boldsymbol{\mu}}\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A))\text{ is }\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{E}}\text{-}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}\text{-measurable}\,\right\}

is a Dynkin system that contains the
\pi
-system
\mathcal{E}
.
So

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subseteq\mathcal{D}
by Dynkin’s lemma (see
[
Kle20
]
Thm. 1.19).
So all
j_{A}
are
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{E}}
-
\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}
-measurable.
So by definition of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}
as the smallest
\sigma
-algebra for which all
j_{A}
are measurable we get:

\sigma\left(\left\{j_{A}^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}\,|\,A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\right\}\right)=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{E}}=\sigma\left(\left\{j_{A}^{*}\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}\,|\,A\in\mathcal{E}\right\}\right).

Thus equality:
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{E}}
.
Since
\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}=\sigma(\mathcal{F})
with the countable
\pi
-system
\mathcal{F}:=\{(\alpha,1]\,|\,\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}\}
we get:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}=\sigma\left(j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\,|\,A\in\mathcal{E},\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}\right).

∎

Lemma B.39
.

Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z}
be measurable spaces.

1.

If
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is measurable then the induced map:

f_{*}:\,\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}),\quad{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\mapsto f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(B\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(f^{-1}(B)))

is measurable as well.

2.

The map:

\delta:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}),\quad x\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{x},

is measurable and
\delta^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
.
\delta
is injective iff
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
separates points.

3.

The map:

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})&\to&\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})\\
({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})&\mapsto&{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\end{array}

is measurable.

4.

If
g:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Z}
is measurable then the map:

\begin{array}[]{crlll}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{Y}&\to&\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z}),\\
({\boldsymbol{\mu}},y)&\mapsto&g^{y}_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=g_{*}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{y})\\
&=&(C\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\{x\in\mathcal{X}\,|\,g(x,y)\in C\}))\end{array}

is measurable as well.

Proof.

1.) For every
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
we have:

(j_{B}\circ f_{*})({\boldsymbol{\mu}})={\boldsymbol{\mu}}(f^{-1}(B))=j_{f^{-1}(B)}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}).

Since the latter is measurable in
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
by definition the former is as well. Since this holds for all
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
the measurability of
f_{*}
is shown.

2.) Let
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
then we have:

x\mapsto j_{A}\circ{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{x}=\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)

is measurable in
x
. So
x\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{x}
is measurable.

3.) For
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
the composition of maps:

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{A}\times j_{B}}}{{\longrightarrow}}[0,1]\times[0,1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{\longrightarrow}}[0,1],\quad({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A)\cdot{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(B),

is clearly measurable. Since this map agrees with the following map:

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{A\times B}}}{{\longrightarrow}}[0,1],\quad({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A)\cdot{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(B),

we have the measurability of the latter map as well.
Now let:

\mathcal{D}:=\{D\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\,|\,({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\mapsto({\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\nu}})(D)\text{ is measurable}\}.

By aboves arguments we see that
\mathcal{D}
contains the system
\mathcal{E}:=\{A\times B\,|\,A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}},B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\}
, which is stable under finite intersections.
Since
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
are probability measures it is easily verified that with
D\in\mathcal{D}
also
D^{c}\in\mathcal{D}
and with
D_{n}\in\mathcal{D}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, pairwise disjoint also
\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}D_{n}\in\mathcal{D}
. So
\mathcal{D}
is a Dynkin system. By Dynkin’s lemma (see
[
Kle20
]
Thm. 1.19) we see that:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subseteq\mathcal{D}.

This means that for every
D\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
the composition of maps:

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}[0,1],\quad({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\mapsto({\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\nu}})(D),

is measurable. So the map

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}),\quad({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\nu}},

is measurable by definition of the induced
\sigma
-algebras.

4.) If
g:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Z}
and
C\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
then by the previous points we get the measurability of the maps:

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{id}\times{\boldsymbol{\delta}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\otimes}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g_{*}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{C}}}{{\longrightarrow}}[0,1].

It maps
({\boldsymbol{\mu}},y)\mapsto(g_{*}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{y}))(C)
. Furthermore, we have:

g^{-1}(C)^{y}=\{x\in\mathcal{X}\,|\,g(x,y)\in C\}=(g^{y})^{-1}(C).

Furthermore, for
D\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
we have:

({\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{y})(D)={\boldsymbol{\mu}}(D^{y}),

which again by Dynkin’s lemma is enough to test on sets
D=A\times B
, where it is obvious:

({\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{y})(A\times B)={\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)={\boldsymbol{\mu}}((A\times B)^{y}).

Together we get:

j_{C}(g^{y}_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=(g^{y}_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}})(C)={\boldsymbol{\mu}}(g^{-1}(C)^{y})=({\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{y})(g^{-1}(C))=j_{C}(g_{*}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{y})).

Since the latter is measurable in
({\boldsymbol{\mu}},y)
by aboves arguments also the former is. Since this holds for all
C\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
we have shown the measurability of the claimed map:
({\boldsymbol{\mu}},y)\mapsto g^{y}_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.
∎

Lemma B.40
.

If
\mathcal{X}
is countably generated then
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
is countably generated and countably separated.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\sigma(\mathcal{E})
with countable
\mathcal{E}
, then the algebra generated by
\mathcal{E}
is countable and closed under
finite intersections, so w.l.o.g.
\mathcal{E}
an algebra. Then
\mathcal{E}\times\mathbb{Q}
is countable and
we have by Lemma
B.38
:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}=\sigma\left(\left\{j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\,\Big|\,A\in\mathcal{E},\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}\right\}\right).

This shows that
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}
is countably generated.
Now consider
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
with
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\neq{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
.
Assume that
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A)={\boldsymbol{\nu}}(A)
for all
A\in\mathcal{E}
. Then define:

\mathcal{D}:=\left\{A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\,|\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A)={\boldsymbol{\nu}}(A)\right\}.

Then
\mathcal{D}
is a Dynkin system that contains the
\pi
-system
\mathcal{E}
.
So
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subseteq\mathcal{D}
by Dynkin’s lemma (see
[
Kle20
]
Thm. 1.19).
But this means
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
agree on
all
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
and are thus equal, in contradiction to the assumption.
So there must be an
A\in\mathcal{E}
such that
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(A)\neq{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A)
. So there exists an
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
with
either
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(A)>\alpha>{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A)
or
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(A)<\alpha<{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A)
.
In the first case

{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\notin j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])

and in the second case

{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\notin j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])
and
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])
.
So the above system also separates points of
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
.
This shows the claim.
∎

Theorem B.41

(See
[
Res77
]
Thm. 3)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be an analytic measurable space.

1.

If
A\in\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
then
j_{A}
is

\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})})
-
\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}
-measurable.

2.

If
A\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})

then
j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})})
for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem B.42

(See
[
Res77
]
Thm. 4)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be any measurable space.

1.

If
A\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
then
j_{A}
is
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})})_{\bullet}
-
\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}
-measurable.

2.

We have the inclusions:
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\bullet})}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\bullet})})_{\bullet}
.

Proof.

The first statement is due to
[
Res77
]
Thm. 4. We reproduce the proof here.
Let
A\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.
For the claim we need to show that

j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})})_{\bullet}
for
all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. So we fix
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}))
.
It is thus enough to show that
j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})})_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
.
Now define
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
for
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
as follows:

{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(B):=\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}\mu(B)\,d{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(\mu)=\int_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}j_{B}(\mu)\,d{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(\mu).

Since
A\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
there exist:
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}

such that:

B_{1}\subseteq A\subseteq B_{2},\qquad{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(B_{2}\setminus B_{2})=0.

The last condition shows:

0={\boldsymbol{\nu}}(B_{2}\setminus B_{1})=\int j_{B_{2}\setminus B_{1}}(\mu)\,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(\mu).

Since
j_{B_{2}\setminus B_{1}}(\mu)\geq 0
and
j_{B_{2}\setminus B_{1}}:\,\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable
we get that:

C:=j_{B_{2}\setminus B_{1}}^{-1}((0,1])=\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\,|\,j_{B_{2}\setminus B_{1}}(\mu)>0\}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(C)=0.

We now have the inclusions:

j_{B_{1}}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\subseteq j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\subseteq j_{B_{2}}^{-1}((\alpha,1]),

with
j_{B_{i}}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}
,
i=1,2
, and:

j_{B_{2}}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\setminus j_{B_{1}}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\subseteq j_{B_{2}\setminus B_{1}}^{-1}((0,1])=C.

Indeed, if
\mu(B_{2})>\alpha
and
\mu(B_{1})\leq\alpha
, then:

\mu(B_{2}\setminus B_{1})=\mu(B_{2})-\mu(B_{1})>\alpha-\alpha=0.

Since we have
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(C)=0
we also have:

0\leq{\boldsymbol{\rho}}\left(j_{B_{2}}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\setminus j_{B_{1}}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\right)\leq{\boldsymbol{\rho}}(C)=0.

This shows that
j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})})_{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
,
which shows the first claim
as
\alpha
and
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}
were arbitrary.

Now consider the second statement.

The inclusion
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\bullet})}
is clear since
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.

The inclusion
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\bullet})}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})})_{\bullet}
follows from the first point,
since
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\bullet})}
is per definition the smallest
\sigma
-algebra such that all
j_{A}
for

A\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
are measurable.

The equality
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\bullet})})_{\bullet}
follows from the sandwich:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\bullet})}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})})_{\bullet}.

∎

Corollary B.43
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be universally countably generated then
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
is universally countably generated and universally countably
separated.

Proof.

By assumption we have a countably generated
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{E}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
such

(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.
By Lemma
B.40
we have that
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{E})
is
countably generated and countably separated.
If we now apply Theorem
B.42
twice, once to
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
and once to
\mathcal{E}
, we get the equalities:

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{E})})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet})})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet})})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})})_{\bullet}.

So
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{E})}
is a countably generated and countably separated
\sigma
-algebra that universally generates

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})})_{\bullet}
. This shows the claim.
∎

Theorem B.44

(See
[
Sch74
]
Appendix §5 Thm. 7+8)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a standard measurable space, an analytic measurable space, resp.,
then the space of probability measures

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
is also a standard measurable space, an analytic measurable space, resp.,
(in the
\sigma
-algebra from this section).

Lemma B.45
.

Let
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a measurable map between measurable spaces
with
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Then the map:

f_{*}:\,\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}),\quad{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\mapsto f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}},

is injective, measurable and satisfies:
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}=(f_{*})^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})}
.

Proof.

f_{*}
is measurable since for any
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
we have:

j_{B}(f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}})=j_{f^{-1}(B)}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}),

and
j_{f^{-1}(B)}
is measurable since
f^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
.

f_{*}
is injective. Indeed, if
f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
and
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is of the form
A=f^{-1}(B)

with
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
then:

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A)={\boldsymbol{\mu}}(f^{-1}(B))=(f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}})(B)=(f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\nu}})(B)={\boldsymbol{\nu}}(f^{-1}(B))={\boldsymbol{\nu}}(A),

which implies
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}={\boldsymbol{\nu}}
.

The measurability of
f_{*}
shows:
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}\supseteq(f_{*})^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})}
.

For the reverse, consider generators:
j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}

and
A=f^{-1}(B)\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
We then get:

\displaystyle j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])

\displaystyle=\left\{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\,|\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A)>\alpha\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\,|\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(f^{-1}(B))>\alpha\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\,|\,(f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}})(B)>\alpha\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\,|\,j_{B}((f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}))\in(\alpha,1]\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\,|\,(f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}})\in j_{B}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\,|\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in(f_{*})^{-1}(j_{B}^{-1}((\alpha,1]))\right\}

\displaystyle=(f_{*})^{-1}(j_{B}^{-1}((\alpha,1]))

\displaystyle\in(f_{*})^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})}.

This implies:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}=\sigma\left(\left\{j_{A}^{-1}((\alpha,1])\,|\,\alpha\in\mathbb{R},A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}\right\}\right)\subseteq(f_{*})^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})}.

∎

Theorem B.46
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be an analytic measurable space and
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
,
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
, resp.
Then the inclusion map
\iota:\,(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|\mathcal{X}})\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})

induces a canonical measurable isomorphism:

\iota_{*}:\,\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|\mathcal{X}})\cong\{\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})\,|\,\nu(\mathcal{X})=1\}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}).

Furthermore,
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|\mathcal{X}})\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})}
,

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|\mathcal{X}})\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})})
, resp.

Proof.

W.l.o.g. assume
\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
The bijection can be found in
[
Fre15
]
437Q, 434F(c).
The additional claims follow from
[
HK99
]
,
[
Bog07
]
8.10.28.
∎

Lemma B.47
.

Let
f:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a measurable injective map between countably perfect measurable spaces
with
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Then
f(\mathcal{X})\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
and

f_{*}
induces an identification:

f_{*}:\quad\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\cong\left\{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\,|\,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(f(\mathcal{X}))=1\right\},

where the latter lies in
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})})_{\bullet}
.

Proof.

Since by definition we can embed
\mathcal{Y}
into
[0,1]
we can for the first statement w.l.o.g. assume
\mathcal{Y}=[0,1]
.
Since
\mathcal{X}
is perfect by
[
Bog07
]
Thm. 7.5.7 (i) we get the claim:
f(\mathcal{X})\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
.

Since
f(\mathcal{X})\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
we can evaluate at
f(\mathcal{X})
.
By Theorem
B.42
the evaluation map
j_{f(\mathcal{X})}
is
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})})_{\bullet}
-
\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]}
-measurable.
So we get that:

\mathcal{P}_{1}:=\left\{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\,|\,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(f(\mathcal{X}))=1\right\}=j_{f(\mathcal{X})}^{-1}(1)\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})})_{\bullet}.

By Lemma
B.45
we know that
f_{*}
is injective, measurable and satisfies:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})}=(f_{*})^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})}
. It is clear that
f_{*}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{1}
.

If now
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}
then we define for
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
:

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(A):={\boldsymbol{\nu}}(B\cap f(\mathcal{X}))

for
A=f^{-1}(B)
,
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is well-defined.
If
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
with
f^{-1}(B_{1})=A=f^{-1}(B_{2})
then, because of the injectivity of
f
, we get:

B_{1}\cap f(\mathcal{X})=B_{2}\cap f(\mathcal{X})
.
Also note that
B\cap f(\mathcal{X})\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
can be measured by
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}
.

It is clear that
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is a probability measure and satisfies
f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}={\boldsymbol{\nu}}
.

(f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}})(B)={\boldsymbol{\mu}}(f^{-1}(B))={\boldsymbol{\nu}}(B\cap f(\mathcal{X}))={\boldsymbol{\nu}}(B\cap f(\mathcal{X}))+\underbrace{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(B\cap f(\mathcal{X})^{\mathsf{c}})}_{=0}={\boldsymbol{\nu}}(B).

∎

Corollary B.48
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a countably perfect measurable space
then the space of probability measures
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
is also a countably perfect measurable space.

Proof.

\mathcal{X}
by definition can be embedded as a universally measurable set into
\mathcal{Y}=[0,1]
.
By Lemma
B.47
we get that

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
is measurably isomorphic to a universally measurable subset of
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
.
By Theorem
B.44

\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
is a standard measurable space.
A universally measurable subset of standard measurable space with the induced
\sigma
-algebra
is countably perfect (see
[
Bog07
]
Thm. 7.5.7 (i)).
∎

Corollary B.49
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a universal measurable space
then the space of probability measures
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
is a universal measurable space.

Proof.

By definition there is a countably generated and countably separated
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}

such that
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}})
is countably perfect and
(\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.
By Corollary
B.48
the space
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}})
is countably perfect.
By Theorem
B.42
we also have the equalities:

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}})})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},(\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet})})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet})})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})})_{\bullet}.

So
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})})_{\bullet}
is the universal completion of the
\sigma
-algebra of a countably perfect space.
This shows that
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
is a universal measurable space.
∎

B.7
Measurable Selection Theorem

Lemma B.50

(See
[
Fre15
]
423G)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
be an analytic measurable space and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
a countably separated measurable space (e.g. another analytic measurable space) and let
f:\,(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\to(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a measurable map. We then have:

A\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\,\implies\,f(A)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}).

We also have that the graph
\Gamma_{f}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
.

Proof.

See
[
Fre15
]
423G for analytic measurable spaces. If
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
is countably separated we find an injective measurable map

\iota:\,(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})\hookrightarrow([0,1],\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})

by
[
Bog07
]
Thm. 6.5.7. Then by the previous argument
\iota(f(A))\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})
and because of the injectivity

f(A)=\iota^{-1}(\iota(f(A)))\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
.
∎

Theorem B.51

(Measurable selection theorem,
[
Bog07
]
Thm. 6.9.12)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
be an analytic measurable space and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
be any measurable space.
Let
D\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
.
Then
\mathrm{pr}_{\mathcal{Y}}(D)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
and
there exists a
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|\mathrm{pr}_{\mathcal{Y}}(D)})
-
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
-measurable map:

g:\,\ \mathrm{pr}_{\mathcal{Y}}(D)\to\mathcal{X},

such that
(g(y),y)\in D\subseteq\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
for every
y\in\mathrm{pr}_{\mathcal{Y}}(D)
.

Corollary B.52

(
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B})
-measurable right inverse)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
be an analytic measurable space and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
a countably separated measurable space (e.g. another analytic measurable space) and let
f:\,(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\to(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a measurable map.
Then there exists a
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|f(\mathcal{X})})
-
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
-measurable map:

g:\,f(\mathcal{X})\to\mathcal{X}

such that
f(g(y))=y
for all
y\in f(\mathcal{X})
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the measurable selection theorem

B.51
with
D=\Gamma_{f}\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
(and lemma
B.50
).
∎

The following corollary highlights the power of working in the category of analytic measurable spaces.

Corollary B.53

(Maps between analytic measurable spaces)
.

Let
f:\,(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\to(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a measurable map between analytic measurable spaces.
Then
f(\mathcal{X})\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
,
(f(\mathcal{X}),\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|f(\mathcal{X})})
is an analytic measurable space and
f
factors into a surjective and an injective measurable map:

f:\,(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\twoheadrightarrow(f(\mathcal{X}),\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|f(\mathcal{X})})\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}).

Furthermore, the induced maps also give a factorization into a surjective and an injective measurable map of analytic measurable spaces (w.r.t. induced
\sigma
-algebras):

f_{*}:\,(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}),\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})})\twoheadrightarrow(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|f(\mathcal{X})}),\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|f(\mathcal{X})})})\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}),\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})})

with
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|f(\mathcal{X})})\in\mathcal{S}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})}\right)
.
We get the identification of analytic measurable spaces:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}|f(\mathcal{X})})&=&\{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})\,|\,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\mathcal{Y}\setminus f(\mathcal{X}))=0\}.\end{array}

This shows the equality of sets:

\{f_{*}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\,|\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\}=\{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})\,|\,{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\mathcal{Y}\setminus f(\mathcal{X}))=0\}.

B.8
Measurable Extension Theorems

Theorem B.54

(Kuratowski extension theorem for standard measurable spaces, see
[
Kec95
]
12.2)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
be any measurable space,

\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
any subset endowed with the subspace
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}|\mathcal{W}}

and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a standard measurable space.
Let
f:\,(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}|\mathcal{W}})\to(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a measurable map.
Then there exists a measurable map:

F:\,(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\to(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})

such that the restriction to
\mathcal{W}
equals
f
, i.e.
F_{|\mathcal{W}}=f
.

In short: There exists
F
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}|\mathcal{W}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists F}

Corollary B.55

(Extensions of Kuratowski’s extension theorem)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
be any measurable space,

\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
any subset endowed with the subspace
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}|\mathcal{W}}

and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
be an analytic measurable space, a countably perfect measurable space, resp.
Let
f:\,(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}|\mathcal{W}})\to(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a measurable map.
Then there exists a measurable map:

F:\,(\mathcal{X},\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}))\to(\mathcal{Y},\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})),

a measurable map:

F:\,(\mathcal{X},(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet})\to(\mathcal{Y},(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}),

resp., such that the restriction to
\mathcal{W}
equals
f
, i.e.
F_{|\mathcal{W}}=f
.

Proof.

Since
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
is analytic, countably perfect, resp., we have a
a standard measurable space

\mathcal{Z}=[0,1]
and a
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
-
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
-measurable
injective map
j:\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}
with

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}=j^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
and
j(\mathcal{Y})\in\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})
,
j(\mathcal{Y})\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
, resp.

By Kuratowski’s extension theorem
B.54
we now have a measurable extension of
j\circ f
:

G:\;(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\to(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}).

So the following outer diagram commutes:

\textstyle{(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}|\mathcal{W}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists G}
\scriptstyle{\exists?F}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}).}

Now let
\mathcal{C}:=G^{-1}\left(j(\mathcal{Y})\right)
and
y_{0}\in\mathcal{Y}
be an arbitrary point.
Then
\mathcal{C}\in\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
,
\mathcal{C}\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
, resp., and we define:

\begin{array}[]{cccc}F:&\mathcal{X}&\to&\mathcal{Y},\\
F_{|\mathcal{C}}:&\mathcal{C}&\to&\mathcal{Y},\\
&x&\mapsto&j^{-1}(G(x)),\\
F_{|\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{c}}}:&\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{c}}&\to&\mathcal{Y},\\
&x&\mapsto&y_{0}.\end{array}

For
B\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
we have:

F^{-1}(B)=\left(F_{|\mathcal{C}}^{-1}(B)\cap\mathcal{C}\right)\cup\left(F_{|\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{c}}}^{-1}(B)\cap\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{c}}\right).

It is thus clear that
F
is
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
-
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
-measurable,

F
is
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
-measurable, resp.

Since
G(w)=j(f(w))\in j(\mathcal{Y})
we have that:
w\in\mathcal{C}
and
F(w)=j^{-1}(G(w))=f(w)
.
∎

Corollary B.56

(Kuratowski’s extension theorem for universal measurable spaces)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
be any measurable space,

\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
any subset and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
be a universal measurable space.
Let

f:\,(\mathcal{W},(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet,|\mathcal{W}})\to(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})

be a measurable map.
Then there exists a universally measurable map:

F:\,(\mathcal{X},(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet})\to(\mathcal{Y},(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet})

such that the restriction to
\mathcal{W}
equals
f
, i.e.
F_{|\mathcal{W}}=f
.

Proof.

Since
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
is universal we have an universal embedding into a standard measurable space

(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})
(in fact
[0,1]
), i.e. an
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
-measurable
injective map
j:\mathcal{Y}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Z}
with

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}=j^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
and
j(\mathcal{Y})\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
.

Let
\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}}=j^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
. We consider
f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet,|\mathcal{W}}
.
Then we can pick for every
A\in f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}}
a
\hat{A}\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
such that:

A=\hat{A}\cap\mathcal{W}.

We then consider the sub-
\sigma
-algebra:

\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{X}}=\sigma\left(\left\{\hat{A}\,|\,A\in f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}}\right\}\right)\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}.

It is clear that
\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{X}|\mathcal{W}}=f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}}
and that
j\circ f
is

(f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}})
-
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
-measurable.

By Kuratowski’s extension theorem
B.54
we now have a measurable extension of

j\circ f:\,(\mathcal{W},f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}})\to(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})
to
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}},\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{X}})
:

G:\;(\mathcal{X},\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{X}})\to(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}).

So the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{(\mathcal{W},f^{*}\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{X},\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{X}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exists G}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}).}

Now let
\mathcal{C}:=G^{-1}\left(j(\mathcal{Y})\right)
and
y_{0}\in\mathcal{Y}
be an arbitrary point.
Then
\mathcal{C}\in(\hat{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
and we define:

\begin{array}[]{rlcc}\hat{G}:&\mathcal{X}&\to&\mathcal{Y},\\
\hat{G}_{|\mathcal{C}}=G_{|\mathcal{C}}:&\mathcal{C}&\to&\mathcal{Z},\\
&x&\mapsto&G(x),\\
\hat{G}_{|\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{c}}}:&\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{c}}&\to&\mathcal{Z},\\
&x&\mapsto&j(y_{0}).\end{array}

For
C\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
we have:

\hat{G}^{-1}(C)=\left(G_{|\mathcal{C}}^{-1}(C)\cap\mathcal{C}\right)\cup\underbrace{\left(\hat{G}_{|\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{c}}}^{-1}(C)\cap\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{c}}\right)}_{=\emptyset\text{ or }\mathcal{C}^{\mathsf{c}}}.

It is thus clear that
\hat{G}
is
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
-measurable.
Furthermore,
G(\mathcal{X})\subseteq j(\mathcal{Y})
, where
j
is injective. So we can define
F:=j^{-1}\circ G
.
Then
F
is
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
-measurable because
j\circ F=\hat{G}
is

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
-measurable
and:

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}=j^{*}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
.

Finally, for
w\in\mathcal{W}
we have
j(f(w))\in j(\mathcal{Y})
, so:

F(w)=j^{-1}\circ G(w)=j^{-1}(j(f(w)))=f(w).

∎

Appendix C
Proofs - Disintegration of Transition Probabilities

Here we will prove the existence and essential uniqueness of regular conditional Markov kernels for standard, analytic and universal measurable spaces.
For classical results about disintegrations of probability distributions also see
[
Kle20
,
Rao05
,
Fre15
,
Bog07
]
.
After the first public preprint of this paper it was brought to the author’s attention that similar results have been provided for standard measurable spaces in
[
Kal17
]
Thm. 1.25 and analytic measurable spaces in
[
BM20
]
. The disintegration results for universal measurable spaces in Theorem
C.7
and its generalization in Corollary

C.8
still seem to be new.

C.1
Definition of Regular Conditional Markov Kernels

Definition C.1

(Regular conditional Markov kernel)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})
,
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})
,
(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})
be measurable spaces and:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z):\,(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})\dashrightarrow(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}),

be a Markov kernel in two variables, and

\mathbf{K}(Y|Z):\,(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})\dashrightarrow(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}),\quad(B,z)\mapsto\mathbf{K}(X\in\mathcal{X},Y\in B|Z=z),

the marginal Markov kernel.
A
regular (
\sigma\mathcal{S}
-regular,
\bullet
-regular, resp.) conditional Markov kernel
of
\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)
conditioned on
Y
given
Z
is a Markov kernel:

\begin{array}[]{rclccc}\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z)&:&(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z},&\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})&\dashrightarrow&(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}),\\
\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z)&:&(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z},&\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}))&\dashrightarrow&(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}),\\
\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z)&:&(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z},&(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet})&\dashrightarrow&(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}),\\
\end{array}

resp., such that:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)=\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|Z).

C.2
Essential Uniqueness of Regular Conditional Markov Kernels

Lemma C.2

(Essential uniqueness)
.

Consider Markov kernels:

\mathbf{P}(X|Y,Z),\,\mathbf{Q}(X|Y,Z):\,\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X},

and

\mathbf{K}(Y|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}

with any measurable spaces
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
,
\mathcal{Z}
such that:

\mathbf{P}(X|Y,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Y,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|Z).

We then have the following statements.

1.

For every
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
the set:

N_{A}:=\left\{(y,z)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\,|\,\mathbf{P}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)\neq\mathbf{Q}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)\right\}

is a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set with
N_{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
.

2.

If
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
is countably generated then
N:=\bigcup_{A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}}N_{A}
is a

\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set with
N\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
.

3.

If
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
is universally countably generated then

\tilde{N}:=\bigcup_{A\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}}N_{A}
is a

\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set with
\tilde{N}\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
.

Analogous results hold for
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})
-measurable or
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
-measurable
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{Q}
.

Proof.

For
A\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}

we put
N_{A}^{>}:=h^{-1}(D^{>})
, where
D^{>}:=\{(x_{1},x_{2})\,|\,x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R},x_{1}>x_{2}\}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
and
h
is given
as the following composition of maps:

\textstyle{h:\;\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{P}\times\mathbf{Q}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{A}\times j_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}.}

We define
N_{A}^{<}:=h^{-1}(D^{<})
similarly.
Then
N_{A}=N_{A}^{>}\cup N_{A}^{<}
.

1.) If
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
then the evaluation map
j_{A}
is measurable by definition of the
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
.
So
N_{A}^{>},N_{A}^{<}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
.
By assumption we have:

\mathbf{P}(X|Y,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Y,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|Z).

Evaluating boths sides on
A\times N_{A}^{>}
gives thus the same value. So their difference equals
0
:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int\mathbbm{1}_{N_{A}^{>}}(y,z)\cdot\left(\mathbf{P}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)-\mathbf{Q}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)\right)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z),

where the integrand is:

\mathbbm{1}_{N_{A}^{>}}(y,z)\cdot\left(\mathbf{P}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)-\mathbf{Q}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)\right)\geq 0.

This implies that:
N_{A}^{>}
must be a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
.
By symmetry we get that also
N_{A}^{<}
is a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}

and thus
N_{A}=N_{A}^{<}\cup N_{A}^{>}
is
a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
as well.

2.) If now
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
is countably generated then
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\sigma\left(\mathcal{A}\right)

with a countable set
\mathcal{A}
that is closed under finite intersections.
One then puts
M:=\bigcup_{A\in\mathcal{A}}N_{A}
, which is, as countable union of measurable
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null sets,
a measurable
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set.
Then one can define:

\mathcal{D}:=\{A\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}\,|\,\forall(y,z)\in M^{\mathsf{c}}:\,\mathbf{P}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)=\mathbf{Q}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)\}.

One easily sees that
\mathcal{D}
is closed under complements, countable disjoint unions and contains
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{D}
.
This shows that
\mathcal{D}
is a Dynkin system (aka
\lambda
-system).
Furthermore, we have:
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
and that
\mathcal{A}
is closed under finite intersections.
By Dynkin’s lemma we get that:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\sigma(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{D}.

If now
(y,z)\in N=\bigcup_{A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}}N_{A}
then there is an
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
such that:

\mathbf{P}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)\neq\mathbf{Q}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z).

This implies
(y,z)\in M
since
A\in\mathcal{D}
(otherwise we had equality above). Since this holds for all
(y,z)
we get:

N=\bigcup_{A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}}N_{A}\subseteq M=\bigcup_{A\in\mathcal{A}}N_{A}\subseteq N,

thus equality. This shows that
N=M
is a measurable
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set.

3.) If now
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
is universally countably generated then
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{A})_{\bullet}

with a countable set
\mathcal{A}\subseteq(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
that is closed under finite intersections.
For
A\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
the map
j_{A}
is universally measurable by Theorem
B.42
.
This implies that
N_{A}\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
and

M:=\bigcup_{A\in\mathcal{A}}N_{A}\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
are
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null sets.
By the same arguments as above we
get
\sigma(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{D}
. This shows that for
(y,z)\in M^{\mathsf{c}}
we have that:

\forall A\in\sigma(\mathcal{A}):\quad\mathbf{P}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)=\mathbf{Q}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z).

Since every probability measure extends uniquely to the universal completion we even get
for those points
(y,z)\in M^{\mathsf{c}}
:

\forall A\in(\mathcal{A})_{\bullet}:\quad\mathbf{P}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)=\mathbf{Q}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z).

This then shows that even:
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{A})_{\bullet}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
.
As before we get that:

\tilde{N}=\bigcup_{A\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}}N_{A}=M\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet},

and thus that
\tilde{N}
a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set in
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
.
∎

C.3
Existence of Regular Conditional Markov Kernels

Remark C.3

(Existence of conditional Markov kernels)
.

If
\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)
is a Markov kernel then we want
\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z)
such that:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)=\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)

holds. The heuristic here is to find something like a Radon-Nikodym derivative:

\mathbf{K}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)=\frac{\mathbf{K}(X\in A,Y\in dy|Z=z)}{\phantom{X\in A,\,}\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z)}(y),

in a way that it is still a probability measure in
X
and jointly measurable in
(y,z)
.

To achieve measurability from the start we could restrict to
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}
(or
\bar{\mathbb{R}}
or
[0,1]
, etc.) and
make use of Besicovitch derivation theorem, see
[
Fre15
]
472D.
For
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-almost-all
(y,z)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}
and all
x\in\mathcal{X}
we could construct:

\mathbf{K}(X\leq x|Y=y,Z=z)=\inf_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\liminf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\mathbf{K}(X\leq\lceil x\rceil_{m},Y\in B_{1/n}(y)|Z=z)}{\phantom{X\leq\lceil x\rceil_{m},\,}\mathbf{K}(Y\in B_{1/n}(y)|Z=z)}.

An alternative, which we will follow below, is to use Doob’s derivation theorem for
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}
(or
\bar{\mathbb{R}}
or
[0,1]
, etc.)
and countably generated
\mathcal{Y}
,
see
[
DM83
]
Thm. 58,
[
Kle20
]
Ex. 11.17, or Theorem
C.4
below.
This would yield that
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-almost-all
(y,z)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}
and all
x\in\mathcal{X}
:

\mathbf{K}(X\leq x|Y=y,Z=z)=

\inf_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\liminf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\in\mathcal{E}_{n}\\
\mathbf{K}(Y\in B|Z=z)>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbf{K}(X\leq\lceil x\rceil_{m},Y\in B|Z=z)}{\phantom{X\leq\lceil x\rceil_{m},\,}\mathbf{K}(Y\in B|Z=z)}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)\right),

where
x<\lceil x\rceil_{m}:=\frac{\lfloor mx+1\rfloor}{m}\leq x+\frac{1}{m}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
and

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\sigma((B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})
and:

\mathcal{E}_{0}:=\{\mathcal{Y}\},\qquad\quad\mathcal{E}_{n}:=\{D\setminus B_{n},D\cap B_{n}\,|\,D\in\mathcal{E}_{n-1}\}\setminus\{\emptyset\},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

In both approaches,
on the remaining points
(y,z)
, which lie inside the
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set,
we can take a somewhat arbitrary choice, e.g. we can put:

\mathbf{K}(X\leq x|Y=y,Z=z):=\mathbf{K}(X\leq x|Z=z).

Theorem C.4

(Doob’s derivation theorem, see
[
DM83
]
Thm. 58,
[
Kle20
]
Ex. 11.17)
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
be measurable spaces with
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
countably generated
7
7

7

The assumption of being countably generated is
necessary according to
[
DM83
]
Thm. 58 referring to
[
YM76
]
Thm. 3.
. Consider two non-negative finite transition measures:

\mathbf{Q}(Y|Z),\,\mathbf{P}(Y|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathcal{Y}),

where
\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathcal{Y})
is the set of non-negative finite measures on
\mathcal{Y}
.
Furthermore, we assume for every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
that
\mathbf{Q}(Y|Z=z)
is absolute continuous w.r.t.
\mathbf{P}(Y|Z=z)
.
Then there exists a (jointly) measurable function:

g:\,\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},

such that
g
is the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
\mathbf{Q}(Y|Z)
w.r.t.
\mathbf{P}(Y|Z)
(for all
Z=z
simultaneously):

g(y,z)=\frac{\mathbf{Q}(Y\in dy|Z=z)}{\mathbf{P}(Y\in dy|Z=z)}(y).

The latter means that for all
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
we have:

\mathbf{Q}(Y\in B|Z=z)=\int_{B}g(y,z)\,\mathbf{P}(Y\in dy|Z=z).

Proof sketch.

Since
\mathcal{Y}
is countably generated:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}=\sigma((B_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})
. We inductively then put:

\mathcal{E}_{0}:=\{\mathcal{Y}\},\qquad\quad\mathcal{E}_{n}:=\{D\setminus B_{n},D\cap B_{n}\,|\,D\in\mathcal{E}_{n-1}\}\setminus\{\emptyset\},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Then the following sequence of (jointly) measurable functions
g_{n}:\,\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
defined by:

g_{n}(y,z):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\in\mathcal{E}_{n}\\
\mathbf{P}(Y\in B|Z=z)>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbf{Q}(Y\in B|Z=z)}{\mathbf{P}(Y\in B|Z=z)}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y),

is a uniformly integrable martingale w.r.t.
\left(\sigma(\mathcal{E}_{n})\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for
\mathbf{P}(Y|Z=z)
for every fixed
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.
The proof requires the absolute continuity of
\mathbf{Q}(Y|Z)
w.r.t.
\mathbf{P}(Y|Z)
.
So the limit:

g:=\lim_{n\in\mathbb{N}}g_{n}

exists in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

\mathbf{P}(Y|Z)
-almost-surely and the convergence is also in
L^{1}

by the martingale convergence theorem, see
[
Kle20
]
Thm. 11.7.
As a countable limit of jointly measurable functions,
g
is jointly measurable.
The
L^{1}
-convergence and martingale property then implies that
g
is the wanted Radon-Nikodym derivative. ∎

Proposition C.5

(Existence of regular conditional Markov kernels for the unit interval)
.

Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z}
be measurable spaces where
\mathcal{X}=[0,1]
and
\mathcal{Y}
countably generated.
Let

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},

be a Markov kernel in two variables.
Then a (regular) conditional Markov kernel
conditioned on
Y
given
Z
:

\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z):\,\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X},

exists.

Proof.

For fixed
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
and all
z\in Z

we have a finite measure
\mathbf{K}(X\in A,Y|Z=z)
in
Y
, which is
absolute continuous w.r.t. the marginal
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z=z)
.
Since also
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is countably generated, by Doob’s derivation theorem, see Theorem
C.4
,
we get a (jointly) measurable map:

g_{A}:\,\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},

such that for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
:

\mathbf{K}(X\in A,Y\in B|Z=z)=\int_{B}g_{A}(y,z)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z).

For
x\in\mathcal{X}
we will define:

G(x|y,z):=g_{[0,x]}(y,z).

As a next step we want to modify
G(x|y,z)
such that it becomes a cumulative distribution function in
x
, i.e. it corresponds to a probability distribution on
\mathcal{X}
.
For this define
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}:=\mathcal{X}\cap\mathbb{Q}
, which is countable and dense in
\mathcal{X}
.
First note that:

S:=\left\{(y,z)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\,|\,G(1|y,z)\neq 1\right\}

is a measurable
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set.
Then, for every pair
x_{1}<x_{2}
in
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}
consider:

E_{(x_{1},x_{2})}:=\{(y,z)\,|\,G(x_{1}|y,z)>G(x_{2}|y,z)\}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}.

Since we have the equations:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&&\int\mathbbm{1}_{E_{(x_{1},x_{2}),z}}(y)\cdot G(x_{1}|y,z)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z)\\
&=&\mathbf{K}(X\leq x_{1},Y\in E_{(x_{1},x_{2}),z}|Z=z)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x_{1}<x_{2}}}{{\leq}}&\mathbf{K}(X\leq x_{2},Y\in E_{(x_{1},x_{2}),z}|Z=z)\\
&=&\int\mathbbm{1}_{E_{(x_{1},x_{2}),z}}(y)\cdot G(x_{2}|y,z)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle G(x_{2}|y,z)<G(x_{1}|y,z)}}{{\leq}}&\int\mathbbm{1}_{E_{(x_{1},x_{2}),z}}(y)\cdot G(x_{1}|y,z)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z)\end{array}

we necessarily have
\mathbf{K}(Y\in E_{(x_{1},x_{2}),z}|Z=z)=0
for every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.

Then
E:=S\cup\bigcup_{x_{1}<x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}}E_{(x_{1},x_{2})}
is also a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
.

Now for
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}
we can define:

D_{x}:=\{(y,z)\,|\,G(x|y,z)<\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}G(x+1/n|y,z)\}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}.

By the dominated convergence theorem (see
[
Kle20
]
Cor. 6.26) we get:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&&\int\mathbbm{1}_{D_{x,z}}(y)\cdot G(x|y,z)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle D_{x}}}{{\leq}}&\int\mathbbm{1}_{D_{x,z}}(y)\cdot\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}G(x+\frac{1}{n}|y,z)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z)\\
&=&\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int\mathbbm{1}_{D_{x,z}}(y)\cdot G(x+\frac{1}{n}|y,z)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z)\\
&=&\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{K}(X\leq x+\frac{1}{n},Y\in D_{x,z}|Z=z)\\
&=&\mathbf{K}(X\leq x,Y\in D_{x,z}|Z=z)\\
&=&\int\mathbbm{1}_{D_{x,z}}(y)\cdot G(x|y,z)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z).\end{array}

This shows that
\mathbf{K}(Y\in D_{x,z}|Z=z)=0
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
.
So
D:=E\cup\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}}D_{x}
is again a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
.

So far, we got that
G
, when restricted to
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}\times D^{\mathsf{c}}
,
is jointly measurable in
(y,z)
for fixed
x
and monotone non-decreasing and continuous from above in
x
for fixed
(y,z)
with
G(1|y,z)=1
.
We now aim to extend
G
to
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}
.

For
x\in\mathcal{X}=[0,1]
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
put
\lceil x\rceil_{m}:=\min(1,\lfloor mx+1\rfloor/m)
.
Then
\lceil x\rceil_{m}\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}=\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
The map
x\mapsto\lceil x\rceil_{m}
is measurable and for
x\in[0,1)
we have:

x<\lceil x\rceil_{m}\leq x+\frac{1}{m}.

So
\lceil 1\rceil_{m}=1
and
\lceil x\rceil_{m}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}
converges to
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
x\neq 1
, from above for
m\to\infty
.

We then define for all
(x,y,z)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}
:

F(x|y,z):=\inf_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\left\{G(\lceil x\rceil_{m}|y,z)\right\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{D^{\mathsf{c}}}(y,z)+\mathbf{K}(X\leq x|Z=z)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{D}(y,z).

It is clear that
F
is again jointly measurable in
(y,z)
for fixed
x
and agrees with
G
on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}\times D^{\mathsf{c}}
by construction.
As a monotone approximation from above it is clearly continuous from above, monotone non-decreasing and satifies
F(1|y,z)=1
for all
(y,z)
.
So for fixed
(y,z)
now
F(\cdot|y,z)
corresponds to a probability distribution
\mathbf{K}(X|Y=y,Z=z)
on
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
, uniquely given by the defining relations on sets
[0,x]
:

F(x|y,z)=:\mathbf{K}(X\leq x|Y=y,Z=z),

for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

Now define
\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
as the set of all
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
that satisfy:

1.

the map
(y,z)\mapsto\mathbf{K}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)
is
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})
-
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}}
-measurable, and:

2.

for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
the following equation holds:

\mathbf{K}(X\in A,Y\in B|Z=z)=\int\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)\cdot\mathbf{K}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z).

Since
\mathbf{K}(X,Y\in B|Z=z)
and
\mathbf{K}(X|Y=y,Z=z)
are probability measures in
X
the system
\mathcal{D}
is closed under countable disjoint unions and
complements and contains
\mathcal{X}=[0,1]
. So
\mathcal{D}
is a Dynkin system.
We already know that for
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}
the map
(y,z)\mapsto\mathbf{K}(X\leq x|Y=y,Z=z)=F(x|y,z)
is measurable.
Since for
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and every
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
,
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, we have:

\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)\cdot\mathbf{K}(X\leq x|Y=y,Z=z)=\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)\cdot G(x|y,z)

up to the
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z=z)
-null set
D_{z}
we already get for those
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}
:

\mathbf{K}(X\leq x,Y\in B|Z=z)=\int\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)\cdot\mathbf{K}(X\leq x|Y=y,Z=z)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z).

This shows that
\mathcal{E}:=\{[0,x]\,|\,x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}\}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
. Since
\mathcal{E}
is closed under finite intersections
Dynkin’s lemma (see
[
Kle20
]
Thm. 1.19) implies:

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}=\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subseteq\mathcal{D}.

This shows that the two conditions hold for all
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
and thus that
\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z)
is the desired regular conditional Markov kernel.
∎

Theorem C.6

(Existence of regular conditional Markov kernels)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a standard measurable space, an analytic measurable space, a countably perfect measurable space, resp., and

\mathcal{Y}
be a countably generated measurable space and
\mathcal{Z}
be any measurable space.
Let

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},

be a Markov kernel in two variables.
Then there exists a regular,
\sigma\mathcal{S}
-regular,
\bullet
-regular, resp., conditional Markov kernel
\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z)
conditioned on
Y
given
Z
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{X}
is standard, analytic, countably perfect, resp., we find an injective measurable map:

\varphi:\,(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\hookrightarrow([0,1],\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})=:(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})

that induces a measurable isomorphism
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})\cong(\varphi(\mathcal{X}),\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}|\varphi(\mathcal{X})})

with
\varphi(\mathcal{X})\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
(
\varphi(\mathcal{X})\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})
,
\varphi(\mathcal{X})\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})_{\bullet}
,
resp.).
So we can consider the push-forward Markov kernel
\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime},Y|Z):=\mathbf{K}(\varphi(X),Y|Z)
:

\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime},Y|Z):\,(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)}}{{\dashrightarrow}}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi\times\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{Y}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}).

Since
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=[0,1]
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is countably generated we can apply Prp.
C.5
and we then get the regular conditional Markov kernel
\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime}|Y,Z)
:

\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime}|Y,Z):\,(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})\dashrightarrow(\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}).

If we put
A^{\prime}:=\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\setminus\varphi(\mathcal{X})
we have
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
(
A^{\prime}\in\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})
,

A^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})_{\bullet}
, resp.).
So
\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}|Y=y,Z=z)
is well-defined for every
(y,z)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}
.
Consider the set:

D:=\{(y,z)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\,|\,\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}|Y=y,Z=z)>0\}.

We first show that
D\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
(
D\in\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})
,

D\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
, resp.).
We now consider the Markov kernel
\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime}|Y,Z)
as the measurable map:

\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime}|Y,Z):\,(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})\longrightarrow(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})}).

Now consider the map:

j_{A^{\prime}}:\,\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})\to[0,1],\quad\mu\mapsto\mu(A^{\prime}).

By Theorems
B.42
and
B.41

the map
j_{A^{\prime}}
is measurable w.r.t.
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})}
(w.r.t.
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})})
, w.r.t.
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})})_{\bullet}
, resp.).
Then the composition:

H:\,(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime}|Y,Z)}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}),\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{A^{\prime}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}([0,1],\mathcal{B}_{[0,1]})

is measurable w.r.t.
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
(w.r.t.
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})
, w.r.t.
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
, resp.).
It follows that
D=H^{-1}((0,1])\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
(
D\in\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})
,

D\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
, resp.).
Since
y\mapsto(y,z)
is
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})
-measurable it follows that
D_{z}\in(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
.
So we can evaluate
\mathbf{K}(Y\in D_{z}|Z=z)=\mathbf{K}((Y,Z)\in D|Z=z)
for every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and the map:

\mathcal{Z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|Z)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}[0,1],\quad z\mapsto\mathbf{K}(Y\in D_{z}|Z=z),

is measurable w.r.t.
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
(w.r.t.
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})
, w.r.t.
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
, resp.) again
by Theorems
B.42
and
B.41
.
So we can integrate:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}0&=&\mathbf{K}(\varphi(X)\in A^{\prime},Y\in D_{z}|Z=z)\\
&=&\int\mathbbm{1}_{D_{z}}(y)\cdot\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}|Y=y,Z=z)\,\mathbf{K}(dy|Z=z).\end{array}

It follows that for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
we have:
\mathbf{K}(Y\in D_{z}|Z=z)=0
.

For
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
let
\tilde{A}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
such that
A=\varphi^{-1}(\tilde{A})
.
Since
\varphi
is injective we have
\varphi(A)=\tilde{A}\cap f(\mathcal{X})\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
(in
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})
, in
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}})_{\bullet}
, resp.).
So we can define:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}&&\mathbf{K}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)\\
&:=&\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime}\in\varphi(A)|Y=y,Z=z)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{D^{c}}(y,z)+\mathbf{K}_{0}(X\in A)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{D}(y,z),\end{array}

with any probability distribution
\mathbf{K}_{0}
.
So we get the Markov kernel:

\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z):\,(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})\dashrightarrow(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}),

(with
\sigma\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})
-measurability,
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
-measurability, resp.) again by Theorems
B.42
and
B.41
.
Furthermore, we have that for all
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
,
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and
z\in\mathcal{Z}
:

\mathbf{K}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)=\int\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)\cdot\mathbf{K}(X\in A|Y=y,Z=z)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z).

This shows the claim.
∎

The following two results now directly generalize the results from
[
Fad85
]
Thm. 6 from probability distributions to Markov kernels.

Theorem C.7

(Existence of
\bullet
-regular conditional Markov kernels)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a universal measurable space,
\mathcal{Y}
be a universally countably generated measurable space and
\mathcal{Z}
be any measurable space.
Let

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},

be a Markov kernel in two variables.
Then there exists a
\bullet
-regular conditional Markov kernel
\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z)
conditioned on
Y
given
Z
.

Proof.

By assumption we have that:
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}})_{\bullet}
with a countably generated
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}}
.
We put
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{Y}})
.
We also have that:
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
with a countably generated and countably separated
\sigma
-algebra

\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}}
such that
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}})
is countably perfect.
Then the Markov kernel:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},

induces the Markov kernel on the universal completions:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}_{\bullet}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\bullet}\times\mathcal{Y}_{\bullet},

and then restricts to:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}_{\bullet}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}.

By Theorem
C.6
we then have a
\bullet
-regular conditional Markov kernel:

\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z):\,(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{Z}_{\bullet})_{\bullet}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}^{\prime},

such that:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)=\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|Z).

Note that:
(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{Z}_{\bullet})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z})_{\bullet}
.
Since
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
-
\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}}
-measurability is equivalent to

(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
-
(\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
-measurability and

(\mathcal{B}_{|\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
, we get the wanted
\bullet
-regular conditional Markov kernel:

\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z):\,(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z})_{\bullet}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}.

∎

Corollary C.8

(Existence of
\bullet
-regular conditional Markov kernels)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be universally countably generated measurable spaces, where
\mathcal{X}
is perfect,
and
\mathcal{Z}
be any measurable space.
Let

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},

be a Markov kernel in two variables.
Then there exists a
\bullet
-regular conditional Markov kernel
\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z)
conditioned on
Y
given
Z
.

Proof.

By assumption we have
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}
with countable
\mathcal{E}
.
Define the equivalence relation:
x_{1}\sim x_{2}
iff
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
can’t be separated by
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}
.
Then
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}:=\mathcal{X}/\!\!\sim
is countably generated with

(\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\mathcal{X}}})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}})_{\bullet}=(\mathcal{E})_{\bullet}
, countably separated (since
\mathcal{E}
separated the points, see Lemma
B.19
) and perfect, thus a universal measurable space. Furthermore, we can identify:

\mathcal{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet}\times\mathcal{Y})=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\bullet}\times\mathcal{Y})

and
\mathcal{P}(\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\bullet})=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\bullet})
.
We can then apply Theorem
C.7
to get the result for
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
and thus for
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

The above results inspire for a new definition:

Definition C.9

(Universal disintegration space)
.

A measurable space
\mathcal{X}
is called
universal disintegration space
if for every
universally countably generated measurable space
\mathcal{Y}
, every measurable space
\mathcal{Z}
and every Markov kernel

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},

there exists a
\bullet
-regular conditional Markov kernel conditioned on
Y
given
Z
:

\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z):\,(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z})_{\bullet}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}.

Remark C.10
.

All the results above show that every standard, analytic, countably perfect and every universal measurable space or even every perfect universally countably generated measure space
is a universal disintegration space.

Lemma C.11
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
,
\mathcal{Z}
be measurable spaces and:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},

a Markov kernel in two variables.
Assume one of the following:

1.

X\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}(Y,Z)
.

2.

Y\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}Z
.

then there exists a regular conditional Markov kernel conditioned on
Y
given
Z
.

Proof.

1.) If
X\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}(Y,Z)
then there exists a measurable map
\varphi:\,\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{X}
such that:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|Z).

So
\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z):={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y,Z)
is a regular conditional Markov kernel.

2.) If
Y\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}Z
then there exists a measurable map
\varphi:\,\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Y}
such that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(Y|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(X|Z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}(X|Z)\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(Y|Z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|Z).

So
\mathbf{K}(X|Y,Z):=\mathbf{K}(X|Z)
is a regular conditional Markov kernel.
∎

Remark C.12
.

To get further existence results for
\bullet
-regular conditional Markov kernels
one could use the following slightly weaker conditions:

1.

X\precsim_{\mathbf{K},\bullet}(Y,Z)
, i.e. if there exists a universally
measurable map
\varphi:\,\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{X}
such that:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|Z).

2.

Y\precsim_{\mathbf{K},\bullet}Z
, i.e. if there exists a universally
measurable map
\varphi:\,\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Y}
such that:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(Y|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(X|Z).

The proof would follow the same lines as in Lemma
C.11
.

C.4
Consistency of Regular Conditional Markov Kernels

We now show that regular conditional Markov kernels are conistent when coming from a measurable map.
We follow similar arguments as used in
[
Fre15
]
452E, 452G, adapted to our case.

Theorem C.13

(Regular conditional Markov kernels are consistent with maps)
.

Let
\mathbf{K}(X|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}
be a Markov kernel and

f:\,\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a measurable map,
Y:=f(X,Z)
and

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)
the joint (push-forward) Markov kernel.
Assume that we have a regular conditional Markov kernel
\mathbf{Q}(X|Y,Z)
, which then satisfies:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|Z)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Y,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|Z).

1.

Consistency: For every
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
we have:

\mathbf{Q}(X\in f^{-1}_{z}(B)|Y=y,Z=z)=\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)

for all
(y,z)
outside a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
(dependent on
B
).

2.

Strong consistency: If
\mathcal{Y}
is (universally) countably separated, then we have:

\mathbf{Q}(X\in f^{-1}_{z}(y)|Y=y,Z=z)=1,

for all
(y,z)
outside a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
, in
(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}})_{\bullet}
, resp.

Proof.

1.) Fix
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and put:
