N^{>}_{B}:=\left\{(y,z)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\,|\,\mathbf{Q}(X\in f^{-1}_{z}(B)|Y=y,Z=z)>\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)\right\}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}.

Similarly we define
N^{<}_{B}
. We then get:

\displaystyle\int_{N^{>}_{B,z}}\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}(Y\in B\cap N^{>}_{B,z}|Z=z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}(X\in f^{-1}_{z}(B),Y\in N^{>}_{B,z}|Z=z)

\displaystyle=\int_{N^{>}_{B,z}}\mathbf{Q}(X\in f^{-1}(B)|Y=y,Z=z)\,\mathbf{K}(Y\in dy|Z=z).

This shows that
N^{>}_{B,z}
is a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set. Similarly we get that
N^{<}_{B,z}
is a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set. This shows the first claim.

2.) Since
\mathcal{Y}
is countably separated there exists a countable algebra
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
separating the points of
\mathcal{Y}
.
For
E\in\mathcal{E}
we define:

N_{E}:=\left\{(y,z)\in E\times\mathcal{Z}\,|,\mathbf{Q}(X\in f^{-1}_{z}(E)|Y=y,Z=z)\neq 1\right\}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}.

By the first point we know that
N_{E}
is a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set.
Since
\mathcal{E}
is countable then also
N:=\bigcup_{E\in\mathcal{E}}N_{E}
is a
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set in

\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
.

Since
\mathcal{E}
separates points and is closed under complements we get for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
:

\{y\}=\bigcap_{y\in E\in\mathcal{E}}E,

and thus for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
:

f^{-1}_{z}(y)^{\mathsf{c}}=\bigcup_{y\in E\in\mathcal{E}}f^{-1}_{z}(E)^{\mathsf{c}}.

Then for
(y,z)\in N^{\mathsf{c}}
we get:

0\leq\mathbf{Q}(X\in f^{-1}_{z}(y)^{\mathsf{c}}|Y=y,Z=z)\leq\sum_{y\in E\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbf{Q}(X\in f^{-1}_{z}(E)^{\mathsf{c}}|Y=y,Z=z)=0.

This shows that for
(y,z)\in N^{\mathsf{c}}
we have:

\mathbf{Q}(X\in f^{-1}_{z}(y)|Y=y,Z=z)=1.

This shows the second claim.
∎

Remark C.14
.

For
\sigma\mathcal{S}
-regular or
\bullet
-regular conditional Markov kernels in Theorem
C.13

similar statements hold, but with the null sets lying in

\sigma\mathcal{S}\left(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}\right)
, in
\left(\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}\right)_{\bullet}
, resp.
For the
\bullet
-regular conditional Markov kernels in Theorem
C.13
one only needs universally measurable
f

and for the second claim it is enough to ask for universally countably separated
\mathcal{Y}
.

Remark C.15

(Proper regular conditional Markov kernels)
.

Consider the setting in Theorem
C.13
.
One then could, in analogy to probability measures, call a conditional Markov kernel
\mathbf{Q}(X|Y,Z)
a
proper regular conditional Markov kernel

if the strong consistency property holds for all
(y,z)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}
with
f^{-1}_{z}(y)\neq\emptyset

and not just up to some
\mathbf{K}(Y|Z)
-null set

N\subseteq\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}
.
To go from a strongly consistent to a proper regular conditional Markov kernel
one could try to find a
conditional measurable selection map

g:\,D\to\mathcal{X}
defined on the subset
D:=f(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Z})\cap N
of
\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}

such that for all
(y,z)\in D
:

f(g(y,z),z)=y.

Then we would have:
g(y,z)\in\{x\in\mathcal{X}\,|\,f(x,z)=y\}=f^{-1}_{z}(y)
and thus:

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{g}(X\in f^{-1}_{z}(y)|Y=y,Z=z)=1.

for such
(y,z)\in D
.
Then one could define:

\mathbf{K}(X|Y=y,Z=y):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\mathbf{Q}(X|Y=y,Z=z)&\text{ for }&(y,z)\notin D,\\
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{g}(X|Y=y,Z=z)&\text{ for }&(y,z)\in D.\end{array}\right.

Then clearly we would have:

\mathbf{K}(X\in f^{-1}_{z}(y)|Y=y,Z=z)=1,

for all
(y,z)
with
f^{-1}_{z}(y)\neq\emptyset
.
Finding such a
g
or such a disintegration, already for probability measures, is not always possible, not even for standard measurable spaces,
without weakening the measurability properties, see measurable selection theorems in
[
Bog07
,
Fre15
,
BD75
]
and the discussions there.
In the unconditional case, for probability measures on analytic spaces, one can at least find a
\sigma\mathcal{S}
-measurable right inverse
g
to
f
.
It is unclear to the author if such results would generalize to the needed conditional versions for analytic spaces or if there are
universally measurable selection theorems (in the unconditional case for universal measurable spaces).

Appendix D
Proofs - Join-Semi-Lattice Rules for Transitional Random Variables

In this section we will collect properties of the relation
\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}
introduced in the main paper in Notations
2.19
.
For this let
\left(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T},\mathbf{K}(W|T)\right)
be a transition probability space and
\mathbf{X}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}
and

\mathbf{Y}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathbf{Z}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Z}

and
\mathbf{U}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{U}

be transitional random variables, i.e. Markov kernels. We put:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z,U|T):=\left(\mathbf{X}(X|W,T)\otimes\mathbf{Y}(Y|W,T)\otimes\mathbf{Z}(Z|W,T)\otimes\mathbf{U}(U|W,T)\right)\circ\mathbf{K}(W|T).

The relation
\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}
will be a main ingredient to show that
transitional conditional independence
, see Definition

3.1
, forms a
T
-
\ast
-separoid
, see Definition
A.4
and Appendix

E
.
According to Remark
A.1
in Appendix
A
we also need to check the compatibility of
\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}
with the equivalence relation,
\cong
, of isomorphisms of measurable spaces. This will be done in Appendix
E
in Lemma
E.2
.

Remark D.1
.

1.

In general we do
not
have:
\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}
for arbitrary Markov kernels.

2.

In general we do
not
have anti-symmetry, i.e. that:

\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\implies\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{Y}.

Notation D.2
.

Recall that we write:

1.

\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}
if there exists a measurable map
\varphi:\,\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
such that:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|T).

We further define:

2.

\mathbf{X}\approx_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\;:\iff\;\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}.

The next Lemma
D.3
is crucial for most of following results where
\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}
is involved.
Note that a similar result for
\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}^{\ast}
would not hold, i.e. where
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(Y|Z,T)
would be replaced by an arbitrary Markov kernel
\mathbf{Y}(Y|Z,T)
.

Lemma D.3
.

Consider a Markov kernel
\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)
such that the marginal factorizes:

\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(Y|Z,T)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T),

with a measurable map
\varphi:\,\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{Y}
.
Then the joint Markov kernel also factorizes:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(Y|Z,T)\otimes\mathbf{K}(X,Z|T).

Proof.

By assumption the set:

M:=\left\{(y,z,t)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{T}\,|\,y\neq\varphi(z,t)\right\}

is a
\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)
-null set. Indeed:

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}((Y,Z)\in M_{t}|T=t)

\displaystyle=\left({\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(Y|Z,T=t)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T=t)\right)(M_{t})

\displaystyle=\int{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(Y\in M_{z,t}|Z=z,T=t)\,\mathbf{K}(Z\in dz|T=t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathbbm{1}_{M_{z,t}}(\varphi(z,t))\,\mathbf{K}(Z\in dz|T=t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathbbm{1}_{\{y|y\neq\varphi(z,t)\}}(\varphi(z,t))\,\mathbf{K}(Z\in dz|T=t)

\displaystyle=\int\mathbbm{1}_{\varphi(z,t)\neq\varphi(z,t)}\,\mathbf{K}(Z\in dz|T=t)

\displaystyle=0.

Then
N:=\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times M
is a
\mathbf{K}(X,\tilde{Y},Y,Z|T)
-null set, where
\tilde{Y}=\varphi(Z,T)
.
This implies:

\displaystyle\quad\;\left({\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(Y|Z,T)\otimes\mathbf{K}(X,Z|T)\right)(A\times B\times C,t)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}(X\in A,\varphi(Z,T)\in B,Z\in C|T=t)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}(X\in A,\varphi(Z,T)\in B,Y\in\mathcal{Y},Z\in C|T=t)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N}}{{=}}\mathbf{K}(X\in A,\varphi(Z,T)\in\mathcal{Y},Y\in B,Z\in C|T=t)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}(X\in A,Y\in B,Z\in C|T=t).

This shows the claim.
∎

Lemma D.4

(Product extension)
.

We have the implication:

\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\implies\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

By assumption we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|T)

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{Lemma }\ref{lem:ismapof-null-set}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T).

∎

Lemma D.5

(Bottom element)
.

We always have:

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}.

Proof.

We have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(\ast,X|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\otimes\mathbf{K}(X|T).

∎

Lemma D.6

(Transitivity)
.

We have the implication:

\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

We have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\psi}(Y|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T),

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|T)

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{Lemma }\ref{lem:ismapof-null-set}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y)\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\psi}(Y|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\left({\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y)\circ{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\psi}(Y|Z)\right)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi\circ\psi}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T)

∎

Lemma D.7

(Restricted reflexivity)
.

If
X:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{X}
is a measurable map and
\mathbf{X}={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(X|W,T)
.
Then we have:

\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}.

Proof.

Let
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
be copies of
X
. Then using
\varphi:=\mathrm{id}:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
gives:

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathrm{id}}(X_{1}|X_{2})\otimes\mathbf{K}(X_{2}|T).

∎

Lemma D.8

(Product stays bounded)
.

We have the implication:

\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\quad\land\quad\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\quad\implies\quad\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

By the assumptions we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\psi}(Y|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T),

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Z|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T)

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{Lemma }\ref{lem:ismapof-null-set}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Z)\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\psi}(Y|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi\times\psi}(X,Y|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T).

∎

Lemma D.9

(Product compatibility)
.

We have the implication:

\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\quad\land\quad\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{U}\quad\implies\quad\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{U}.

Proof.

\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}
implies
\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{U}
by Lemma
D.4
.
Similarly,
\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{U}
implies
\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{U}
. By Lemma
D.8
we then get the claim:

\quad\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{U}.

∎

Theorem D.10

(The bounded join-semi-lattice of transitional random variables)
.

Let
\left(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T},\mathbf{K}(W|T)\right)
be a transition probability space and
X:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{X}
and

Y:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{Y}
and
Z:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{Z}

be transitional random variables.
We put:
\mathbf{X}:={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(X|W,T)
and
\mathbf{Y}:={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(Y|W,T)
and
\mathbf{Z}:={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(Z|W,T)
and:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T):=\left(\mathbf{X}(X|W,T)\otimes\mathbf{Y}(Y|W,T)\otimes\mathbf{Z}(Z|W,T)\right)\circ\mathbf{K}(W|T).

We then have:

1.

Reflexivity:
\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}
.

2.

Transitivity:
\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}
.

3.

Almost-sure anti-symmetry (per definition):

\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\implies:\mathbf{X}\approx_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}.

4.

Closed under join:
\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Y}={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(X,Y|W,T)
.

5.

Join is upper bound:
\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Y}
.

6.

Join is smallest upper bound:

\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\quad\land\quad\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\quad\implies\quad\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}.

7.

Bottom element:
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}
.

8.

Bottom element is neutral:
\mathbf{X}\approx_{\mathbf{K}}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\otimes\mathbf{X}
.

9.

Idempotent:
\mathbf{X}\approx_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{X}
.

So the class of transitional random variables
of the form
\mathbf{X}={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(X|W,T)
,
\mathbf{Y}={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(Y|W,T)
,
\mathbf{Z}={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(Z|W,T)
, etc.,
for some measurable maps
X
,
Y
,
Z
, etc., on the transition probability space

(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T},\mathbf{K}(W|T))
together with the relation
\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}
, join
\otimes
and bottom element
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}
forms
a
bounded join-semi-lattice
modulo almost-sure anti-symmetry
\approx_{\mathbf{K}}
(and up to the fact that such a class might not be a set).

Proof.

Reflexivity: See Lemma
D.7
.

Transitivity: See Lemma
D.6
.

Almost-sure anti-symmetry: This holds per definition. The next point is just a straight forward reformulation.

Join is upper bound: This follows from reflexivity, Lemma
D.7
,
and Lemma
D.4
.

Join is smallest upper bound: See Lemma
D.8
.

Bottom element: See Lemma
D.5
.

Bottom element is neural:
\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\otimes\mathbf{X}
is clear. For the other direction,

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\otimes\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}
, note:

\mathbf{K}(\ast,X_{1},X_{2}|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast\times\mathrm{id}}(\ast,X_{1}|X_{2})\otimes\mathbf{K}(X_{2}|T).

Idempotent:
\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{X}
is clear. For the other direction,

\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}
, note:

\mathbf{K}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\mathrm{id}\times\mathrm{id}}(X_{1},X_{2}|X_{3})\otimes\mathbf{K}(X_{3}|T).

∎

Appendix E
Proofs - Separoid Rules for Transitional Conditional Independence

In this section we want to prove that the class of
transitional random variables
together with
the equivalence relation,
\cong
, isomorphism of measurable spaces, the relation
\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}
,
the product
\otimes
and the ternary relation
\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}
of
transitional conditional independence
, see
Definition
3.1
,
forms an
\mathbf{T}
-
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}
-separoid
(or in different symbols:
T
-
\ast
-separoid
), see Definition
A.4
in Appendix
A
, at least when restricted to codomains that are standard measurable spaces (or universal measurable spaces, when one replaces measurability with universal measurability everywhere).

For this let
\left(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T},\mathbf{K}(W|T)\right)
be a transition probability space and
\mathbf{X}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}
and

\mathbf{Y}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathbf{Z}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Z}
and

\mathbf{U}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{U}
be Markov kernels.
We denote by
T:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}
the canonical projection map and

\mathbf{T}(T|W,T):={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(T|W,T)
. We also consider the constant map
\ast:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\to\Asterisk:=\{\ast\}
and

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\ast|W,T)
the corresponding Markov kernel.
We put:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z,U|T):=\left(\mathbf{X}(X|W,T)\otimes\mathbf{Y}(Y|W,T)\otimes\mathbf{Z}(Z|W,T)\otimes\mathbf{U}(U|W,T)\right)\circ\mathbf{K}(W|T),

or similarly if more or other Markov kernels are involved.

Recall that we say that
\mathbf{X}
is
transitionally independent of
\mathbf{Y}
conditioned on
\mathbf{Z}
w.r.t.
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{K}(W|T)
, in symbols:

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z},

if there exists a Markov kernel
\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)
such that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T),

where
\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)
is the marginal of
\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)
.

Notation E.1
.

Recall that we write:

1.

\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}
if there exists a measurable map
\varphi:\,\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
such that:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|T).

2.

\mathbf{X}\approx_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\;:\iff\;\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}.

We further define:

3.

\mathbf{X}\cong\mathbf{Y}
if there exists a measurable isomorphism
\varphi:\,\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
, i.e. a
bijective measurable map with a measurable inverse, such that:
\varphi_{*}\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{X}
.

According to Remark
A.1
we first need to check that
\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}
,
\otimes
,
\cong
,
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}
,
\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}

are all sufficiently compatible with each other. This will be done in the next Lemma.

Lemma E.2

(Compatibility of
\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}
,
\otimes
,
\cong
,
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}
,
\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}
, see
A.1
)
.

We have the following:

1.

(\mathbf{X}\cong\mathbf{X}^{\prime})\,\land\,(\mathbf{Y}\cong\mathbf{Y}^{\prime})\,\implies\,(\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Y})\cong(\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{Y}^{\prime})
.

Proof. With isomorphisms
\varphi:\,\mathcal{X}\cong\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
and
\psi:\,\mathcal{Y}\cong\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
with
\varphi_{*}\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{X}^{\prime}
and

\psi_{*}\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}
we get:

\quad(\varphi\times\psi)_{*}(\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Y})=(\varphi_{*}\mathbf{X})\otimes(\psi_{*}\mathbf{Y})=\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}.

2.

(\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Y})\cong(\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{X})
.

Proof. Use the isomorphism:
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\cong\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X}
with
(x,y)\mapsto(y,x)
.

3.

(\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Y})\otimes\mathbf{Z}\cong\mathbf{X}\otimes(\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{Z}).

Proof. Use the isomorphism:
\mathrm{id}:\,(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})\times\mathcal{Z}\cong\mathcal{X}\times(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z})
.

4.

(\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y})\,\land\,(\mathbf{X}\cong\mathbf{X}^{\prime})\,\land\,(\mathbf{Y}\cong\mathbf{Y}^{\prime})\,\implies\,(\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}).

Proof. Consider
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}=\xi_{*}\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}=\zeta_{*}\mathbf{Y}
with isomorphisms
\xi
,
\zeta
.

Let
\varphi:\,\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
such that:
\mathbf{K}(X,Y|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|T)
. Then:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}|T)

\displaystyle=\left({\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\xi}(X^{\prime}|X)\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\zeta}(Y^{\prime}|Y)\right)\circ\mathbf{K}(X,Y|T)

\displaystyle=\left({\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\xi}(X^{\prime}|X)\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\zeta}(Y^{\prime}|Y)\right)\circ\left({\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(X|Y)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|T)\right)

\displaystyle=\left({\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\xi\circ\varphi}(X^{\prime}|Y)\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\zeta}(Y^{\prime}|Y)\right)\circ\mathbf{K}(Y|T)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\xi\circ\varphi\circ\zeta^{-1}}(X^{\prime}|Y^{\prime})\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y^{\prime}|T).

5.

(\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y})\,\implies\,(\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}(\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{Z}))
.

Proof. This is proven in Lemmata
D.3
and
D.4
.

6.

\left(\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\right)\,\land\,(\mathbf{X}\cong\mathbf{X}^{\prime})\,\land\,(\mathbf{Y}\cong\mathbf{Y}^{\prime})\,\land(\mathbf{Z}\cong\mathbf{Z}^{\prime})\,\implies\,\left(\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\given\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\right).

Proof. If
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}=\varphi_{*}\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}=\psi_{*}\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}=\xi_{*}\mathbf{Z}
and:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T).

Then we get:

\mathbf{K}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime}|T)=\mathbf{Q}(\varphi(X)|Z=\xi^{-1}(Z^{\prime}))\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime}|T).

7.

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}
.

Proof.
\mathbf{K}(\ast,X|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\otimes\mathbf{K}(X|T).

8.

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\otimes\mathbf{X}\cong\mathbf{X}
.

Proof. Use isomorphism:
\{\ast\}\times\mathcal{X}\cong\mathcal{X}
.

E.1
Core Separoid Rules for Transitional Conditional Independence

Lemma E.3

(
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}
-Extended Left Redundancy)
.

We have for any
\mathbf{Y}
the implication:

\mathbf{U}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathbf{U}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

The assumption implies the existence of a factorization:

\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T).

Lemma
D.3
then shows that this extends to:

\mathbf{K}(U,Y,Z|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\varphi}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T),

which shows the claim.
∎

Lemma E.4

(Left Redundancy)
.

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\qquad\text{always holds.}

Proof.

\mathbf{K}(\ast,Y,Z|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T).

∎

Lemma E.5

(
\mathbf{T}
-Restricted Right Redundancy)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be standard and
\mathcal{Z}
be countably generated. Then:

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\given\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{T}.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{X}
is standard and
\{\ast\}\times\mathcal{Z}
is countably generated by
Theorem
C.6
we get the factorization:

\mathbf{K}(X,\ast,Z|T)=\mathbf{K}(X|\ast,Z,T)\otimes\mathbf{K}(\ast,Z|T).

Multiplying both sides with
\mathbf{T}={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(T|T)
gives:

\mathbf{K}(X,\ast,T,Z|T)=\mathbf{K}(X|\ast,Z,T)\otimes\mathbf{K}(\ast,T,Z|T).

This shows the claim.
∎

Lemma E.6

(Left Decomposition)
.

\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{U}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathbf{U}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

By assumption we have the factorization:

\mathbf{K}(X,U,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X,U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T).

Marginalizing out
X
gives:

\mathbf{K}(U,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T).

This shows the claim.
∎

Lemma E.7

(Right Decomposition)
.

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{U}\given\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{U}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

By assumption we have the factorization:

\mathbf{K}(X,U,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T).

Marginalizing out
Y
gives:

\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T).

This shows the claim.
∎

Lemma E.8

(
\mathbf{T}
-Inverted Right Decomposition)
.

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{T}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

By the assumption
\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}
we have a factorization:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T).

Multiplying both sides with
\mathbf{T}={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(T|T)
gives:

\mathbf{K}(X,T,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(T,Y,Z|T).

This shows the claim.
∎

Lemma E.9

(Left Weak Union)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be standard and
\mathcal{U}
be countably generated. Then:

\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{U}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

By assumption we have:

\mathbf{K}(X,U,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X,U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T),

for some Markov kernel
\mathbf{Q}(X,U|Z)
.
If we marginalize out
X
we get:

\mathbf{K}(U,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T).

Because
\mathcal{X}
is standard and
\mathcal{U}
countably generated
we have a factorization:

\mathbf{Q}(X,U|Z)=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{Q}(U|Z),

with the conditional Markov kernel
\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)
(via Theorem
C.6
).

Putting these equations together we get:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X,U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{Q}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Y,Z|T).

This shows the claim.
∎

Remark E.10
.

Left Weak Union
E.9
was formulated to rely on the assumption that
\mathcal{X}
is a standard
and
\mathcal{U}
a countably generated
measurable space. The reason was that we relied on the existence of a regular conditional Markov kernel,
Theorem
C.6
. If we instead use the
Theorem
C.7
with the weaker assumption of
universal measurable
\mathcal{X}
and universally countably generated
\mathcal{U}

we would get a (universally measurable)
\bullet
-regular conditional Markov kernel instead.
So if we replaced measurability for all maps and Markov kernels everywhere with the weaker notion of
universal measurability we could conclude similarly, but with weaker assumptions and for a bigger class of measurable spaces,
so would be much more general.
The price one has to pay is rather small, besides dealing with slightly more technical definitions,
since measurablity always implies universal measurability, and also because
every probability measure can uniquely be extended to measure all universally measurable subsets.

If one does not want to make any assumptions about the underlying measurable spaces one could resort to the following:

Lemma E.11

(Restricted Left Weak Union)
.

\left(\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{U}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\right)\land\left(\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\given\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{Z}\right)\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

By assumption we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X,U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T),

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T),

for some Markov kernels
\mathbf{Q}(X,U|Z)
,
\mathbf{P}(X|U,Z)
.
If we marginalize out
Y
and then
X
in the first equation we get:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X,U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T),

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T).

This together with the second equation gives:

\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)=\mathbf{P}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{Q}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T).

Comparing this to the above equation we get:

\mathbf{Q}(X,U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T)=\mathbf{P}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{Q}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T).

By the essential uniqueness (see Lemma
C.2
) of such factorization we get that for every
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}

and
D\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{U}}
:

\mathbf{Q}(X\in A,U\in D|Z)=\int_{B}\mathbf{P}(X\in A|U=u,Z)\,\mathbf{Q}(U\in du|Z)\quad\mathbf{K}(Z|T)\text{-a.s.}

Then this holds also
\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)
-a.s. Plugging this back into the first equation we get:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{Q}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T).

Marginalizing
X
out gives:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(U,Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T).

Plugging that back in finally gives:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{Q}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Y,Z|T).

This shows the claim.
∎

Lemma E.12

(Right Weak Union)
.

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{U}\given\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

We have the factorization:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,U,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T),

with some Markov kernel
\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)
. If we view
\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)
as a function in
(u,z)
via:

(u,z)\mapsto\mathbf{Q}(X|Z=z),

by just ignoring the argument
u
then the claim follows from the same factorization above.
∎

Lemma E.13

(Left Contraction)
.

(\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{Z})\land(\mathbf{U}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z})\implies\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{U}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

By assumption we have the two factorizations:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T),

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T),

with some Markov kernels
\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)
,
\mathbf{P}(U|Z)
. Putting these equations together using
\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{P}(U|Z)
we get:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,U,Z|T)=\left(\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{P}(U|Z)\right)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T).

This shows the claim.
∎

Lemma E.14

(Right Contraction)
.

(\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{Z})\land(\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{U}\given\mathbf{Z})\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{U}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

By assumption we have the two factorizations:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T),

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T),

with some Markov kernels
\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)
,
\mathbf{P}(X|Z)
.

Marginalizing out
Y
we get the equalities:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T),

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T).

By the essential uniqueness (see Lemma
C.2
) of such factorization we get that for every
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
:

\mathbf{Q}(X\in A|U,Z)=\mathbf{P}(X\in A|Z)\qquad\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T)\text{-a.s.}

The same equation then holds also
\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T)
-a.s. (by ignoring argument
y
).
Plugging that back into the first equation gives:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,U,Z|T)=\mathbf{P}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T).

This shows the claim.
∎

Lemma E.15

(Right Cross Contraction)
.

(\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{Z})\land(\mathbf{U}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\given\mathbf{Z})\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{U}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

By assumption we have the two factorizations:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T),

(4)

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(X,Z|T),

(5)

with some Markov kernels
\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)
,
\mathbf{P}(U|Z)
.

We then define the Markov kernel:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}(X,U|Z)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{P}(U|Z).

(6)

We will now show that its marginal:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}(X|Z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\circ\mathbf{P}(U|Z).

(7)

will satisfy the claim.

If we marginalize out
Y
from equation
4
we get:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T).

(8)

Equating equations
5
and
8
gives:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(X,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T).

(9)

Marginalizing out
X
in equation
9
on both sides gives:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T).

(10)

If we now plug equation
10
into
8
then we get:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{P}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T)

(11)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:rcc-c}}}{{=}}\mathbf{R}(X,U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T).

(12)

If we marginalize out
U
in equation
12
and use definition
7
we arrive at:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T).

(13)

We now get:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:rcc-d}}}{{=}}\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)

(14)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:rcc-b}}}{{=}}\mathbf{P}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(X,Z|T)

(15)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:rcc-f}}}{{=}}\mathbf{P}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{R}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T)

(16)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{P}(U|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T)

(17)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{eq:rcc-r}}}{{=}}\mathbf{R}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T).

(18)

By the essential uniqueness (see Lemma
C.2
) of such a factorization we get that for every
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}(X\in A|U,Z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}(X\in A|Z)\qquad\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T)\text{-a.s.}

(19)

The same equation then holds also
\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T)
-a.s. (by ignoring the non-occuring argument
y
).
Plugging
19
back into the equation
4
we get:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T),

(20)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T).

(21)

This shows the claim.
∎

Lemma E.16

(Flipped Left Cross Contraction)
.

(\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{U}\otimes\mathbf{Z})\land(\mathbf{Y}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{U}\given\mathbf{Z})\implies\mathbf{Y}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{U}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

By assumption we have the two factorizations:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T),

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(Y|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T),

with some Markov kernels
\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)
,
\mathbf{P}(Y|Z)
.

Marginalizing out
Y
in the first equation we get the equality:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T).

Plugging all three equations into each other we get:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{P}(Y|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(Y|Z)\otimes\mathbf{Q}(X|U,Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(U,Z|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(Y|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(X,U,Z|T).

This shows the claim.
∎

Corollary E.17
.

The class of all transitional random variables with standard measurable spaces as codomain
on transition probability space
\left(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T},\mathbf{K}(W|T)\right)

together with the product of Markov kernels
\otimes
, the equivalence of isomorphisms of measurable spaces
\cong
,
the relation
\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}
, the one-point Markov kernel
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}
and transitional conditional independence
\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}
forms a

\mathbf{T}
-
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}
-separoid (or in simpler symbols, a
T
-
\ast
-separoid).

Furthermore, the class of transitional random variables of the form
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(X|W,T)
with standard measurable spaces as codomains
on
\left(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T},\mathbf{K}(W|T)\right)

form a join-semi-lattice (with
\otimes
and
\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}
) up to almost-sure anti-symmetry and a
T
-
\ast
-separoid.

Remark E.18
.

Similarly, if one replaces measurability with universal measurability everywhere and standard measurable spaces
with universal measurable spaces, then also the class of transitional random variables with universal measurable spaces as codomains
form a
\mathbf{T}
-
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}
-separoid.
Furthermore, the ones of the form
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(X|W,T)
with universal measurable spaces as codomains form a join-semi-lattice
(with
\otimes
and
\precsim_{\mathbf{K},\bullet}
) up to almost-sure anti-symmetry and a

T
-
\ast
-separoid.

E.2
Derived Separoid Rules for Transitional Conditional Independence

Most the following rules follow directly from the general
T
-
\ast
-separoid rules proven in the last subsection and
the general theory developed in Section
A
. Nonetheless, since we have to track which of the spaces
are standard or countably generated we will go through those proofs carefully again.

Lemma E.19

(Extended
\mathbf{T}
-Restricted Right Redundancy)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be standard and
\mathcal{Z}
be countably generated. Then:

\mathbf{T}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

Extended
\mathbf{T}
-Restricted Right Redundancy
E.19
can be proven using

\mathbf{T}
-Restricted Right Redundancy
E.5

(
\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\given\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{T}
) together with Extended Left Redundancy
E.3
(
\implies\mathbf{T}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\given\mathbf{Z}
)
and Right Cross Contraction
E.15
(
\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{T}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\given\mathbf{Z}
) and
then Right Decomposition
E.7

(
\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\given\mathbf{Z}
).
∎

Lemma E.20

(Restricted Symmetry)
.

(\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z})\land(\mathbf{Y}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\given\mathbf{Z})\implies\mathbf{Y}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

This follows from Flipped Left Cross Contraction
E.16
with
\mathbf{U}={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}
.
∎

Lemma E.21

(
\mathbf{T}
-Restricted Symmetry)
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be standard and
\mathcal{Z}
be countably generated.
Then:

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{T}\implies\mathbf{Y}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\given\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{T}.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{Y}
is standard and
\mathcal{Z}
countably generated we get by
\mathbf{T}
-Restricted Right Redundancy
E.5
:

\mathbf{Y}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\given\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{T}.

Together with
\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{T}
and Restricted Symmetry
E.20
we get:

\mathbf{Y}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\given\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{T}.

∎

Lemma E.22

(Symmetry)
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be standard and
\mathcal{Z}
be countably generated and
\mathcal{T}=\Asterisk=\{\ast\}
the one-point space.
Then:

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\implies\mathbf{Y}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

This follows similarly to
\mathbf{T}
-Restricted Symmetry
E.21
with
T=\ast
.
∎

Lemma E.23

(Inverted Left Decomposition)
.

\left(\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\right)\land\left(\mathbf{U}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Z}\right)\implies\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{U}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

Inverted Left Decomposition
E.23
can be proven using Extended
Left Redundancy
E.3
(
\implies\mathbf{U}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Z}
)
together with the assumption (
\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}
) and Left Contraction
E.13

(
\implies\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{U}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}
).
∎

Lemma E.24

(
\mathbf{T}
-Extended Inverted Right Decomposition)
.

\left(\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\right)\land\left(\mathbf{U}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{T}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{Z}\right)\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{T}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{U}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

\mathbf{T}
-Extended Inverted Right Decomposition
E.24
can be proven using

\mathbf{T}
-Inverted Right Decomposition
E.8
(
\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{T}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}
)
in combination with Extended Left Redundancy
E.3
(
\implies\mathbf{U}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\given\mathbf{T}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{Z}
)
and Flipped Left Cross Contraction
E.16
(
\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{T}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{U}\given\mathbf{Z}
).
∎

Lemma E.25

(Equivalent Exchange)
.

\left(\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\right)\land\left(\mathbf{Z}\approx_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\right)\implies\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}.

Proof.

We get:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Lemma \ref{lem:join-upper-bound-2}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{T}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{Z},

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\mathbf{T}\text{-Ext. Inv. Right Decomposition \ref{sep:tci:ext-inv-r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{T}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\given\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Decomposition \ref{sep:tci:r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\given\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Weak Union \ref{sep:tci:r-uni}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbf{Z},

(a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Extended Left Redundancy \ref{sep:tci:ext-l-red}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}^{\prime},

(b)

\displaystyle\ \eqref{eq:int-a}\land\eqref{eq:int-b}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Contraction \ref{sep:tci:l-con}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Z}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Decomposition \ref{sep:tci:l-dec}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}.

∎

Lemma E.26

(Full Equivalent Exchange)
.

If
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\approx_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\approx_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\approx_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}

then we have the equivalence:

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\qquad\iff\qquad\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\given\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Lemma \ref{lem:join-upper-bound-2}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Inverted Left Decomposition \ref{sep:tci:inv-l-dec}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\otimes\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Left Decomposition \ref{sep:tci:l-dec}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z},

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Lemma \ref{lem:join-upper-bound-2}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{T}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\mathbf{T}\text{-Ext. Inv. Right Decomposition \ref{sep:tci:ext-inv-r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{T}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\otimes\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\given\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Right Decomposition \ref{sep:tci:r-dec}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\given\mathbf{Z},

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}\approx_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Z}

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{\text{Equivalent Exchange \ref{sep:tci:eq-ex}}}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\prime}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\given\mathbf{Z}^{\prime}.

The other direction works similarly.
∎

Appendix F
Proofs - Applications to Statistical Theory

Next we will give a proof that the classical Fisher-Neyman factorization criterion (see
[
Fis22
,
Ney36
,
HS49
,
Bur61
]
)
implies sufficiency reformulated as transitional conditional independence.

Theorem F.1

(Fisher-Neyman)
.

Consider a statistical model, witten as a Markov kernel,
\mathbf{P}(X|\Theta)
.
Assume that we can write it in the following form:

\mathbf{P}(X\in A|\Theta=\theta)=\int_{A}h(x)\cdot g(S(x);\theta)\,d{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(x),

where
X
takes values in a standard measurable space
\mathcal{X}
,
S:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{S}
is measurable,
\mathcal{S}
countably generated,

{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is a measure on
\mathcal{X}
,

g,h\geq 0
are measurable and
h
is
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
-integrable (e.g. exponential families are of such form).

Then
S
is a sufficient statistic of
X
for
\mathbf{P}(X|\Theta)
:

\quad\displaystyle X\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X|\Theta)}\Theta\given S.

Proof.

Consider the function:

f:=\left(\int h\,d{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right)\cdot g

and the probability measure
\mathbf{Q}(X)
given by:

\mathbf{Q}(X\in A):=\frac{1}{\int h\,d{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\int_{A}h(x)\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(dx),\qquad\text{ i.e.\ }\quad\frac{d\mathbf{Q}}{d{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(x)=\frac{h(x)}{\int h\,d{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}.

Then we have the joint distribution
\mathbf{Q}(X,S)
and a regular conditional probability distribution
\mathbf{Q}(X|S)
, by
Theorem
C.6
.
With this we get:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(S\in B|\Theta=\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(X\in S^{-1}(B)|\Theta=\theta)

\displaystyle=\int\mathbbm{1}_{B}(S(x))\cdot g(S(x);\theta)\cdot h(x)\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(dx)

\displaystyle=\int\mathbbm{1}_{B}(S(x))\cdot f(S(x);\theta)\,\mathbf{Q}(X\in dx)

\displaystyle=\int\mathbbm{1}_{B}(s)\cdot f(s;\theta)\,\mathbf{Q}(S\in ds).

This means:

\frac{\mathbf{P}(S\in ds|\Theta=\theta)}{\mathbf{Q}(S\in ds)\phantom{|\Theta=\theta}}(s)=f(s;\theta).

With this we get:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(X\in A,S\in B|\Theta=\theta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(X\in A\cap S^{-1}(B)|\Theta=\theta)

\displaystyle=\int\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(S(x))\cdot g(S(x);\theta)\cdot h(x)\,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(dx)

\displaystyle=\int\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(S(x))\cdot f(S(x);\theta)\,\mathbf{Q}(X\in dx)

\displaystyle=\int\int\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(s)\cdot f(s;\theta)\,\mathbf{Q}(X\in dx,S\in ds)

\displaystyle=\int\int\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(s)\cdot f(s;\theta)\,\mathbf{Q}(X\in dx|S=s)\,\mathbf{Q}(S\in ds)

\displaystyle=\int\mathbf{Q}(X\in A|S=s)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(s)\cdot f(s;\theta)\,\mathbf{Q}(S\in ds)

\displaystyle=\int\mathbf{Q}(X\in A|S=s)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(s)\,\mathbf{P}(S\in ds|\Theta=\theta)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{Q}(X|S)\otimes\mathbf{P}(S|\Theta)\right)(A\times B,\theta).

This implies:

\mathbf{P}(X,S|\Theta)=\mathbf{Q}(X|S)\otimes\mathbf{P}(S|\Theta),

and thus:

X\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X|\Theta)}\Theta\given S.

∎

This is one direction of the Fisher-Neyman factorization theorem for sufficiency (see
[
Fis22
,
Ney36
,
HS49
]
).
Our definition of conditional independence generalizes the factorization theorem to Markov kernels (per definition) without the necessity of densities and/or reference measures.

The next theorem contains the proof for the propensity score of Section
4.4
and the
weak likelihood principle of Section
4.5
.

Theorem F.2
.

Let
\mathbf{P}(Y|X)
be a Markov kernel.
For
x\in\mathcal{X}
we put:

E(x):=\mathbf{P}(Y|X=x)\;\in\;\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}).

Then
E:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
is measurable,
E\precsim X
and we have:

Y\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(Y|X)}X\given E.

Furthermore, if
S:\,\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{S}
is another measurable map (
S\precsim X
) with:

Y\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(Y|X)}X\given S,

then:

E\precsim S\precsim X.

Proof.

For the first claim we define
\mathcal{E}:=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
and the Markov kernel:

\mathbf{Q}(Y|E):\,\mathcal{E}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y},\quad\mathbf{Q}(Y\in B|E=e):=e(B),

for
e\in\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
and
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Evaluating this gives:

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{Q}(Y|E)\otimes\mathbf{P}(E|X)\right)(B\times C,x)

\displaystyle=\int_{C}\mathbf{Q}(Y\in B|E=e)\,{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(E\in de|X=x)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(Y\in B|E=E(x))\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{C}(E(x))

\displaystyle=E(x)(B)\cdot{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(E\in C|X=x)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(Y\in B|X=x)\cdot{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(E\in C|X=x)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(Y\in B,E\in C|X=x)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(Y,E|X)(B\times C,x).

Multiplying both sides with
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(X|X)
implies:

\mathbf{Q}(Y|E)\otimes\mathbf{P}(X,E|X)=\mathbf{P}(Y,X,E|X),

and thus:

Y\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(Y|X)}X\given E.

This shows the first claim.

For the second claim, the conditional independence:

Y\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(Y|X)}X\given S.

implies that there exists a measurable function:

\mathbf{P}(Y|S,\cancel{X}):\,\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}),

such that:

\mathbf{P}(Y,S|X)=\mathbf{P}(Y|S,\cancel{X})\otimes\mathbf{P}(S|X).

Noting that
\mathbf{P}(S|X)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(S|X)
and marginalizing
S
out we get:

E(x)=\mathbf{P}(Y|X=x)=\mathbf{P}(Y|S=S(x),\cancel{X}),

for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
.
This shows the second claim.
∎

We now turn to Bayesian statistics.

Theorem F.3

(Bayesian statistics)
.

Let
\mathbf{P}(X|\Theta)
be a Markov kernel between standard measurable spaces
and
\mathbf{P}(\Theta|\Pi)
be another Markov kernel.
Then put:

\mathbf{P}(X,\Theta|\Pi):=\mathbf{P}(X|\Theta)\otimes\mathbf{P}(\Theta|\Pi).

Then by Theorem
C.6
we have a (regular) conditional Markov kernel:

\mathbf{P}(\Theta|X,\Pi),

which is unique up to
\mathbf{P}(X|\Pi)
-null set.
We now define the transitional random variable:

Z(x,\pi):=\mathbf{P}(\Theta|X=x,\Pi=\pi),

which gives us a joint (transition) probability distribution:
\mathbf{P}(X,\Theta,Z|\Pi)
.

With the above notations we have the conditional independence:

\Theta\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X,\Theta|\Pi)}X\given Z.

Furthermore, if
S
is another deterministic measurable function in
(X,\Pi)
such that:

\Theta\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X,\Theta|\Pi)}X\given S,

then:

Z\precsim S\quad\mathbf{P}(X|\Pi)\text{-a.s.}

Proof.

For the first statement consider the Markov kernel given by:

\mathbf{K}(\Theta\in D|Z=z):=z(D).

Then we get:

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{K}(\Theta|Z)\otimes\mathbf{P}(Z,X|\Pi)\right)(B\times C\times A,\pi)

\displaystyle=\int_{C\times A}\mathbf{K}(\Theta\in B|Z=z)\,\mathbf{P}(Z\in dz,X\in dx|\Pi=\pi)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\int_{C}\mathbf{K}(\Theta\in B|Z=z)\,{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(Z\in dz|X=x,\Pi=\pi)\,\mathbf{P}(X\in dx|\Pi=\pi)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\mathbbm{1}_{C}(Z(x,\pi))\cdot\mathbf{P}(\Theta\in B|X=x,\Pi=\pi)\,\mathbf{P}(X\in dx|\Pi=\pi)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\int_{C}\mathbf{P}(\Theta\in B|X=x,\Pi=\pi)\,{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(Z\in dz|X=x,\Pi=\pi)\,\mathbf{P}(X\in dx|\Pi=\pi)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(\Theta\in B,Z\in C,X\in A|\Pi=\pi).

This shows the first claim.

The conditional independence in the second claim gives us the factorization:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\Theta,S,X|\Pi)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(\Theta|S,\cancel{X,\Pi})\otimes\mathbf{P}(S,X|\Pi)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(\Theta|S,\cancel{X,\Pi})\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(S|X,\Pi)\otimes\mathbf{P}(X|\Pi).

On the other hand we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\Theta,S,X|\Pi)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(\Theta|X,\Pi)\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(S|X,\Pi)\otimes\mathbf{P}(X|\Pi).

Marginalizing
S
out in those equations gives:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(\Theta|S=S(X,\Pi),\cancel{X,\Pi})\otimes\mathbf{P}(X|\Pi)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(\Theta|X,\Pi)\otimes\mathbf{P}(X|\Pi).

Because conditional Markov kernels are essentially unique by
C.2
we get:

\mathbf{P}(\Theta|S=S(X,\Pi),\cancel{X,\Pi}))=\mathbf{P}(\Theta|X,\Pi)=Z(X,\Pi)\qquad\mathbf{P}(X|\Pi)\text{-a.s.}.

This shows:

Z\precsim S\quad\mathbf{P}(X|\Pi)\text{-a.s.}

∎

Theorem F.4

(A weak likelihood principle for Bayesian statistics)
.

We also have the transitional conditional independence with
L(\theta):=\mathbf{P}(X|\Theta=\theta)
:

X\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X,\Theta|\Pi)}\Theta,\Pi\given L.

Furthermore, if
\mathcal{X}
is countably generated, then any other measurable map
S
in
\Theta
with:

X\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X,\Theta|\Pi)}\Theta,\Pi\given S,

satisfies:

L\precsim S\precsim\Theta\quad\mathbf{P}(\Theta|\Pi)\text{-a.s.}

Proof.

We have:

\mathbf{P}(X,L,\Theta,\Pi|\Pi)=\mathbf{Q}(X|L)\otimes\mathbf{P}(L,\Theta,\Pi|\Pi),

with
\mathbf{Q}(X\in A|L=\ell):=\ell(A)
for
\ell\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
.

For the reverse direction we get a factorization:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(S,X,\Theta|\Pi)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}(X|S)\otimes\mathbf{P}(S,\Theta|\Pi)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}(X|S)\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(S|\Theta)\otimes\mathbf{P}(\Theta|\Pi).

Marginalizing
S
out on both sides gives:

\mathbf{P}(X|\Theta)\otimes\mathbf{P}(\Theta|\Pi)=\mathbf{K}(X|S=S(\Theta))\otimes\mathbf{P}(\Theta|\Pi).

Since such factorizations are essentially unique by Lemma
C.2
and
\mathcal{X}
is countably generated, we have that
for
\mathbf{P}(\Theta|\Pi)
-almost-all
(\theta,\pi)
we get:

L(\theta)=\mathbf{P}(X|\Theta=\theta)=\mathbf{K}(X|S=S(\theta)).

This shows:

L\precsim S\precsim\Theta\quad\mathbf{P}(\Theta|\Pi)\text{-a.s.}

∎

Appendix G
Proofs - Reparameterization of Transitional Random Variables

In this section we generalize a few folklore results via now standard techniques that were introduced after
[
Dar53
,
Č82
]
.

Lemma G.1
.

Let
\bar{\mathbb{R}}:=[-\infty,\infty]
be endowed with the usual ordering and Borel
\sigma
-algebra.
Let
\mathbf{P}
be a probability measure on
\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and
F(x):=\mathbf{P}([-\infty,x])
.
Then
F:\bar{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
is non-decreasing, right-continous with at most countably many discontinuities and
F(\infty)=1
.
So
R(t):=\inf F^{-1}([t,1])
is a well-defined map
R:[0,1]\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
, non-decreasing, left-continous with at most countably many discontinuities and
R(0)=-\infty
.
Furthermore, for
x\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and
t\in[0,1]
we have:

t\leq F(x)\iff R(t)\leq x.

In particular, we have
F(R(t))\geq t
, thus
R(t)\in F^{-1}([t,1])
the minimal element. We also have
R(F(x))\leq x
, with equality if and only if
x\in R([0,1])
.
Furthermore,
F
and
R
are measurable and
R_{*}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\mathbf{P}
. We also have that
R
is a reflexive generalized inverse of
F
, i.e.:

F\circ R\circ F=F,\qquad R\circ F\circ R=R.

Proof.

From the properties of
\mathbf{P}
it is clear that
F
is non-decreasing, right-continuous and
F(\infty)=1
.

Let
D_{F}\subseteq\bar{\mathbb{R}}
be the set of discontinuities of
F
and
x\in D_{F}
. Then there exists a
q(x)\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
F_{-}(x)<q(x)<F_{+}(x)
. If now
x_{1}<x_{2}
are two such points we get:

q(x_{1})<F_{+}(x_{1})\leq F_{-}(x_{2})<q(x_{2})
. So the map
q:D_{F}\to\mathbb{Q}
is injective. Thus
D_{F}
is countable.

Next, we show that
R(t)\in F^{-1}([t,1])
, thus
R(t)=\min F^{-1}([t,1])
.
For this let
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq F^{-1}([t,1])
be a non-increasing sequence converging to
R(t)
.
Then by the right-continuity
F(x_{n})
converges to
F(R(t))
from above.
So we have:

F(R(t))=\inf_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F(x_{n})\geq t.

It follows that
F(R(t))\geq t
and thus
R(t)\in F^{-1}([t,1])
. This shows the claim.

R
is clearly non-decreasing, thus has only a countable set of discontiuities
D_{R}\subseteq[0,1]
by the same arguments before, and
R(0)=-\infty
. To see that
R(t)
is left-continuous let
t\in[0,1]
and
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
a non-decreasing sequence converging to
t
from below. Then by the monotonicity of
R
we have

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R(t_{n})\leq R(t)
. On the other hand we have:

t=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}t_{n}\leq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}F(R(t_{n}))\leq F(\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R(t_{n})),

implying:
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R(t_{n})\in F^{-1}([t,1])
and thus
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R(t_{n})\geq R(t)
, leading to equality, which shows the claim.

For any
x\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}
we have the implication:

x\geq R(t)\;\implies\;F(x)\geq F(R(t))\geq t.

For any
x\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and any
t\in[0,1]
we have the implications:

\begin{array}[]{llll}t\leq F(x)&\iff&F(x)\in[t,1]\\
&\iff&x\in F^{-1}([t,1])\\
&\implies&x\geq\inf F^{-1}([t,1])=R(t).\end{array}

Together this shows for any
x\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and
t\in[0,1]
the equivalence:

t\leq F(x)\iff R(t)\leq x.

Since
F(x)\leq F(x)
we get
R(F(x))\leq x
for all
x\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}
. If equality holds then
x\in R([0,1])
. And, if
x=R(t)
for some
t\in[0,1]
then we use the inequalities
x\geq R(F(x))
and
F(R(t))\geq t
to conclude:

x\geq R(F(x))=R(F(R(t)))\geq R(t)=x,

showing equality, and that:

R\circ F\circ R=R.

Similarly for
t=F(x)
we get:

t\leq F(R(t))=F(R(F(x)))\leq F(x)=t,

showing

F\circ R\circ F=F.

Now consider the uniform distribution
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
on
[0,1]
and any
x\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then we have:

\begin{array}[]{llll}(R_{*}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})([-\infty,x])&=&{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(R^{-1}([-\infty,x]))\\
&=&{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(t\in[0,1]\,|\,R(t)\leq x)\\
&=&{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(t\in[0,1]\,|\,t\leq F(x))\\
&=&{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}([0,F(x)])\\
&=&F(x)\\
&=&\mathbf{P}([-\infty,x]).\end{array}

It follows that:
R_{*}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\mathbf{P}
.∎

Lemma G.2
.

Let the notation be like in
G.1
. For
u\in[0,1]
and
x\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}
define:

\displaystyle F_{u}(x):=E(x;u)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{P}([-\infty,x))+u\cdot\mathbf{P}(\{x\}).

Then
E:\bar{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]\to[0,1]
is measurable, non-decreasing in both arguments with
F_{0}(-\infty)=0
,
F_{1}(\infty)=1
,
F_{0}
is left-continuous and

F_{u}(\tilde{x})\leq F_{1}(\tilde{x})\leq F_{0}(x)\leq F_{u}(x)

for any
\tilde{x}<x
,
u\in[0,1]
.
We further have for every
u\in(0,1]
:

R\circ F_{u}\circ R=R,

and
R\circ F_{u}=\mathrm{id}_{\bar{\mathbb{R}}}

\mathbf{P}
-almost-surely for any
u\in(0,1]
.

Proof.

Most of the properties are clear from its definition.
Let
x<\tilde{x}
then
[-\infty,x]\subseteq[-\infty,\tilde{x})
and thus
F_{1}(x)\leq F_{0}(\tilde{x})
.

To show
R\circ F_{u}\circ R=R
fix a
t\in[0,1]
,
u\in(0,1]
and let
x:=R(t)
. If
F_{1}
is continuous in
x
then
F_{u}=F_{1}
and the claim
R\circ F_{1}\circ R=R
was already shown using the inequalities:

x\geq R(F_{1}(x))=R(F_{1}(R(t)))\geq R(t)=x.

So let us assume that
F_{1}
is discontinuous in
x=R(t)
.
Then
F_{u}(x)\in(F_{0}(x),F_{1}(x)]
. We have:

R(F_{u}(x))=\min\{\tilde{x}\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}\,|\,F_{1}(\tilde{x})\geq F_{u}(x)\}.

If
F_{1}(\tilde{x})\geq F_{u}(x)>F_{0}(x)
then
\tilde{x}\geq x
, otherwise
\tilde{x}<x
leads to the contradiction
F_{1}(\tilde{x})\leq F_{0}(x)
. Since clearly
F_{1}(x)\geq F_{u}(x)
we must have:

R(F_{u}(x))=x,

with
x=R(t)
, which proves the claim:
R\circ F_{u}\circ R=R
for
u\in(0,1]
.

We now want to show that
R\circ F_{u}=\mathrm{id}_{\bar{\mathbb{R}}}

\mathbf{P}
-a.s. for
u\in(0,1]
.
From
R\circ F_{u}\circ R=R
we already see, that
R\circ F_{u}|_{R([0,1])}=\mathrm{id}_{R([0,1])}
.
We will see below that
C:=\bar{\mathbb{R}}\setminus R([0,1])
is measurable and
\mathbf{P}(C)=0
, which will prove the claim.

In the following we will only need
F=F_{1}
.
First, by
G.1
we know that for any
x\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}
we have
R(F(x))\leq x
with equality if and only if
x\in R([0,1])
. So this gives us the equivalence:

x\in C\;\iff\;x>R(F(x)).

We now claim that
(R(F(x)),x]\subseteq C
for every
x\in C
:
Indeed, If
\tilde{x}\in(R(F(x)),x]
then:

F(x)=F(R(F(x)))\leq F(\tilde{x})\leq F(x)

and thus
F(\tilde{x})=F(x)
, from which follows that
R(F(\tilde{x}))=R(F(x))<\tilde{x}
and ergo
\tilde{x}\in C
.

It follows that
C
is the union of such intervals
(R(F(x)),x]
with
x\in C
. Furthermore,
F(C)
is contained in the set of discontinuities
D_{R}
of
R
: otherwise there would be an
x\in C
and a
t\geq F(x)
such that
R(t)\in(R(F(x)),x]\subseteq C
, which is a contradiction.
Since
D_{R}
is countable it must follow that
F(C)
and thus also
R(F(C))
is at most countable. Write
R(F(C))=\{x_{n}\,|\,n\in\mathbb{N}\}
, which is the set of the possible left end-points of the above intervals.
For each fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
let

C_{n}:=\{x\in C\,|\,R(F(x))=x_{n}\},

which is, as a union of intervals
(x_{n},x]
,
x\in C_{n}
, either of the form
(x_{n},\bar{x}_{n}]
or
(x_{n},\bar{x}_{n})
with
\bar{x}_{n}:=\sup C_{n}
. In both cases we can cover
C_{n}
by

C_{n,m}:=(x_{n},x_{n,m}]
with
x_{n,m}\in C_{n}
either equal to
\bar{x}_{n}
or converging to it from below for running
m
.
So we can write
C
as the countable union:

C=\bigcup_{n,m\in\mathbb{N}}C_{n,m}.

We now have for each
x=x_{n,m}
:

\mathbf{P}(C_{n,m})=\mathbf{P}((x_{n},x])=\mathbf{P}((R(F(x)),x])=F(x)-F(R(F(x)))=F(x)-F(x)=0.

This implies:

\mathbf{P}(C)=\mathbf{P}\left(\bigcup_{n,m\in\mathbb{N}}C_{n,m}\right)\leq\sum_{n,m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbf{P}(C_{n,m})=0,

showing that
\mathbf{P}(C)=0
and thus:

R\circ F_{u}=\mathrm{id}_{\bar{\mathbb{R}}}\qquad\mathbf{P}\text{-a.s.}

for
u\in(0,1]
.
∎

Lemma G.3
.

Let the notations be like in
G.1
and
G.2
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
be the uniform distribution on
[0,1]
and

\bar{\mathbf{P}}:=\mathbf{P}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
the product distribution on
\bar{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]
.
For every
e\in[0,1]
define the event:

\{E\leq e\}:=\{(x,u)\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]\;|\;E(x;u)\leq e\}.

Then
\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E\leq e)=e
.
In other words, the random variable:

\begin{array}[]{ccccl}E&:&\bar{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]&\to&[0,1],\\
&&(x,u)&\mapsto&\mathbf{P}([-\infty,x))+u\cdot\mathbf{P}(\{x\}),\end{array}

is uniformly distributed under
\bar{\mathbf{P}}=\mathbf{P}\otimes{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
.

Furthermore,
R(E)=X

\bar{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s., where
X:\bar{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
is the canonical projection onto the first factor:
X(x,u):=x
, and which has distribution
\mathbf{P}
.

Proof.

First, since
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\{0\})=0
we can w.l.o.g. exclude
u=0
and restrict
\bar{\mathbf{P}}
to

\bar{\mathbb{R}}\times(0,1]
.
We have seen in
G.2
that
R\circ F_{u}\circ R=R
for
u\in(0,1]
,
which translates to:

R\circ E|_{R([0,1])\times(0,1]}=X|_{R([0,1])\times(0,1]}.

Also with
C:=\bar{\mathbb{R}}\setminus R([0,1])
we get:

\bar{\mathbf{P}}(C\times(0,1])=\mathbf{P}(C)\cdot{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}((0,1])=0\cdot 1=0.

So we get the second claim that:

R\circ E=X\qquad\bar{\mathbf{P}}\text{-a.s.}

Now we turn to
\{E\leq e\}
for
e\in[0,1]
. We abbreviate

U:\bar{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]\to[0,1]
to be the projection onto the
second factor:
U(x,u):=u
, which is uniformly distributed under
\bar{\mathbf{P}}
,
and also
p(x):=\mathbf{P}(\{x\})=F_{1}(x)-F_{0}(x)
. With these notations:
E=F_{0}(X)+U\cdot p(X)
.

First, we show that
\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E=e)=0
for all
e\in[0,1]
. For this let
x:=R(e)
.
Then by the above (
R(E)=X

\bar{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s.) we have:

\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E=e)=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E=e,\,X=x).

We have to distinguish between two cases:
p(x)=0
and
p(x)>0
.

Case
p(x)=0
: We have:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E=e)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E=e,X=x)

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\mathbf{P}}(X=x)

\displaystyle=p(x)

\displaystyle=0.

Case
p(x)>0
: We get:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E=e)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E=e,X=x)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(F_{0}(X)+U\cdot p(X)=e,\,X=x)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}\left(U=\frac{e-F_{0}(x)}{p(x)},\,X=x\right)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\left(\left\{\frac{e-F_{0}(x)}{p(x)}\right\}\right)\cdot p(x)

\displaystyle=0.

To prove
\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E\leq e)=e
for
e\in[0,1]
we have several cases:

Case
e\in F_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{R}})
:
Let
\tilde{x}
be any element in
\bar{\mathbb{R}}
with
e=F_{1}(\tilde{x})
(e.g.
\tilde{x}=R(e)
). Then we get:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E\leq e)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E\leq F_{1}(\tilde{x}))

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(R(E)\leq\tilde{x})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R\circ E=X}}{{=}}\bar{\mathbf{P}}(X\leq\tilde{x})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}([-\infty,\tilde{x}])\cdot{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}((0,1])

\displaystyle=F_{1}(\tilde{x})\cdot 1

\displaystyle=e.

For the cases
e\notin F_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{R}})
we put
x:=R(e)
and
\tilde{e}:=F_{0}(x)
.

Then by definition,
x
is minimal with
F_{1}(x)\geq e
.
We also have
\tilde{e}=F_{0}(x)\leq e
.
Otherwise:
e<F_{0}(x)=\sup_{\tilde{x}<x}F_{1}(\tilde{x})

implied that there existed
\tilde{x}<x
with
e<F_{1}(\tilde{x})\leq F_{0}(x)
, which is a contradiction to the minimality of
x=R(e)
.
Since
\tilde{e}\leq e
we can decompose:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E\leq e)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E<\tilde{e})+\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E=\tilde{e})+\bar{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{e}<E\leq e).

We have already seen that the second term
\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E=\tilde{e})=0
vanishes.

For the first term we have:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E<\tilde{e})

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E<F_{0}(x))

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E<F_{0}(x))

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E<\sup_{\tilde{x}<x}F_{1}(\tilde{x}))

\displaystyle=\sup_{\tilde{x}<x}\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E\leq F_{1}(\tilde{x}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{=}}\sup_{\tilde{x}<x}F_{1}(\tilde{x})

\displaystyle=F_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\tilde{e}.

Equation (*) comes from the previous case for
F_{1}(\tilde{x})\in F_{1}(\bar{\mathbb{R}})
.

For the third term
\bar{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{e}<E\leq e)
first note that
E\in(\tilde{e},e]
implies that
X=x

\bar{\mathbf{P}}
-a.s. by applying
R
: Indeed, every element

t\in(\tilde{e},e]\subseteq(F_{0}(x),F_{1}(x)]
can be written as
t=F_{\tilde{u}}(x)
for an
\tilde{u}\in(0,1]
and we can use:

R(t)=R(F_{\tilde{u}}(R(e))=R(e)=x.

For
p(x)>0
and the above we get:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{e}<E\leq e)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{e}<E\leq e,\,X=x)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(0<F_{0}(X)+U\cdot p(X)-F_{0}(x)\leq e-\tilde{e},X=x)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(0<U\leq\frac{e-\tilde{e}}{p(x)},X=x)

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\left(\left(0,\frac{e-\tilde{e}}{p(x)}\right]\right)\cdot\mathbf{P}(\{x\})

\displaystyle=\frac{e-\tilde{e}}{p(x)}\cdot p(x)

\displaystyle=e-\tilde{e}.

For the case
p(x)=0
, the first row can be upper bounded by
\bar{\mathbf{P}}(X=x)=p(x)=0
as before, but we also have
\tilde{e}-e=0
in this case, and the equality stays trivially true as well.

Putting all together we get:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E\leq e)

\displaystyle=\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E<\tilde{e})+\bar{\mathbf{P}}(E=\tilde{e})+\bar{\mathbf{P}}(\tilde{e}<E\leq e)

\displaystyle=\tilde{e}+0+e-\tilde{e}

\displaystyle=e.

This shows the claim.
∎

Theorem G.4
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be any measurable space and
\mathcal{X}
be a standard measurable space with a fixed embedding
\iota:\mathcal{X}\hookrightarrow\bar{\mathbb{R}}=[-\infty,\infty]
onto a Borel subset (which always exists, so w.l.o.g.
\mathcal{X}=\bar{\mathbb{R}}
endowed with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra).
Let
\mathbf{K}(X|Z):\mathcal{Z}\dasharrow\mathcal{X}
be a Markov kernel.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{U}:=[0,1]
and
\mathbf{K}(U)
be the uniform distribution/Markov kernel on
\mathcal{U}
. We write:

\mathbf{K}(U,X|Z):=\mathbf{K}(U)\otimes\mathbf{K}(X|Z).

Also put:

\displaystyle F(x;u|z)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{K}(X<x|Z=z)+u\cdot\mathbf{K}(X=x|Z=z)

\displaystyle R(e|z)

\displaystyle:=\inf\left\{\tilde{x}\in\mathcal{X}\,|F(\tilde{x};1|z)\geq e\right\}.

Let
E:=F(X;U|Z)
. We consider
X,U,Z,E
as the measurable maps:

\begin{array}[]{cccl}X:&\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{Z}&\to&\mathcal{X},\\
&(x,u,z)&\mapsto&x,\\
U:&\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{Z}&\to&\mathcal{U},\\
&(x,u,z)&\mapsto&u,\\
Z:&\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{Z}&\to&\mathcal{Z},\\
&(x,u,z)&\mapsto&z,\\
E:&\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{U}\times\mathcal{Z}&\to&\mathcal{E}:=[0,1],\\
&(x,u,z)&\mapsto&F(x;u|z).\end{array}

Then for all
e\in\mathcal{E}
and
z\in\mathcal{Z}
we have:

\mathbf{K}(E\leq e|Z=z)=e,

implying
E\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(U,X|Z)}Z
.
Furthermore, we have:

X=R(E|Z)\quad\mathbf{K}(U,X|Z)\text{-a.s.}.

Proof.

After the measurabilities are checked the statement directly follows from
G.3
by applying it for every
z
separately.
∎

Corollary G.5
.

Let
X
and
Z
be random variables with values in any standard measurable spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Z}
, resp., and with a joint distribution
\mathbf{P}(X,Z)
.
Then there exists a uniformly distributed random variable
E
on
[0,1]
that is
\mathbf{P}
-independent of
Z
and a measurable function
g
such that
X=g(E,Z)

\mathbf{P}
-almost-surely.
Furthermore,
E
can be constructed via a deterministic measurable function in
X
and
Z
and (uniformly distributed) independent noise
U
(on
[0,1]
).

Proof.

The regular conditional probability distribution
\mathbf{P}(X|Z)
exists for standard measurable spaces (and is unique up to a
\mathbf{P}(Z)
-zero-set), and is a Markov kernel. Then apply the result from above for
\mathbf{K}(X|Z):=\mathbf{P}(X|Z)
to get
g(e,z):=R(e|z)
and
E
.
∎

Remark G.6
.

Any Polish space, i.e. any completely metrizable topological space with a countable dense subset (separable), is a standard measurable space in its Borel
\sigma
-algebra. These are fundamental theorems in classical descriptive set theory, see

[
Bog07
,
Fre15
,
Kec95
]
.
Examples of Polish and thus standard measurable spaces are
[0,1]
,
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbb{N}
, any (discrete) finite or countable set, any topological or smooth manifold
\mathcal{X}
, any finite (or even countable) CW-complex
\mathcal{Y}
, etc., (in its usual Borel
\sigma
-algebra). So these are all measurably isomorphic to a Borel subset of
[0,1]
(or
\bar{\mathbb{R}}
), and measurably isomorphic to
[0,1]
(or
\bar{\mathbb{R}}
) itself if non-countable (excluding finite and countable sets).

Appendix H
Operations on Graphs

In this section we present how one can create new CDMGs from old ones through operations like hard interventions,
extensions by soft intervention nodes, marginalization and acyclification.

Definition H.2

.

Remark H.4

(Marginalization preserves ancestral relations and acyclicity)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))
be a CDMG and
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}=\mathbf{G}(V\setminus W|\doit(J))
its marginalization.

1.

Then for
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbf{G}
with
v_{1},v_{2}\notin W
we have the equivalence:

v_{1}\in\mathrm{Anc}^{\mathbf{G}}(v_{2})\quad\iff\quad v_{1}\in\mathrm{Anc}^{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}(v_{2}).

2.

If the CDMG
\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))
is acyclic then also
\mathbf{G}(V\setminus W|\doit(J))
and
a topological order
<
of
\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))
induces a
topological order on
\mathbf{G}(V\setminus W|\doit(J))
(by just ignoring the nodes from
W
).

Lemma H.5

(Marginalization preserves
\sigma
-separation, see
[
FM17
,
FM18
,
FM20
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))
be a CDMG.
Let
A,B,C\subseteq J\,\dot{\cup}\,V
and
W\subseteq V
such that
(A\cup B\cup C)\cap W=\emptyset
.
Then we have the equivalence:

A\Perp_{\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))}^{\sigma}B\given C\qquad\iff\qquad A\Perp_{\mathbf{G}(V\setminus W|\doit(J))}^{\sigma}B\given C.

Notation H.6
.

Let
\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))=(J,V,E,L)
be a CDMG.
Let us write:
\mathbf{G}(V\,\dot{\cup}\,J|\doit(\emptyset))
for the CDMG:
(\emptyset,J\,\dot{\cup}\,V,E,L)
, where we interprete
all nodes from
J
the same as the nodes from
V
. Then we have by definition:

A\Perp_{\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))}^{\sigma}B\given C\qquad\iff\qquad A\Perp_{\mathbf{G}(V\,\dot{\cup}\,J|\doit(\emptyset))}^{\sigma}J\cup B\given C.

Definition H.7

(Acyclification of CDMGs, see
[
FM17
,
FM18
,
FM20
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))
be a CDMG.
We define the
acyclification

\mathbf{G}^{\mathrm{acy}}=\mathbf{G}^{\mathrm{acy}}(V|\doit(J))=(J,V,E^{\mathrm{acy}},L^{\mathrm{acy}})
of
\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))
as follows:

L^{\mathrm{acy}}:=\left\{v\mathrel{\hbox to13.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.95277pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
