\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}u_{k}\in J\cup B^{\prime},

is a
(C^{\prime}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{v\})
-open walk from
A^{\prime}
to
J\cup B^{\prime}
, in contradiction to:

A^{\prime}\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))}J\cup B^{\prime}\given C^{\prime}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{v\}.

So the claim:

A^{\prime}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{v\}\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))}J\cup B^{\prime}\given C^{\prime}\qquad\lor\qquad A^{\prime}\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))}J\cup(B^{\prime}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{v\})\given C^{\prime},

must be true.
So we reduced case C. to case A. or case B., which then imply:

X_{A^{\prime}},X_{v}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J}))}X_{B^{\prime}}\given X_{C^{\prime}}\qquad\lor\qquad X_{A^{\prime}}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J}))}X_{B^{\prime}},X_{v}\given X_{C^{\prime}}.

If we apply Left Weak Union
E.9
to the left and Right Weak Union
E.12
to the right we get:

X_{A^{\prime}}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J}))}X_{B^{\prime}}\given X_{C^{\prime}},X_{v},

which - as before - implies:

X_{A}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J}))}X_{B}\given X_{C}.

This shows the claim in case C.
∎

Remark J.2
.

1.

Note that we only needed to use Left Weak Union
E.9
for nodes
\{v\}\cup A^{\prime}
, which were in
V
.
So no assumptions about standard measurable spaces for
\mathcal{X}_{j}
,
j\in J
, were needed.

2.

All results would also hold under the weaker assumptions where all measurable maps are replaced by universally measurable
maps, all countably generated measurable spaces by universally countably generated ones and all
standard measurable spaces by universal measurable spaces. Analytic versions would also be possible.

Appendix K
Causal Models

K.1
Causal Bayesian Networks - More General

We deviate here from the Section
6.1
of the main paper a bit and
give here a slightly more general definition of causal Bayesian networks that allow, besides input variables, also for latent variables.
The reason is that we want to demonstrate how the correspondence of operations on graphs and operations on causal Bayesian networks, like
interventions, marginalizations, etc., immediately implies the global Markov property, Theorem
6.3
and Appendix
J
, for such more general cases.

Definition K.1

(Causal Bayesian network)
.

A
causal Bayesian network (CBN)

\mathbf{M}
consists of:

1.

finite pairwise disjoint index sets:
J
,
V
,
U
,

corresponding to (non-stochastic) input variables
X_{j}
,
j\in J
,
(stochastic) observed output variables
X_{v}
,
v\in V
, and stochastic unobserved/latent output variables
X_{u}
,
u\in U
.

2.

a conditional directed acyclic graph (CDAG):
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J))=(J,U\,\dot{\cup}\,V,E,\emptyset)
,

i.e. an acyclic CDMG without bi-directed edges:
L=\emptyset
,

3.

a measurable space
\mathcal{X}_{v}
for every
v\in J\,\dot{\cup}\,U\,\dot{\cup}\,V
,
where
\mathcal{X}_{v}
is standard
8
8

8

We could also work under the weaker assumption of universal measurable spaces if we would replace all mentionings of measurability with the weaker notion of universal measurability.

if
v\in U\,\dot{\cup}\,V
,

4.

a Markov kernel, suggestively written as:
\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right)
:

\begin{array}[]{ccrcl}&&\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}&\dashrightarrow&\mathcal{X}_{v},\\
&&\qquad(A,x_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)})&\mapsto&\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\in A\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}=x_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right),\end{array}

for every
v\in U\,\dot{\cup}\,V
, where we write for
D\subseteq J\,\dot{\cup}\,U\,\dot{\cup}\,V
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{D}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{v\in D}\mathcal{X}_{v},

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle:=\Asterisk=\{\ast\},

\displaystyle X_{D}

\displaystyle:=(X_{v})_{v\in D},

\displaystyle X_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle:=\ast,

\displaystyle x_{D}

\displaystyle:=(x_{v})_{v\in D},

\displaystyle x_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle:=\ast.

By abuse of notation, we denote the causal Bayesian network as:

\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J))=\left(\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J)),\left(\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right)\right)_{v\in U\,\dot{\cup}\,V}\right)

Definition K.2
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J))
be a CBN.

1.

We write the
marginal CDMG
of

\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J))
, where the latent nodes
U
are marginalized out, as
\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))=(J,V,E,L)
, which now might have bi-directed edges.

2.

The CBN
\mathbf{M}
comes with the
joint Markov kernel
:

\mathbf{P}(X_{U},X_{V}|\doit(X_{J})):\,\mathcal{X}_{J}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{U\,\dot{\cup}\,V},

given by:

\mathbf{P}(X_{U},X_{V}|\doit(X_{J})):=\bigotimes_{v\in U\,\dot{\cup}\,V}^{>}\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right),

where the product
\otimes
is taken in reverse order of a fixed topological order
<
, i.e. parents proceed children variables.
Note that by remark
2.8
about associativity and (restricted) commutativity of the product the
joint Markov kernels does actually not depend on the topological order.

3.

The marginal Markov kernel
\mathbf{P}\left(X_{V}|\doit\left(X_{J}\right)\right)
is called the
observational Markov kernel
of
\mathbf{M}
.

K.2
Operations on Causal Bayesian Networks

Definition K.3

(Hard interventions on CBNs)
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J))
be a CBN and
W\subseteq J\cup V
.
Then the
hard interventional causal Bayesian network intervened on
W
is given by:
\quad\mathbf{M}(U,V\setminus W|\doit(J\cup W)):=

\left(\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V\setminus W|\doit(J\cup W)),\left(\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right)\right)_{v\in U\,\dot{\cup}\,V\setminus W}\right).

It then comes with joint Markov kernel:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{U},X_{V\setminus W}\Big|\doit\left(X_{J\cup W}\right)\right)=\bigotimes_{v\in U\,\dot{\cup}\,V\setminus W}^{>}\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right).

Definition K.4

(Marginalizing CBNs)
.

Let
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J))
be a CBN and
W\subseteq V
.
Then the
marginal causal Bayesian network
, where
W
is marginalized out, is given by:
\quad\mathbf{M}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,W,V\setminus W|\doit(J)):=

\left(\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J)),\left(\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right)\right)_{v\in U\,\dot{\cup}\,V}\right),

with the only difference that nodes from
W
are moved from
V
to
U
, i.e. are declared latent.
It then comes with the (same) joint Markov kernel:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{U\,\dot{\cup}\,W},X_{V\setminus W}\Big|\doit\left(X_{J}\right)\right)=\bigotimes_{v\in U\,\dot{\cup}\,V}^{>}\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right),

but with the marginalized CDMG as marginal CDMG:
\mathbf{G}(V\setminus W|\doit(J))
, and the marginalized Markov kernel
as marginal Markov kernel:
\mathbf{P}(X_{V\setminus W}|\doit(X_{J}))
.

Remark K.5

(Modelling soft interventions)
.

If we wanted to model
soft interventions
onto some variables given by index set
W
in a CBN
\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J))

with CDMG
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J))
we would introduce soft intervention variables
X_{I_{w}}
for each
w\in W
and then model
the effect of the variable
X_{I_{w}}
onto
X_{w}
together with its “natural” causes
X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(w)}
. This would result
in specifying a Markov kernel
\mathbf{P}(X_{w}|\doit(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(w)},X_{I_{w}}))
for each
w\in W
and leave the Markov kernels

\mathbf{P}(X_{v}|\doit(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}))
for all other variables the same. This then would constitute a new CBN
\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J,I_{W}))

with CDMG
\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J,I_{W}))
.

Example K.6

(Modelling hard interventions as soft interventions)
.

If we now wanted to model hard interventions in a CBN
\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J))

on variables corresponding to
W\in V
as soft interventions we would define for
w\in W
:

1.

\mathcal{X}_{I_{w}}:=\mathcal{X}_{w}\,\dot{\cup}\,\{\star\}
with a new symbol
\star
that represents “no intervention”,

2.

Markov kernels:

\mathbf{P}(X_{w}\in A|\doit(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(w)}=x_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(w)},X_{I_{w}}=x_{I_{w}})):=

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathbf{P}(X_{w}\in A|\doit(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(w)}=x_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(w)}))&\text{ if }&x_{I_{w}}=\star,\\
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(X_{w}\in A|X_{w}=x_{I_{w}})=\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x_{I_{w}})&\text{ if }&x_{I_{w}}\in\mathcal{X}_{w}.\end{array}\right.

This then defines a CBN
\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J,I_{W}))

with CDMG
\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J,I_{W}))
.

K.3
Global Markov Property for More General Causal Models

Here we now extend the global Markov property from causal Baysian networks with input nodes but without latent variables,
see Theorem
6.3
and Appendix
J
,
to causal Bayesian networks with both, input variables and latent variables. We also shortly discuss and indicate how one could
even go beyond and use the results and techniques from this paper to prove a global Markov property based on transitional
conditional independence for, say, structural causal models with input variables, latent variables, variables introducing selection bias
and that also allows for (negative) feedback cycles.

Theorem K.7

(Global Markov property for causal Bayesian networks)
.

Consider a causal Bayesian network
\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J))
with marginal CADMG:
\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))
,
and observational Markov kernel:
\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J}))
.
Then for all
A,B,C\subseteq J\,\dot{\cup}\,V
(not-necessarily disjoint) we have the implication:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))}B\given C\qquad\implies\qquad X_{A}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J}))}X_{B}\given X_{C}.

Recall that we have - per definition - an implicit dependence on
J
,
X_{J}
, resp., in the second argument on each side.

Proof.

Because
\sigma
-separation is preserved under marginalization we have the equivalence:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))}B\given C\quad\iff\quad A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J))}B\given C.

Note that the CBN
\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J))
can mathematically also be considered as the CBN

\mathbf{M}(\emptyset,U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J))
, where all latent variables
are treated like observed ones. Then the global Markov property for
\mathbf{M}(\emptyset,U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J))
, proven in Appendix
J
, implies the mentioned transitional conditional independence:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J))}B\given C\qquad\implies\qquad X_{A}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J}))}X_{B}\given X_{C}.

For this note that the CBN

\mathbf{M}(\emptyset,U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J))
has the same graph

\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J))
as the original CBN
\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J))
.
∎

Remark K.8
.

The above proof technique would work similarly if we would include another set of nodes
S
and variables
X_{S}
, representing

selection bias
. Then we would get a graph that we could denote with
\mathbf{G}(V|S,\doit(J))
, either representing
S
directly
or allowing for undirected edges. An extension of
\sigma
-separation would then be adjusted, see e.g.
[
FM18
,
FM20
,
BFPM21
]
,
such that:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V|S,\doit(J))}B\given C\quad\iff\quad A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V\,\dot{\cup}\,S|\doit(J))}B\given S\cup C.

Then we could just directly apply the proven global Markov property to the right hand side and we would be done:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V\,\dot{\cup}\,S|\doit(J))}B\given S\cup C\quad\implies\quad X_{A}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V},X_{S}|\doit(X_{J}))}X_{B}\given X_{S},X_{C}.

One could even go further and allow for input, latent, selection variables and cycles in a notion of

structural causal models
(SCM), e.g. see again
[
FM18
,
FM20
,
BFPM21
]
, since the compatibility conditions for such
cyclic SCM lead for every interventional setting to an acyclic resolution and we would get:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V|S,\doit(J))}B\given C\quad\iff\quad A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}^{\mathrm{acy}}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V\,\dot{\cup}\,S|\doit(J))}B\given S\cup C.

Again the proven global Markov property would apply to the right hand side:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}^{\mathrm{acy}}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V\,\dot{\cup}\,S|\doit(J))}B\given S\cup C\quad\implies\quad X_{A}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V},X_{S}|\doit(X_{J}))}X_{J},X_{B}\given X_{S},X_{C},

showing the global Markov property based on transitional
conditional independence even for such general cases.

K.4
Causal/Do-Calculus

In this section we will prove the 3 main rules of
do-calculus
, see
[
Pea09
,
Pea93a
,
Pea93b
,
FM20
]
.
for causal Bayesian networks (CBN) that allow for input variables
and latent variables. We will demonstrate how the use of transitional conditional independence circumvents the usual measure-theoretic
problems of matching functions/densities on null-sets, etc. Such questions were investigated e.g. also in
[
GR01
]
. First recall the following.

Remark K.9

(Marginal conditional interventional Markov kernels)
.

Consider a causal Bayesian network:

\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J))=\left(\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J)),\left(\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right)\right)_{v\in U\,\dot{\cup}\,V}\right),

<
a fixed topological order for
\mathbf{G}
.
For any disjoint subsets
D\subseteq J\cup V
,
W\subseteq V
we then have the hard and soft interventional Markov kernel

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{U},X_{V\setminus D}\Big|\doit\left(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{W}}\right)\right)=

\bigotimes_{v\in U\,\dot{\cup}\,V\setminus(D\cup W)}^{>}\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right)\otimes^{>}\bigotimes^{>}_{v\in W}\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)},X_{I_{v}}\right)\right),

where we reorder all factors in reverse order of
<
, see Section
K.2
.
If, furthermore,
A,B\subseteq J\cup V
then we can extend, marginalize and condition to get the marginal conditional interventional
Markov kernels, see Sections
2.2
,
2.7
:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}\Big|X_{B},\doit\left(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{W}}\right)\right),

which are unique up to
\mathbf{P}\left(X_{B}\Big|\doit\left(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{W}}\right)\right)
-null set.
These are related to each other by the graphical structure of the CDMG
\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J,I_{W}))
as we will see next.

We now prove the do-calculus rules, see
[
Pea09
]
, for causal Bayesian networks, which were most of the time proposed and proven
only for discrete spaces, but postulated for arbitrary ones. Even when densities were used the measure theoretical difficulties coming from dealing with the null-sets were typically ignored. Here we want to resolve these problems by making use of the our strong
version of the global Markov property for causal Bayesian networks, Thereom
K.7
, in combination with transitional conditional independence, Definition
3.1
.
It turns out the occuring Markov kernels will automatically aline all the null sets.

Corollary K.10

(Do-calculus)
.

Consider a causal Bayesian network:

\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J))=\left(\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J)),\left(\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{\mathbf{G}}(v)}\right)\right)\right)_{v\in U\,\dot{\cup}\,V}\right),

Let
A,B,C\subseteq V
and
D\subseteq J\cup V
. For simplicity, we assume that
A,B,C,D
are pairwise disjoint. Then we have the following rules relating marginal conditional interventional Markov kernels to each other.
Note that for a more suggestive notation we reorder the conditioning and
\doit
-parts in the Markov kernels arbitrarily.

1.

Insertion/deletion of observation
: If we have:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V\setminus D|\doit(D\cup J))}B\given C\,\dot{\cup}\,D,

then there exists a Markov kernel:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|\cancel{X_{B}},X_{C},\doit(X_{D},\cancel{X_{J\setminus D}})\right):\;\mathcal{X}_{C}\times\mathcal{X}_{D}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{A}

that is a version of:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|X_{\tilde{B}},X_{C},\doit(X_{D\cup J})\right),

for every subset
\tilde{B}\subseteq B
simultaneously.
Such a Markov kernel is unique up to
\mathbf{P}\left(X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J})\right)
-null set.
In particular and in short we could write:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|X_{B},X_{C},\doit(X_{D})\right)=\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|X_{C},\doit(X_{D})\right).

2.

Action/observation exchange
: If we have:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V\setminus D|\doit(D\cup J,I_{B}))}I_{B}\given B\,\dot{\cup}\,C\,\dot{\cup}\,D,

then there exists a Markov kernel:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|\cancel{\doit}(X_{B}),X_{C},\doit(X_{D},\cancel{X_{J\setminus D}})\right):\;\mathcal{X}_{B}\times\mathcal{X}_{C}\times\mathcal{X}_{D}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{A}

that is a version of:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|X_{B_{1}},\doit(X_{B_{2}}),X_{C},\doit(X_{D\cup J})\right),

for every disjoint decomposition:
B=B_{1}\,\dot{\cup}\,B_{2}
, simultaneously.
Such a Markov kernel is unique up to
\mathbf{P}\left(X_{B\,\dot{\cup}\,C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B}})\right)
-null set.
In particular and in short we could write:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|\doit(X_{B}),X_{C},\doit(X_{D})\right)=\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|X_{B},X_{C},\doit(X_{D})\right).

3.

Insertion/deletion of action
: If we have:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V\setminus D|\doit(D\cup J,I_{B}))}I_{B}\given C\,\dot{\cup}\,D,

then there exists a Markov kernel:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|\cancel{\doit(X_{B})},X_{C},\doit(X_{D},\cancel{X_{J\setminus D}})\right):\;\mathcal{X}_{C}\times\mathcal{X}_{D}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{A}

that is a version of:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|\doit(X_{\tilde{B}}),X_{C},\doit(X_{D\cup J})\right)

for every subset
\tilde{B}\subseteq B
simultaneously.
Such a Markov kernel is unique up to
\mathbf{P}\left(X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B}})\right)
-null set.
In particular and in short we could write:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|\doit(X_{B}),X_{C},\doit(X_{D})\right)=\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|X_{C},\doit(X_{D})\right).

Proof.

We make use of the global Markov property (GMP), Theorem
K.7
.

Point 1.) The assumption:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V\setminus D|\doit(D\cup J))}B\given C\,\dot{\cup}\,D,

implies the following transitional conditional independence by the global Markov property
K.7
:

X_{A}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{D\cup J}))}X_{B}\given X_{C},X_{D}.

So we get the following factorization:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A},X_{B},X_{C},X_{D}|\doit(X_{D\cup J})\right)=\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{C},X_{D})\otimes\mathbf{P}\left(X_{B},X_{C},X_{D}|\doit(X_{D\cup J})\right),

for some Markov kernel
\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{C},X_{D})
. If we marginalize out the deterministic variables
X_{D}
we get:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A},X_{B},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J})\right)=\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{C},X_{D})\otimes\mathbf{P}\left(X_{B},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J})\right).

Clearly
\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{C},X_{D})
serves here as a regular version of the conditional Markov kernel:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|X_{B},X_{C},\doit(X_{D\cup J})\right).

If we marginalize out
X_{B_{1}}
for any decomposition
B=B_{1}\,\dot{\cup}\,B_{2}

in the above factorization we also get:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A},X_{C},X_{B_{2}}|\doit(X_{D\cup J})\right)=\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{C},X_{D})\otimes\mathbf{P}\left(X_{C},X_{B_{2}}|\doit(X_{D\cup J})\right),

showing that
\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{C},X_{D})
is also a version of:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|X_{B_{2}},X_{C},\doit(X_{D\cup J})\right).

In particular, this holds for
B_{2}=\emptyset
. This shows point 1.

Point 2.) The assumption:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V\setminus D|\doit(D\cup J,I_{B}))}I_{B}\given B\,\dot{\cup}\,C\,\dot{\cup}\,D,

implies the following transitional conditional independence by the global Markov property
K.7
:

X_{A}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B}}))}X_{I_{B}}\given X_{B},X_{C},X_{D}.

As before, we get the following factorization:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A},X_{B},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B}})\right)=\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{B},X_{C},X_{D})\otimes\mathbf{P}\left(X_{B},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B}})\right),

for some Markov kernel
\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{B},X_{C},X_{D})
.
This then serves as a version of a regular conditional Markov kernel for:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|X_{B},X_{C},\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B}})\right),

and which is independent of
X_{I_{B}}
.

We can now look at the different input values for any decomposition:
B=B_{1}\,\dot{\cup}\,B_{2}
.
For this we put:
X_{I_{B_{1}}}=x_{B_{1}}\in\mathcal{X}_{B_{1}}
and
X_{I_{B_{2}}}=\star=(\star)_{v\in B_{2}}
.
This implies:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A},X_{B},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{{B_{1}}}=x_{B_{1}})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A},X_{B},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B_{1}}}=x_{B_{1}},X_{I_{B_{2}}}=\star)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{B},X_{C},X_{D})\otimes\mathbf{P}\left(X_{B},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B_{1}}}=x_{B_{1}},X_{I_{B_{2}}}=\star)\right),

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{B},X_{C},X_{D})\otimes\mathbf{P}\left(X_{B},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{{B_{1}}}=x_{B_{1}})\right).

So we get:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A},X_{B},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{B_{1}})\right)=\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{B},X_{C},X_{D})\otimes\mathbf{P}\left(X_{B},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{B_{1}})\right),

where we can further marginalize out the deterministic
X_{B_{1}}
to get:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A},X_{B_{2}},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{B_{1}})\right)=\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{B},X_{C},X_{D})\otimes\mathbf{P}\left(X_{B_{2}},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{B_{1}})\right).

This implies that
\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{B},X_{C},X_{D})
is a regular version of the conditional Markov kernel:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|X_{B_{2}},X_{C},\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{B_{1}})\right),

for every decomposition:
B=B_{1}\,\dot{\cup}\,B_{2}
, simultaneously, in particular for the two cases

B_{1}=\emptyset
and
B_{1}=B
. This shows point 2.

Point 3.) The assumption:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V\setminus D|\doit(D\cup J,I_{B}))}I_{B}\given C\,\dot{\cup}\,D,

implies the transitional conditional independence by the global Markov property
K.7
:

X_{A}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B}}))}X_{I_{B}}\given X_{C},X_{D}.

So we have the following factorization:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B}})\right)=\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{C},X_{D})\otimes\mathbf{P}\left(X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B}})\right),

for some Markov kernel
\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{C},X_{D})
, which serves as a regular version of the conditional Markov kernel:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|X_{C},\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B}})\right),

and which is independent of
X_{I_{B}}
.

We can now look at the different input values for any decomposition:
B=B_{1}\,\dot{\cup}\,B_{2}
.
For this we put:
X_{I_{B_{1}}}=x_{B_{1}}\in\mathcal{X}_{B_{1}}
and
X_{I_{B_{2}}}=\star=(\star)_{v\in B_{2}}
.
This implies:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{B_{1}}=x_{B_{1}})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B_{1}}}=x_{B_{1}},X_{I_{B_{2}}}=\star)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{C},X_{D})\otimes\mathbf{P}\left(X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{I_{B_{1}}}=x_{B_{1}},X_{I_{B_{2}}}=\star)\right),

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{C},X_{D})\otimes\mathbf{P}\left(X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{{B_{1}}}=x_{B_{1}})\right).

So we get:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A},X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{B_{1}})\right)=\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{C},X_{D})\otimes\mathbf{P}\left(X_{C}|\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{B_{1}})\right),

which shows that
\mathbf{Q}(X_{A}|X_{C},X_{D})
is a regular version of the conditional Markov kernel:

\mathbf{P}\left(X_{A}|X_{C},\doit(X_{D\cup J},X_{B_{1}})\right)

for every decomposition:
B=B_{1}\,\dot{\cup}\,B_{2}
, simultaneously, in particular for the two corner cases:

B_{1}=\emptyset
and
B_{1}=B
. This shows point 3.
∎

Remark K.11
.

1.

Please note in Corollary
K.10
how the null sets are handled. By having one Markov kernel that
serves as versions for all mentioned conditional interventional Markov kernels at once circumvents the problem of
having different versions and then trying to simultaneously match them by changing them
on families of compatible null sets.

2.

The 3 do-calculus rules, Corollary
K.10
, follow directly from the global Markov property, Theorem

K.7
.

3.

Slightly more general rules can be derived from the global Markov property Theorem

K.7
without the assumption of disjointness of
A,B,C,D
and
by combining such graphical separation criteria.
This will become less suggestive and readable though.

4.

In the 3 rules of do-calculus in Corollary
K.10
we were not restricted to assume that
D

contains
J
or is disjoint from it. This is thanks to the Definition
3.1
of transitional conditional independence, which will automatically provide the correct Markov kernel. This is in contrast to e.g.
[
FM20
]
, where a weaker version of extended conditional independence was used. The strategy there was to first construct
a suitable Markov kernel, case by case, before checking the conditional independence. For this they needed to make the
restrictive assumption that
J\subseteq D
.

K.5
Backdoor Covariate Adjustment Formula

Here we will restate the well-known backdoor covariate adjustment rules for causal Bayesian networks,
see
[
Pea93a
,
Pea93b
,
Pea09
,
FM20
]
, which were usually only stated for discrete spaces and probability distributions.
Here we want to show how this rule works out in the general measure theoretic setting and highlight how the difficulties
of matching occurring Markov kernels are addressed.
We are not aiming at finding new adjustment rules, for extensions we refer to:
[
BTP14
,
PP14
,
SVR10
,
PTKM15
,
CB17
,
FM20
]
.
For the identification of causal effects we refer to:
[
Pea09
,
GP95
,
Tia02
,
TP02
,
Tia04
,
SP06
,
HV06
,
HV08
,
RERS17
,
FM20
]
.

Corollary K.12

(Conditional backdoor covariate adjustment formula)
.

Consider a causal Bayesian network:

\mathbf{M}(U,V|\doit(J))=\left(\mathbf{G}(U\,\dot{\cup}\,V|\doit(J)),\quad\left(\mathbf{P}_{v}\left(X_{v}\Big|\doit\left(X_{\mathrm{Pa}^{G^{+}}(v)}\right)\right)\right)_{v\in U\,\dot{\cup}\,V}\right).

Consider
A,B,C,F\subseteq V
and
D\subseteq J\cup V
with
J\subseteq D
that are pairwise disjoint.
Assume that the
conditional backdoor criterion
in the CDGM
\mathbf{G}(V\setminus D|\doit(D,I_{B}))
holds:

1.

\displaystyle F\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V\setminus D|\doit(D,I_{B}))}I_{B}\given(C\cup D)
, and:

2.

\displaystyle A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V\setminus D|\doit(D,I_{B}))}I_{B}\given(B\cup F\cup C\cup D)
.

Then there exists a Markov kernel:

\mathbf{P}(X_{A}|X_{F},X_{C},\cancel{\doit}(X_{B}),\doit(X_{D}))

that simultaneously is a regular version of:

\mathbf{P}(X_{A}|X_{F},X_{C},\doit(X_{B},X_{D}))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{P}(X_{A}|X_{F},X_{C},X_{B},\doit(X_{D}));

and there exists a Markov kernel:

\mathbf{P}(X_{F}|X_{C},\cancel{\doit(X_{B})},\doit(X_{D}))

that simultaneously is a regular version of:

\mathbf{P}(X_{F}|X_{C},\doit(X_{B},X_{D}))\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{P}(X_{F}|X_{C},\doit(X_{D})).

Furthermore, their composition:

\mathbf{P}(X_{A}|X_{F},X_{C},\cancel{\doit}(X_{B}),\doit(X_{D}))\circ\mathbf{P}(X_{F}|X_{C},\cancel{\doit(X_{B})},\doit(X_{D})),

is then a version of:
\quad\mathbf{P}(X_{A}|X_{C},\doit(X_{B},X_{D}))
.

Remark K.13
.

1.

The proof of Corollary
K.12
goes along
the same lines as Rule 2 and Rule 3 in Corollary
K.10
.

2.

The meaning of the backdoor covariate adjustment criterion in Corollary
K.12
is
that when one wants to estimate the (conditional) causal effect
\mathbf{P}(X_{A}|\doit(X_{B}))
, for simplicity we use
C=D=\emptyset
,
and one has a set of measured covariates/features
X_{F}
of the data that shields off
X_{A}
from “backdoor” common confounders
of
X_{A}
and
X_{B}
then one can estimate the causal effect
\mathbf{P}(X_{A}|\doit(X_{B}))
by only using
observational
data
from
\mathbf{P}(X_{F})
and
\mathbf{P}(X_{A}|X_{F},X_{B})
.

Appendix L
Comparison to Other Notions of Conditional Independence

In this section we want to look at other notions of conditional independence and compare them
to transitional conditional independence.

Recall that for transition probability space

\left(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T},\mathbf{K}(W|T)\right)
and transitional random variables
\mathbf{X}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}
and

\mathbf{Y}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathbf{Z}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Z}

with joint Markov kernel:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T):=\left(\mathbf{X}(X|W,T)\otimes\mathbf{Y}(Y|W,T)\otimes\mathbf{Z}(Z|W,T)\right)\circ\mathbf{K}(W|T),

we define the
transitional conditional independence
of
\mathbf{X}
from
\mathbf{Y}
given
\mathbf{Z}
:

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\quad:\iff\quad\exists\mathbf{Q}(X|Z):\;\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T).

L.1
Variation Conditional Independence

We follow
[
CD17a
,
Daw01b
]
and their supplementary material
[
CD17b
]
to review variation conditional independence and then comment on some possible generalizations.

For this let
\mathcal{W}
be a set and
X:\,\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{X}
,
Y:\,\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{Y}
,
Z:\,\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{Z}
,
U:\,\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{U}

be maps.

Notation L.1

(See
[
CD17a
]
§2.2)
.

We define:

\mathcal{R}(X):=X(\mathcal{W})=\{X(w)\,|\,w\in\mathcal{W}\}\in 2^{\mathcal{X}},

and for
z\in\mathcal{Z}
:

\mathcal{R}(X|Z=z):=X\left(Z^{-1}(z)\right)=\{X(w)\,|\,w\in\mathcal{W},Z(w)=z\}\in 2^{\mathcal{X}}.

We then define the map:

\mathcal{R}(X|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\to 2^{\mathcal{X}},\quad z\mapsto\mathcal{R}(X|Z=z).

In this sense we then can also make sense of:

\mathcal{R}(X,Y|Z,U):\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{U}\to 2^{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}},

(z,u)\mapsto\mathcal{R}(X,Y|Z=z,U=u):=\{(X(w),Y(w))\,|\,w\in\mathcal{W},Z(w)=z,U(w)=u\}.

Definition L.2

(Variation conditional independence)
.

We will say that
X
is variation conditionally independent of
Y
given
Z

if:

\forall(y,z)\in\mathcal{R}(Y,Z):\,\mathcal{R}(X|Y=y,Z=z)=\mathcal{R}(X|Z=z).

In symbols we will write then:

X\Indep_{v}Y\given Z.

Notation L.3
.

We will write:

X\precsim_{v}Y

if there exists a map
\varphi:\,\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
such that
\varphi\circ Y=X
.
Note that we use a slightly simpler, but equivalent relation, than
[
CD17a
]
§2.2, Prop. 2.6.

Remark L.4

(See
[
CD17a
]
Thm. 2.7.,
[
CD17b
]
)
.

The ternary relation
\Indep_{v}
together with
\precsim_{v}
and
X\lor Y:=(X,Y)

is a (symmetric) separoid.

Remark L.5
.

The relation between variation conditional independence
and stochastic conditional independence for random variables seems rather on
the structural side, i.e. both follow similar functorial
relations. In short, if one wants to go from variation to stochastic conditional independence
one could start by replacing
2^{\mathcal{X}}
with
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
and maps with measurable maps, etc.
Then maps
\mathcal{Z}\to 2^{\mathcal{X}}
become measurable maps
\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
, which are nothing
else but Markov kernels, reflecting the approach we went down for
transitional conditional independence.
So
\mathcal{R}(X,Y,Z)
can be represented as the (constant) map:

\mathcal{R}(X,Y,Z):\,\Asterisk\to 2^{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}},\quad\ast\mapsto\mathcal{R}(X,Y,Z).

where
\Asterisk=\{\ast\}
is the one-point space. We also have the “marginal”:

\mathcal{R}(Y,Z):\,\Asterisk\to 2^{\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}},\quad\ast\mapsto\mathcal{R}(Y,Z)=\mathrm{pr}_{\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Z}}\left(\mathcal{R}(X,Y,Z)\right),

which is received by ignoring the
X
-entries.

It is then easily seen that we have:
X\Indep_{v}Y\given Z
iff there exists a
map:

\mathcal{Q}(X|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\to 2^{\mathcal{X}},

such that:

\mathcal{R}(X,Y,Z)=\mathcal{Q}(X|Z)\otimes_{v}\mathcal{R}(Y,Z),

where we put:

\mathcal{Q}(X|Z)\otimes_{v}\mathcal{R}(Y,Z):=\bigcup_{(y,z)\in\mathcal{R}(Y,Z)}\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{Q}(X|Z=z)}\{(x,y,z)\}.

Example L.6
.

We can now apply the above to

X,Y,Z
with common domain
\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}
and
T:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}
the canonical projection map.
Then we get:

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\;X\Indep_{v,T}Y\given Z

\displaystyle:\iff\quad X\Indep_{v}T,Y\given Z

\displaystyle\iff\quad\exists\mathcal{Q}(X|Z):\,\mathcal{Z}\to 2^{\mathcal{X}}:\quad\mathcal{R}(X,Y,Z|T)=\mathcal{Q}(X|Z)\otimes_{v}\mathcal{R}(Y,Z|T),

where we again now have:

\mathcal{R}(X,Y,Z|T=t)=\{(x,y,z)\,|\,\exists w\in\mathcal{W}:X(w,t)=x,Y(w,t)=y,Z(w,t)=z\}.

This shows the close formal relationship between variation conditional independence and
transitional conditional independence
\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}
.
It then follows from Remark
L.4
and
the general theory in Appendix
A
with Theorem
A.16

that
\Indep_{v,T}
forms a
T
-
\ast
-separoid.
One can thus combine
\Indep_{\mathbf{K}}
and
\Indep_{v,T}
with a logical “and”, while still preserving the

T
-
\ast
-separoid rules.

It seems, more generally,
that one can formulate a (transitional) conditional independence relation
in any monad with products and some extra structure.
We leave this for future research.

L.2
Transitional Conditional Independence for Random Variables

If we wanted to re-define the notion of
independent
random variables
X
and
Y
on a probability space
(\mathcal{W},\mathbf{P}(W))

we would have a hard time coming up with something else than
the classical definition of:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(X)\otimes\mathbf{P}(Y),

(22)

where
\mathbf{P}(X)
and
\mathbf{P}(Y)
are the marginals. This is in contrast to
conditionally independent

random variables
X
and
Y
given a third
Z
, where many nuances can play a role.
For instance, the direct analogon to relation
22
would read like:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(X,Y|Z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{P}(Y|Z)

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(Z)\text{-a.s.}

(23)

The problem with definition
23
is that the conditional probability distributions, like
\mathbf{P}(X,Y|Z)
,
may not exist on general measurable spaces
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{Y}
,
\mathcal{Z}
, in contrast to the marginals
\mathbf{P}(X)
,
\mathbf{P}(Y)

in equation
22
. This then forces one to restrict oneself to only work
with measurable spaces
where regular conditional probability distributions exist, like standard measurable spaces.
But even if the existence were guaranteed, they would only be unique up to some null sets.
One then either ends up with a notion of conditional independence that would depend on the choices made
or, as the better alternative, one would work with almost-sure equations like we
already indicated in equation
23
.

If one wanted to work with more general measurable spaces one could demand that equation
23

only holds for every
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
individually. Furthermore, one then could replace

\mathbf{P}(X\in A,Y\in B|Z)
with conditional expectations
\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(Y)|Z]
, etc., which exist on all measurable spaces.
One then arrives at the most general
and
weak form of conditional independence
for random variables:

\displaystyle\forall A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}\,\forall B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}:\,\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(Y)|Z]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X)|Z]\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{B}(Y)|Z]

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(W)\text{-a.s.},

(24)

which can, equivalently, but more compactly, also be written as:

\displaystyle\forall A\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{X}}:\;\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X)|Y,Z]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X)|Z]

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(W)\text{-a.s.}

(25)

We will use the following symbols for weak conditional independence:

X\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(W)}^{\omega}Y\given Z.

Furthermore, if we used definition
24
or
25
on standard measurable spaces,
where regular conditional probability distributions like
\mathbf{P}(X,Y|Z)
exist, the equation
23
would
automatically be implied. So the equations
24
or
25
seem to be the way to go,
as one does not need to bother with existence questions, and when existence is secured the above versions
are equivalent anyways. The only downside is that this definition does not provide one with a meaningful factorization.
Furthermore, the conditional expectations, like
\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X)|Z]
, are only defined for each event
A
separately
and thus might not be countably additive in
A
. So we are not given an object like a conditional distribution that we could use
to further work with.

In contrast, our definition of
transitional conditional independence

X\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(W)}Y\given Z
,
when restricted to random variables, would read like:

\displaystyle\exists\mathbf{P}(X|Z):\quad\mathbf{P}(X,Y,Z)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{P}(Y,Z),

(26)

where clearly
\mathbf{P}(X|Z)
would be a regular conditional probability distribution of
X
given
Z
.
So the existence of one of the regular conditional probability distributions
\mathbf{P}(X|Z)

and a proper factorization of the joint distribution
are directly built into the definition of transitional conditional independence.
This makes this notion also meaningful for general measurable spaces, with the tendency that random variables are
declared conditional dependent if such a regular conditional probability distribution does not even exist.
Certainly, all three definitions
26
,
25
,
23
are equivalent
on standard measurable spaces. Note that transitional conditional independence
26
is asymmetric in nature,
which at this level might look like a flaw, but which allows one to generalize the definition of transitional conditional independence
to
transitional random variables
, where dependencies are asymmetric from the start.

L.3
Transitional Conditional Independence for Deterministic Variables

Theorem L.7

(Transitional conditional independence for deterministic variables)
.

Let
F:\,\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{F}
and
H:\,\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{H}
be measurable maps with
\mathcal{F}
standard.
We now consider them as (deterministic) transitional random variables on the transition probability space
(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T},\mathbf{K}(W|T))
.
Let
\mathbf{Y}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
be another transitional random variable.

Then the following statements are equivalent:

1.

\displaystyle F\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}\mathbf{Y}\given H
.

2.

There exists a measurable function
\varphi:\,\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{F}
such that
F=\varphi\circ H
.

Proof.

“
\Longleftarrow
”: This direction follows from Extended Left Redundancy
E.3
.

“
\implies
”: Since
F
and
H
are deterministic and only dependent on
T
we get that:

\mathbf{K}(F,Y,H|T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(F|T)\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(H|T)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|T).

By the conditional independence we now have a Markov kernel
\mathbf{Q}(F|H)
such that we have the factorization:

\mathbf{K}(F,Y,H|T)=\mathbf{Q}(F|H)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,H|T)=\mathbf{Q}(F|H)\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(H|T)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y|T).

Marginalizing out
Y
,
H
and taking
T=t
we get from these equations:

{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{F(t)}={\boldsymbol{\delta}}(F|T=t)=\mathbf{Q}(F|H(t)),

which is a Dirac measure centered at
F(t)
.
We can now define the mapping:

\varphi:\,H(\mathcal{T})\to\mathcal{F},\quad H(t)\mapsto F(t),

which is well-defined, because
h:=H(t_{1})=H(t_{2})
implies that
\mathbf{Q}(F|H=h)
is a Dirac measure centered at
F(t_{1})
and
F(t_{2})
.
Since
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}}
separates points (
\mathcal{F}
is standard) we get:
F(t_{1})=F(t_{2})
.

\varphi
is measurable.
Indeed, its composition with
\delta:\,\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F}),\,z\mapsto{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{z}
equals
\mathbf{Q}(F|H)
, which is measurable.
Since
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{F}}=\delta^{*}\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})}
, see Lemma
B.39
, also
\varphi
is measurable.
Since
\mathcal{F}
is a standard measurable space,
\varphi
extends to a measurable mapping
\varphi:\,\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{F}

by Kuratowski’s extension theorem for standard measurable spaces (see
[
Kec95
]
12.2 and Theorem
B.54
).
Finally, note that we have
F(t)=\varphi(H(t))
for all
(w,t)\in\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}
, which shows the claim.

\textstyle{\mathcal{T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{H}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\exists\varphi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})}

∎

Remark L.8
.

Note that the proof of Theorem
L.7
also works for universal measurable spaces
\mathcal{F}

if one replaces measurability
with universal measurability everywhere. One then needs to use Kuratowski’s extension theorem for univeral measurable spaces,
Theorem
B.56
.

L.4
Equivalent Formulations of Transitional Conditional Independence

Our groundwork of developing the framework of transition probability spaces, transitional random variables
and transitional conditional independence
now allows us to rigorously compare different notions of extended conditional indpendence in the literature.
We will compare to three of them, namely the one from
[
CD17a
,
RERS17
,
FM20
]
.

To relate transitional conditional independence to other notion of conditional independence it is useful to reformulate
transitional conditional independence in other terms. The main result for this will be the next theorem.

Theorem L.9
.

Let
\left(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T},\mathbf{K}(W|T)\right)
be a transition probability space and
\mathbf{X}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{X}
and

\mathbf{Y}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathbf{Z}:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{Z}
transitional random variables.
We put:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T):=\left(\mathbf{X}(X|W,T)\otimes\mathbf{Y}(Y|W,T)\otimes\mathbf{Z}(Z|W,T)\right)\circ\mathbf{K}(W|T),

We will write
\mathbf{K}(X|Z,\cancel{T})

for a fixed version of the Markov kernel appearing in the conditional independence
\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\given\mathbf{Z}

(only in case it holds).

With these notations, the following are equivalent:

1.

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z},

2.

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}\mathbf{T}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z},

3.

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{\ast}\given\mathbf{Z}
and

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{K}(X|Z,\cancel{T})\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)
.

4.

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}^{\ast}\mathbf{Z}
and for every
t\in\mathcal{T}
we have:

\displaystyle X_{t}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T=t)}^{\omega}Y_{t}\given Z_{t}
(in the weak sense).

Furthermore, any of those points implies
the following:

5.

For every probability distribution
\mathbf{Q}(T)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
we have the conditional independence:

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)\otimes\mathbf{Q}(T)}\mathbf{T}\otimes\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Proof.

3.
\implies
1. is clear by definition.

1.
\implies
2.: by
\mathbf{T}
-Inverted Right Decomposition
E.8
.

2.
\implies
4.,5.:
By assumption we have the factorization:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z,T|T)=\mathbf{K}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z,T|T),

for some Markov kernel
\mathbf{K}(X|Z)
. Via marginalization and multiplication this implies the two equations:

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}(X,Z,T|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z,T|T),

\displaystyle\underbrace{\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)\otimes\mathbf{Q}(T)}_{=:\mathbf{Q}(X,Y,Z,T)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}(X|Z)\otimes\underbrace{\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T)\otimes\mathbf{Q}(T)}_{=\mathbf{Q}(Y,Z,T)},

for every
\mathbf{Q}(T)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
. The last equation shows 5.

If we take
\mathbf{Q}(T)={\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}
we get:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T=t)=\mathbf{K}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T=t).

Together with the first of the above equations this shows 4.

4.
\implies
3.: By
\mathbf{X}\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}^{\ast}\mathbf{Z}
we have a factorization:

\mathbf{K}(X,Z|T)=\mathbf{P}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Z|T).

This means that for every
t\in\mathcal{T}
and every measurable
A\subseteq\mathcal{X}
,
C\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
we have:

\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X_{t})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{C}(Z_{t})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\mathbf{P}(X\in A|Z_{t})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{C}(Z_{t})\right],

where the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{t}
is w.r.t.
\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T=t)
. This shows that
\mathbf{P}(X\in A|Z_{t})
is a version of
\mathbb{E}_{t}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X_{t})|Z_{t}]
for every
t\in\mathcal{T}
,
by the defining properties of conditional expectation.

By the assumption
X_{t}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T=t)}^{\omega}Y_{t}\given Z_{t}
we then have for every fixed
t\in\mathcal{T}
and measurable
A\subseteq\mathcal{X}
:

\mathbb{E}_{t}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X_{t})|Y_{t},Z_{t}]=\mathbb{E}_{t}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X_{t})|Z_{t}]=\mathbf{P}(X\in A|Z_{t})\qquad\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T=t)\text{-a.s.}

By the defining properties of conditional expectation for
\mathbb{E}_{t}[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X_{t})|Y_{t},Z_{t}]
we then get that for every measurable
A\subseteq\mathcal{X}
,
B\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
,
C\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
:

\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X_{t})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(Y_{t})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{C}(Z_{t})\right]=\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\mathbf{P}(X\in A|Z_{t})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{B}(Y_{t})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{C}(Z_{t})\right].

Since this holds for every
t\in\mathcal{T}
we get:

\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T)=\mathbf{P}(X|Z)\otimes\mathbf{K}(Y,Z|T),

which shows the claim.
∎

Corollary L.10
.

If
\mathcal{X}
is standard and
\mathcal{Z}
countably generated (e.g. also standard)
then we have the equivalence:

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{T}\qquad\iff\qquad\forall t\in\mathcal{T}:\quad X_{t}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(X,Y,Z|T=t)}^{\omega}Y_{t}\given Z_{t}.

Proof.

This directly follows from Theorem
L.9
4. with
(Z,T)
in the role of
Z

and Remark
3.6
to get the first part of 4.
∎

L.5
The Extended Conditional Independence

We shortly review the definition of
extended conditional independence
introduced in
[
CD17a
]
.

Definition L.11

(Extendend conditional independence, see
[
CD17a
]
Def. 3.2)
.

Let
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{T}
be measurable spaces
and
\mathcal{E}=(\mathbf{P}_{t}(W))_{t\in\mathcal{T}}
be a family of probability measures on
\mathcal{W}
.
Let
X,Y,Z
be measurable maps on
\mathcal{W}
and
\Phi,\Theta
measurable maps on
\mathcal{T}
such that
the joint map
(\Phi,\Theta)
is injective. For these cases
extended conditional independence
was defined as:

X\Indep_{\mathcal{E}}(Y,\Theta)\given(Z,\Phi)

if for all
\phi\in\Phi(\mathcal{T})
and all real bounded measurable
h

there exists a function
g_{h,\phi}
such that for all
t\in\Phi^{-1}(\phi)
we have that:

\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[h(X)|Y,Z]\right]=g_{h,\phi}(Z)\quad\mathbf{P}_{t}(W)\text{-a.s.},

where the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}_{t}
is w.r.t.
\mathbf{P}_{t}(W)
.

We now show that when
(Y,\Theta)
and
(Z,\Phi)
are considered as transitional random variables
on transition probability space
\left(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T},\mathbf{P}(W|T)\right)
, where we put
\mathbf{P}(W|T=t):=\mathbf{P}_{t}(W)
,
then transitional conditional independence implies extended conditional independence.

Lemma L.12
.

We have the implication:

X\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(W|T)}(Y,\Theta)\given(Z,\Phi)\qquad\implies\qquad X\Indep_{\mathcal{E}}(Y,\Theta)\given(Z,\Phi).

Proof.

Indeed, by the assumption we get a Markov kernel
\mathbf{Q}(X|Z,\Phi)
such that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(X,Y,\Theta,Z,\Phi|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z,\Phi)\otimes\mathbf{P}(Y,\Theta,Z,\Phi|T)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z,\Phi)\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\Theta|T)\otimes{\boldsymbol{\delta}}(\Phi|T)\otimes\mathbf{P}(Y,Z|T).

Marginalizing out
\Theta
and
\Phi
gives:

\mathbf{P}(X,Y,Z|T=t)=\mathbf{Q}(X|Z,\Phi=\Phi(t))\otimes\mathbf{P}(Y,Z|T=t).

For any
\phi
and function
h
we then define:

g_{h,\phi}(z):=\int_{\mathcal{X}}h(x)\,\mathbf{Q}(X\in dx|Z=z,\Phi=\phi).

Then for each
t\in\Phi^{-1}(\phi)
and
B\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Y}}
and
C\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{Z}}
we get:

\displaystyle\int_{C}\int_{B}\int_{\mathcal{X}}h(x)\,\mathbf{P}(X\in dx,Y\in dy,Z\in dz|T=t)

\displaystyle=\int_{C}\int_{B}\int_{\mathcal{X}}h(x)\,\mathbf{Q}(X\in dx|Z=z,\Phi=\Phi(t))\,\mathbf{P}(Y\in dy,Z\in dz|T=t)

\displaystyle=\int_{C}\int_{B}g_{h,\phi}(z)\,\mathbf{P}(Y\in dy,Z\in dz|T=t).

Since this is the defining equation for the conditional expectation we get the claim:

\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[h(X)|Y,Z\right]=g_{h,\phi}(Z)\quad\mathbf{P}_{t}(W)\text{-a.s.}.

∎

Remark L.13
.

So transitional conditional independence is the stronger notion and implies extended conditional independence,
but it works for all transitional random variables,
not just of the restricted type in Definition
L.11
.
Furthermore and in contrast to extended conditional independence,
transitional conditional independence satisfies all the (asymmetric) separoid rules, Theorem
3.11
, for all measurable spaces,
except Left Weak Union and
T
-Restricted Right Redundancy, which hold when one can ensure the existence of
regular conditional Markov kernels, e.g. on standard measurable spaces, see Theorem
2.24
.
This makes transitional conditional independence a better fit for the use in graphical models,
see, for instance, the global Markov property,
Theorem
6.3
.
Note that both notions only have a restricted direct relation to variation conditional independence,
see Theorem
3.8
and
[
CD17a
,
CD17b
]
.
A formal analogy between variation conditional independence and transitional conditional independence
was discussed in Remark
L.5
and Example
L.6
.

L.6
Symmetric Extended Conditional Independence

If we wanted to arrive at a symmetric version of extended conditional independence that satisfies all (symmetric)
separoid rules (at least when restricted so standard or universal measurable spaces)
we could just use
symmetrized transitional conditional independence
:

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}^{\lor}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\qquad:\iff\qquad\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\quad\lor\quad\mathbf{Y}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}\mathbf{X}\given\mathbf{Z}.

Since
\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}
forms a
T
-
\ast
-separoid it is immediate that
\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}^{\lor}
is a symmetric separoid
by the general theory of
\tau
-
\kappa
-separoids, see Theorem
A.17
.
We clearly have the implication:

\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z}\qquad\implies\qquad\mathbf{X}\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}^{\lor}\mathbf{Y}\given\mathbf{Z},

showing that the asymmetric version is stronger than the symmetrized version, where the latter might have lost some information about
the interplay between
\mathbf{X}
,
\mathbf{Y}
,
\mathbf{Z}
and
\mathbf{T}
. Furthermore, because of its symmetry it can not equivalently
express classical statistical concepts like ancillarity, sufficiency, adequacy, etc., see Section
4
.
A symmetric notion of extended conditional independence was intoduced in
[
RERS17
]
.
Also in
[
CJ19
,
Fri20
]
a symmetric version of conditional independence for categorical probability theory was proposed.

It is also worth mentioning that the symmetrized
\sigma
-separation in a CDMG
\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))
agrees with
\sigma
-separation in the DMG
\mathbf{G}(V\,\dot{\cup}\,J|\doit(\bullet))
that arises from
\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))
by connecting every two nodes from
J
with bi-directed edges, see Lemma
I.20
. The global Markov property from Theorem
K.7
then immediately implies the following symmetrized version:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V\,\dot{\cup}\,J|\doit(\bullet))}B\given C\qquad\implies\qquad X_{A}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J}))}^{\lor}X_{B}\given X_{C}.

This recovers, generalized and strengthens the corresponding results from
[
RERS17
]
and
[
FM20
]
Appendix C.

L.7
Extended Conditional Independence for Families of Probability Distributions

In this subsection we will introduce a strikingly simple and powerful form of extended conditional independence
that works for all measurable spaces and satisfies all the separoid rules.
For this consider a transition probability space
\left(\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T},\mathbf{K}(W|T)\right)
and transitional random variables

X:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{X}
,
Y:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{Y}
,
Z:\,\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{Z}
.
Furthermore, fix a set
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
of probability measures on
\mathcal{T}
, e.g.
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{T})
or

\mathcal{Q}=\left\{{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}\,|\,t\in\mathcal{T}\right\}
.
Then we can define
\mathcal{Q}
-extended conditional independence
as:

X\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)\otimes\mathcal{Q}}^{\omega}Y\given Z\qquad:\iff\qquad\forall\mathbf{Q}(T)\in\mathcal{Q}:\quad X\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)\otimes\mathbf{Q}(T)}^{\omega}Y\given Z.

It is easily seen that the usual weak conditional independence
\Indep^{\omega}
satisfies all separoid axioms
[
Daw01a
,
CD17a
]

for arbitrary measurable spaces. Furthermore, if one combines several separoids by conjunction

\land
then one gets another separoid, see
[
Daw01a
]
.
So
\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)\otimes\mathcal{Q}}^{\omega}
clearly satisfies all (symmetric) separoid axioms for arbitrary measurable spaces.
By Theorem
L.9
we have the implications:

X\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)}Y\given Z\quad\implies\quad X\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)\otimes\mathcal{Q}}^{\omega}T,Y\given Z\quad\implies\quad X\Indep_{\mathbf{K}(W|T)\otimes\mathcal{Q}}^{\omega}Y\given Z.

The middle ternary relation in
X,Y,Z
satisfy the asymmetric separoid rules
from Theorem
3.11
, but without any requirement for standard or countably generated measurable spaces,
in contrast to transitional conditional indpendence on the left. The asymmetric separoid rules for the middle relation follow from the right relation and Theorem
A.16
.

It seems that
\mathcal{Q}
-extended conditional independence checks all boxes that one would like to have from
a notion of extended conditional independence. It is certainly simpler than most other notions.
It just comes with one drawback: it does not provide one with the existence or factorization of certain Markov kernels.
When the reverse implication holds is stated in Theorem
L.9
, e.g. if
{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{t}\in\mathcal{Q}
for all
t\in\mathcal{T}

and
X\precsim_{\mathbf{K}}^{\ast}Z
, where the latter encodes the existence of a certain Markov kernel, which is thus the main obstruction
to arrive at transitional conditional independence.

To ellaborate further, a specialized version of this
\mathcal{Q}
-extended conditional independence was first introduced in
[
FM20
]
, where it was used to
derive the causal do-calculus rules, see
[
Pea09
,
FM20
]
, for certain structural causal models.
In their proofs they had to construct certain Markov kernels and then check for
\mathcal{Q}
-extended conditional independence.
Since the construction of such Markov kernels became complicated many corner cases could not been proved.
The main ingredient of their proof was a
global Markov property
for
\mathcal{Q}
-extended conditional independence:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))}J\cup B\given C\qquad\implies\qquad X_{A}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J}))\otimes\mathcal{Q}}^{\omega}X_{J},X_{B}\given X_{C}.

Here,
\mathcal{Q}
-extended conditional independence was not strong enough to produce the needed Markov kernels.
This is in contrast to transitional conditional independence,
whose global Markov property, Theorem
6.3
now gives:

A\Perp^{\sigma}_{\mathbf{G}(V|\doit(J))}B\given C\qquad\implies\qquad X_{A}\Indep_{\mathbf{P}(X_{V}|\doit(X_{J}))}X_{B}\given X_{C},

which is a stronger conclusion and provides us with the needed Markov kernels for free.
This was one of the core motivation for developing transitional conditional independence.
For a proof of the do-calculus rules for causal Bayesian networks with non-stochastic input variables and latent variables
that also covers such corner cases easily see Corollary
K.10
,
which is based on the global Markov property, Theorem
6.3
.

ITF21
